# Evolved Educational Intelligence Approved Studies Bank

**Owner:** Peter Brown  
**Status:** Canonical evidence-use register  
**Version:** 1.30  
**Evidence cutoff:** 19 August 2026

## Purpose

This bank records peer-reviewed studies and consensus statements that have been checked closely enough to support a defined Evolved claim, boundary or research decision.

“Approved” is claim-specific. It does not mean that every sentence in a paper is accepted, that one study proves a whole coaching method, or that a paper may be used to diagnose an individual member. The approved wording and exclusions below control every downstream use.

## Evidence-use states

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `APPROVED-ANCHOR` | Suitable upstream evidence for the bounded use recorded here. |
| `APPROVED-BOUNDED` | Useful evidence, but design, population, certainty or transferability requires material qualification. |
| `CONTEXT-ONLY` | Useful for history, mechanism or discovery; not sufficient as the principal support for an Evolved public claim. |
| `NOT-APPROVED-FOR-CLAIM` | The paper is legitimate, but it does not support the claim for which it was presented. |
| `RETRACTED-EXCLUDED` | The publication has been retracted and is retained only for provenance and citation-safety control; it cannot support an Evolved claim. |
| `PENDING` | Identity or full claim-use review is incomplete. |

## Acceptance fields

Every banked record must preserve the citation and stable locator, study type, population, exposure or intervention, outcomes, main finding, material limitations, approved use, prohibited overreach, related doctrine or claim, review date and immediate-review triggers. When a newer synthesis changes the answer, retain the older paper with a supersession note rather than silently deleting it.

## Exercise, recovery and energy-availability records

### STU-EXE-0001 — Acute endocrine response to one HIIT session

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dote-Montero M, et al. *Acute effect of HIIT on testosterone and cortisol levels in healthy individuals: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2021;31:1722–1744. |
| Locator | [PMID 34022085](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34022085/); DOI `10.1111/sms.13999` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis; 10 controlled studies with 213 participants and 50 pre-post groups with 677 participants; mostly healthy younger adults with mixed training status. |
| Finding | Cortisol increased immediately and at 30 and 60 minutes after a single HIIT session, fell below baseline at 120 and 180 minutes in the pre-post evidence, and was around baseline at 24 hours. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | HIIT can produce a normal acute cortisol response whose time course changes after the session. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that every class is harmful; that poor sleep or ordinary life stress creates chronic cortisol elevation; that recovery fails in a given member; or that an acute cortisol rise predicts fat gain, reproductive suppression or overtraining syndrome. |
| Linked authority | DOC-REC-0001; DOC-REC-0002 |

### STU-EXE-0002 — Older overtraining-syndrome review

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Angeli A, et al. *The overtraining syndrome in athletes: a stress-related disorder.* J Endocrinol Invest. 2004;27:603–612. |
| Locator | [PMID 15717662](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15717662/); DOI `10.1007/BF03347487` |
| Design and population | Narrative review concerning athletes under strenuous training schedules. |
| Finding | Describes overtraining syndrome as prolonged performance impairment with systemic features and discusses possible neuroendocrine and immune mechanisms; universally agreed diagnostic criteria were absent. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Historical context showing that overtraining syndrome is a serious, complex athlete condition rather than a synonym for feeling tired after exercise. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot establish a threshold for busy adults over 35, prove that five HIIT classes plus six hours of sleep or 1,400 kcal causes overtraining, or permit a trainer to diagnose HPA-axis dysfunction. |
| Supersession | Use STU-EXE-0007 for the stronger consensus boundary. |

### STU-END-0001 — Glucocorticoids, stress and fertility

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Whirledge S, Cidlowski JA. *Glucocorticoids, stress, and fertility.* Minerva Endocrinol. 2010;35:109–125. |
| Locator | [PMID 20595939](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20595939/); [PMCID PMC3547681](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3547681/) |
| Design and population | Mechanistic narrative review drawing on in-vitro, animal and human clinical observations. |
| Finding | Glucocorticoid signalling can influence reproductive function at the hypothalamus, pituitary and gonads; normal regulated glucocorticoid activity is also physiologically necessary. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Mechanistic background for qualified clinical research or referral-boundary work. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that daily HIIT suppresses ovarian hormones in perimenopausal women or that puffiness, irregular cycles, low libido or low mood are direct outputs of a “cortisol-dominant hormonal environment.” It cannot support diagnosis or treatment by trainers. |
| Linked authority | Clinical boundary under DOC-REC-0001 and DOC-MND-0009 |

### STU-NUT-0001 — Short-term calorie restriction, monitoring and cortisol

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Tomiyama AJ, et al. *Low calorie dieting increases cortisol.* Psychosom Med. 2010;72:357–364. |
| Locator | [PMID 20368473](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20368473/); [PMCID PMC2895000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2895000/); DOI `10.1097/PSY.0b013e3181d9523c` |
| Design and population | Three-week 2×2 controlled experiment; 121 women randomised to monitoring and/or approximately 1,200 kcal/day restriction conditions; 15 participants dropped after initial sampling. |
| Finding | Restriction increased total daily cortisol output; calorie monitoring increased perceived stress. Restriction also produced weight loss, and the authors stated that translation of the cortisol change into later weight gain had not been established in humans. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Severe or burdensome restriction and intensive monitoring can have stress-related effects; calorie awareness should be purposeful, time-limited and monitored for harm. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that any energy deficit prevents fat loss, “suppresses metabolism,” breaks down muscle, drives visceral-fat storage or makes the body hold every calorie. It does not set a universal calorie floor. |
| Linked authority | DOC-NUT-0001; DOC-REC-0001 |

### STU-EXE-0003 — Free-weight resistance training in middle-aged women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Isenmann E, et al. *Resistance training alters body composition in middle-aged women depending on menopause — a 20-week control trial.* BMC Womens Health. 2023;23:526. |
| Locator | [PMID 37803287](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37803287/); [PMCID PMC10559623](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10559623/); DOI `10.1186/s12905-023-02671-y` |
| Design and population | Forty-one healthy women aged 40–60 entered a 10-week no-training control phase then a 10-week twice-weekly resistance-training phase; 31 completed; intensity allocation was randomised. |
| Finding | Squat and bench-press 1-RM improved across groups. Body-composition and muscle-thickness effects differed by menopausal group; postmenopausal participants did not show the same hypertrophy response at the tested volume. No injuries occurred. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Progressive free-weight resistance training can safely improve measured strength in healthy middle-aged women under supervised study conditions. |
| Prohibited overreach | One small study does not establish superiority to HIIT or group fitness, effects on longevity, hormones, bone density, metabolic health or mental health, or a universal volume threshold for all women over 35. |
| Linked authority | Existing Evolved Manual progression authority |

### STU-EXE-0004 — Exercise selection variation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kassiano W, et al. *Does varying resistance exercises promote superior muscle hypertrophy and strength gains? A systematic review.* J Strength Cond Res. 2022;36:1753–1762. |
| Locator | [PMID 35438660](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35438660/); DOI `10.1519/JSC.0000000000004258` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of a small and heterogeneous body of resistance-training studies. |
| Finding | Some systematic variation may improve regional hypertrophy or dynamic strength; excessive or random high-frequency exercise rotation may compromise adaptations. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise selection should be purposeful enough to practise, measure and progress while allowing planned variation for goals and context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that all varied group fitness prevents adaptation, that every session must repeat identical exercises, or that progressive overload is impossible outside fixed programmes. The carousel printed the PMID as `36438660`; the correct PMID is `35438660`. |
| Linked authority | Existing Evolved Manual programming authority |

### STU-EXE-0005 — Overtraining consensus boundary

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Meeusen R, et al. *Prevention, diagnosis, and treatment of the overtraining syndrome: joint consensus statement of the ECSS and ACSM.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2013;45:186–205. |
| Locator | [PMID 23247672](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23247672/); DOI `10.1249/MSS.0b013e318279a10a` |
| Design and population | Joint professional consensus focused on athletes. |
| Finding | Balancing overload and recovery matters, but non-functional overreaching and overtraining syndrome are difficult to distinguish and require clinical outcome and exclusion of other explanations. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Trainers may notice persistent underperformance and recovery concerns but must not label ordinary fatigue, life stress or one difficult week as overtraining syndrome. |
| Prohibited overreach | No fixed class-count, sleep-hour, calorie or cortisol threshold is established for Evolved members. |
| Linked authority | DOC-REC-0001; DOC-REC-0002 |

### STU-NUT-0002 — Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport consensus

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mountjoy M, et al. *2023 International Olympic Committee's consensus statement on Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport (REDs).* Br J Sports Med. 2023;57:1073–1097. |
| Locator | [DOI 10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994) |
| Design and population | International clinical and research consensus concerning athletes exposed to problematic low energy availability. |
| Finding | Prolonged or severe low energy availability can impair physiological and psychological function. Assessment is multifactorial and clinical; cortisol is only one potential indicator and not a stand-alone diagnosis. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Adequate energy availability is relevant to training, recovery and health; suspected REDs or menstrual, bone, endocrine or eating-disorder concerns require qualified clinical assessment. |
| Prohibited overreach | Standard trainers do not diagnose REDs, infer it from a calorie estimate or symptom cluster, or treat it through hormone narratives or a generic meal plan. |
| Linked authority | DOC-REC-0001; DOC-REC-0002; Nutrition and clinical-care boundary |

### STU-EXE-0006 — Resistance training in postmenopausal women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Sá KMM, et al. *Resistance training for postmenopausal women: systematic review and meta-analysis.* Menopause. 2023;30:108–116. |
| Locator | [PMID 36283059](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36283059/); DOI `10.1097/GME.0000000000002079` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of resistance-training studies in postmenopausal women. |
| Finding | Resistance training may improve functional capacity and some other outcomes, but certainty was low to very low and several estimates were imprecise. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training is a useful, individualised component of exercise for postmenopausal women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not support guaranteed bone, symptom, body-composition, hormone, longevity or mental-health outcomes, or universal superiority to other exercise. |

### STU-EXE-0007 — General resistance-training prescription synthesis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Currier BS, et al. *Resistance training prescription for muscle strength and hypertrophy in healthy adults: a systematic review and Bayesian network meta-analysis.* Br J Sports Med. 2023;57:1211–1220. |
| Locator | [PMID 37414459](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37414459/); [PMCID PMC10579494](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10579494/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2023-106807` |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of randomised trials; strength network 178 studies/5,097 participants and hypertrophy network 119 studies/3,364 participants; approximately 45–47% women. |
| Finding | Resistance-training prescriptions generally improved strength and hypertrophy versus no exercise; higher loads ranked highest for strength and multiple sets ranked highest for hypertrophy. Most direct between-prescription differences were small or uncertain, and study risk of bias was mostly moderate to high. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Repeated resistance training with an appropriate progressive prescription supports strength and muscle adaptation; several workable prescriptions exist. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish a single perfect programme, that constant novelty is always harmful, or that one branded strength method is universally superior. |

### STU-EXE-0008 — Current ACSM resistance-training position stand

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Currier BS, et al. *American College of Sports Medicine Position Stand. Resistance Training Prescription for Muscle Function, Hypertrophy, and Physical Performance in Healthy Adults: An Overview of Reviews.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2026;58:851–872. |
| Locator | [PMID 41843416](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41843416/); [PMCID PMC12965823](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12965823/); DOI `10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897` |
| Design and population | Official evidence-based position stand synthesising 137 systematic reviews and more than 30,000 healthy adult participants. |
| Finding | Progressive resistance training improves strength, hypertrophy, power, endurance and several physical-function outcomes; prescription can be adjusted to the goal, and many programme variables do not consistently determine outcomes. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Current scientific ceiling for general healthy-adult resistance-training claims and progressive programme design. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not displace individualisation, clinical restrictions or Evolved Manual authority, and does not establish superiority for every health outcome or every woman over 35. |

### STU-EXE-0009 — HIIT body-composition evidence in women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dupuit M, et al. *Effect of high intensity interval training on body composition in women before and after menopause: a meta-analysis.* Exp Physiol. 2020;105:1470–1490. |
| Locator | [PMID 32613697](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32613697/); DOI `10.1113/EP088654` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 38 studies involving 959 women. |
| Finding | HIIT reduced weight, total fat and abdominal fat overall; effects were clearer before menopause, and evidence for postmenopausal women remained insufficient for firm conclusions. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | HIIT can be a useful exercise option for some women and is not inherently harmful because it is intense or delivered in a group setting. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show HIIT is necessary, suitable at every dose or superior to resistance training; body-composition findings do not answer strength, bone, adherence or individual recovery questions. |

## Evolved Studies spreadsheet intake

The records below reconcile the 18 entries in `SRC-EVO-GS-001`, the `Evolved Studies` tab of the Evolved Blog Topic Queue. The spreadsheet is a discovery index, not evidence authority: its `Evidence Tier` and `Blog Safe?` labels were not imported as approval decisions. STU-EXE-0003 is the one exact duplicate and retains its existing bounded state. Two current syntheses not listed in the sheet were added because they materially control interpretation of the supplied pregnancy and postnatal-depression papers.

### STU-EXE-0010 — Older weighted-exercise and postmenopausal bone review

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zehnacker CH, Bemis-Dougherty A. *Effect of weighted exercises on bone mineral density in post menopausal women. A systematic review.* J Geriatr Phys Ther. 2007;30:79–88. |
| Locator | [PMID 18171491](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18171491/); DOI `10.1519/00139143-200708000-00007` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of 20 English-language intervention reports published from 1990 to February 2005 in postmenopausal women. |
| Finding | The authors reported site-specific maintenance or improvement in bone mineral density with weighted exercise, particularly in programmes using substantial loading over long durations. An independent DARE appraisal found poor reporting of review methods, heterogeneous studies and insufficient supporting data. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Historical context that loading exercise has long been studied for postmenopausal bone health. |
| Prohibited overreach | Not a current primary anchor for osteoporosis prevention, a universal loading prescription or an assurance that resistance training alone increases bone density. Do not use its exact 70%–90% 1-RM, frequency or lifelong prescription as an Evolved rule. |
| Supersession | Prefer STU-EXE-0006 and current bone-specific syntheses for public claims. Clinical osteoporosis decisions remain with qualified clinicians. |

### STU-EXE-0011 — NSCA educational excerpt on acute hormonal responses

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | National Strength and Conditioning Association. *Hormonal Response to Muscle Contraction.* Kinetic Select. September 2021; excerpt from *Strength Training*, 2nd ed. |
| Locator | [NSCA article](https://www.nsca.com/education/articles/kinetic-select/hormonal-response-to-muscle-contraction/) |
| Design and population | Educational book excerpt for coaches; not a peer-reviewed study, systematic review or position statement. |
| Finding | Describes acute endocrine signalling during resistance exercise and offers programme-design interpretation. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Plain-language background and discovery of primary references only. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot establish that transient post-exercise hormones cause hypertrophy, that a particular acute hormonal response predicts long-term results, or that Evolved can diagnose or optimise an individual member's endocrine state. |

### STU-EXE-0012 — Strength training and quality-of-life outcomes during pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Redondo-Delgado P, et al. *Effects of strength training on quality of life in pregnant women: A systematic review.* Acta Obstet Gynecol Scand. 2025;104:1231–1243. |
| Locator | [PMID 40230063](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40230063/); [PMCID PMC12144601](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12144601/); DOI `10.1111/aogs.15122` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of nine supervised RCTs in healthy pregnant women; interventions lasted at least six weeks and often combined strength with other exercise. Outcomes and prescriptions were heterogeneous, and several risk-of-bias concerns remained. |
| Finding | Included trials reported improvements in selected activity, pain, mood, energy, sleep, gestational-weight-gain and health-related quality-of-life outcomes, but strength training alone could not be isolated and the authors could not meta-analyse the diverse outcomes. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Appropriately screened pregnant women without contraindications may obtain wellbeing and functional benefits from supervised strength-containing exercise programmes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove benefit for every outcome, trimester or pregnancy; establish fetal or delivery safety by itself; support maximal lifting; or replace obstetric clearance and pregnancy-specific modification. |
| Linked authority | STU-EXE-0024; pregnancy/postpartum/IVF coaching scope and clinical escalation controls. |

### STU-EXE-0013 — Supervised low-to-moderate strength training adopted in pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | O'Connor PJ, et al. *Safety and efficacy of supervised strength training adopted in pregnancy.* J Phys Act Health. 2011;8:309–320. |
| Locator | [PMCID PMC4203346](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4203346/); DOI `10.1123/jpah.8.3.309` |
| Design and population | Uncontrolled 12-week intervention in 32 previously untrained women at 21–25 weeks of uncomplicated singleton pregnancy, selected for back pain or back-pain history. Training was supervised twice weekly at low-to-moderate perceived intensity. |
| Finding | No musculoskeletal injuries occurred, potentially adverse symptoms were infrequent, training loads progressed and lumbar endurance improved. There was no non-training control group for the efficacy measures. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Feasibility and short-term safety signal for carefully screened, supervised, low-to-moderate resistance training in this narrow population. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish universal safety, safety in complicated pregnancy or every trimester, fetal and birth outcomes, maximal-strength training, unsupervised adoption or treatment of back pain. |

### STU-EXE-0014 — Narrative review of resistance training in pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Sonaiya N. *Resistance Training in Pregnancy — A Comprehensive Narrative Review.* Int J Health Sci Res. 2024;14. |
| Locator | DOI `10.52403/ijhsr.20240811`; [publisher PDF](https://www.ijhsr.org/IJHSR_Vol.14_Issue.8_August2024/IJHSR11.pdf) |
| Design and population | Broad narrative review covering resistance training and several maternal, fetal and postpartum outcomes; no systematic selection, meta-analysis or certainty assessment sufficient for an anchor claim. The spreadsheet misspelled the author's surname as `Sonalya`. |
| Finding | Provides a broad descriptive overview and cites relevant research and guidance. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Discovery source for primary studies and topic mapping. |
| Prohibited overreach | Not principal evidence for pregnancy safety, diastasis, gestational diabetes, incontinence, pain, fetal response or postpartum depression. |

### STU-EXE-0015 — 2025 pregnancy strength review in Quality in Sport

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Demel K, et al. *Strength training in pregnancy. Systematic review of benefits and risks.* Quality in Sport. 2025;38:58312. |
| Locator | DOI `10.12775/QS.2025.38.58312`; [journal record](https://apcz.umk.pl/QS/article/view/58312) |
| Design and population | Review article describing maternal and fetal benefits and risks. Its accessible record does not provide the quantitative synthesis, certainty grading or reporting depth needed to outrank the stronger 2025 pregnancy meta-analysis. |
| Finding | Concludes that properly supervised strength training can be beneficial during uncomplicated pregnancy while requiring individualisation and contraindication screening. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Discovery and corroborative background after stronger evidence has established the bounded claim. |
| Prohibited overreach | Not the principal citation for safety, complication reduction, fetal outcomes or a detailed training prescription. |
| Supersession | STU-EXE-0024 controls pregnancy outcome claims. |

### STU-EXE-0016 — Menopausal status, physical activity, muscle and bone mass

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Sipilä S, et al. *Muscle and bone mass in middle-aged women: role of menopausal status and physical activity.* J Cachexia Sarcopenia Muscle. 2020. |
| Locator | [PMID 32017473](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32017473/); [PMCID PMC7296268](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7296268/) |
| Design and population | Cross-sectional ERMA study of Finnish women aged 47–55, classified by menstrual diaries and hormone measures; physical activity was self-reported. |
| Finding | Muscle mass and femoral-neck bone density declined across menopausal groups. Higher physical activity was associated with greater appendicular lean mass, especially later in the transition; associations with bone density were weaker and less consistent. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Menopause transition is relevant to muscle and bone health, and being physically active is associated with greater muscle mass in midlife women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cross-sectional associations do not prove that physical activity or resistance training caused the differences, quantify an individual's expected loss, or establish an exercise treatment for osteoporosis. |

### STU-EXE-0017 — Resistance training and menopause-specific quality of life

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Berin E, et al. *Effects of resistance training on quality of life in postmenopausal women with vasomotor symptoms.* Climacteric. 2022;25:264–270. |
| Locator | [PMID 34240669](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34240669/); DOI `10.1080/13697137.2021.1941849` |
| Design and population | Open RCT; 65 postmenopausal women older than 45 with daily vasomotor symptoms were allocated to 15 weeks of resistance training three times weekly or untreated control; 57–58 contributed to analyses. |
| Finding | Training improved Women's Health Questionnaire vasomotor, sleep and menstrual-symptom domains versus control. No significant between-group difference appeared in SF-36 summary scores or domains. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | A progressive resistance programme may improve menopause-specific quality-of-life domains for some symptomatic postmenopausal women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish a cure for hot flushes, a guaranteed sleep benefit, changes in hormones, equivalence or superiority to clinical treatment, or effects in asymptomatic or perimenopausal women. |

### STU-EXE-0018 — 2009 youth resistance-training position statement

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Faigenbaum AD, et al. *Youth resistance training: updated position statement paper from the National Strength and Conditioning Association.* J Strength Cond Res. 2009;23 Suppl:S60–S79. |
| Locator | [PMID 19620931](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19620931/); DOI `10.1519/JSC.0b013e31819df407` |
| Design and population | Expert position statement based on the evidence available through 2009 for children and adolescents. |
| Finding | Supports appropriately designed and supervised resistance training for youth and provides age-specific programme considerations. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Historical professional consensus that youth resistance training is not inherently unsafe when properly designed and supervised. |
| Prohibited overreach | Not a current standalone authority for Evolved's adolescent service, readiness, safeguarding, load selection or parent-consent rules. It does not justify treating adolescents as small adults. |

### STU-EXE-0019 — Twice-weekly strength training and adiposity in premenopausal women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Schmitz KH, et al. *Strength training and adiposity in premenopausal women: Strong, Healthy, and Empowered study.* Am J Clin Nutr. 2007;86:566–572. |
| Locator | [PMID 17823418](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17823418/); DOI `10.1093/ajcn/86.3.566` |
| Design and population | Two-year RCT in 164 women aged 25–44 with BMI 25–35; twice-weekly strength training was compared with standard-care brochures recommending aerobic exercise. |
| Finding | The training group had a more favourable change in percentage body fat and a smaller increase in intra-abdominal fat than the comparison group. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Sustained twice-weekly strength training can contribute to long-term adiposity management in overweight and obese premenopausal women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show immediate fat loss, superiority to a matched aerobic programme, effectiveness without adherence or nutrition context, or transferability to all ages, body sizes or menopausal stages. |

### STU-EXE-0020 — Antenatal and postnatal pelvic-floor muscle training

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Woodley SJ, et al. *Pelvic floor muscle training for preventing and treating urinary and faecal incontinence in antenatal and postnatal women.* Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2020;5:CD007471. |
| Locator | [PMID 32378735](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32378735/); [PMCID PMC7203602](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7203602/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD007471.pub4` |
| Design and population | Cochrane review of 46 randomised or quasi-randomised trials involving 10,832 pregnant or recently postnatal women; programmes and populations varied and certainty was generally low to moderate. |
| Finding | Structured antenatal pelvic-floor muscle training for continent women probably reduces urinary incontinence in late pregnancy and may reduce it at three to six months postpartum. Evidence for treatment of existing symptoms, population-wide postnatal programmes and faecal incontinence was uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Early structured antenatal pelvic-floor muscle training may help prevent urinary incontinence in women who are continent at baseline. Symptomatic members should be referred for appropriate assessment and individual supervision. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim that generic gym exercises treat incontinence, that one programme works for all women, that postnatal PFMT is proven as a universal treatment, or that trainers may assess or treat pelvic-floor dysfunction. |
| Linked authority | Pregnancy/postpartum clinical referral boundary; special-populations warning signs and clinician coordination. |

### STU-EXE-0021 — Individualised stabilising exercise for postpartum pelvic-girdle pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Stuge B, et al. *The efficacy of a treatment program focusing on specific stabilizing exercises for pelvic girdle pain after pregnancy: a randomized controlled trial.* Spine. 2004;29:351–359. |
| Locator | [PMID 15094530](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15094530/); DOI `10.1097/01.BRS.0000090827.16926.1D` |
| Design and population | RCT in 81 women with postpartum pelvic-girdle pain; a 20-week individualised physical-therapy programme containing specific stabilising exercise was compared with individualised physical therapy without those exercises. |
| Finding | The specific-stabilisation group reported lower pain and disability and better quality of life after treatment and at one year. Later trials of different home-based stabilisation concepts have not uniformly reproduced the effect. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | An individualised physiotherapy programme incorporating specific stabilising exercise may help some women with diagnosed postpartum pelvic-girdle pain. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not validate a generic core class, self-directed rehabilitation, a particular commercial apparatus, or treatment by a standard gym trainer. Pain assessment and rehabilitation remain clinical or pelvic-health physiotherapy care. |

### STU-EXE-0022 — Exercise and lactation-associated bone loss

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Lovelady CA, et al. *Effect of exercise training on loss of bone mineral density during lactation.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2009;41:1902–1907. |
| Locator | [PMID 19727023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19727023/); DOI `10.1249/MSS.0b013e3181a5a68b` |
| Design and population | Small RCT; 20 lactating women at four weeks postpartum were allocated to 16 weeks of combined weight-bearing aerobic and resistance exercise or no exercise. |
| Finding | The exercise group lost less lumbar-spine bone mineral density and less lean mass; hip and total-body bone outcomes did not differ. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Combined progressive exercise may attenuate lumbar-spine bone loss during lactation, but the evidence is preliminary. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not isolate resistance training, prove fracture prevention, justify high-impact exercise for every postpartum woman, or replace postpartum readiness, pelvic-health and medical screening. |

### STU-MND-0001 — Postnatal-depression exercise trial protocol

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Daley AJ, et al. *The effectiveness of exercise as a treatment for postnatal depression: study protocol.* BMC Pregnancy Childbirth. 2012;12:45. |
| Locator | [PMID 22682671](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22682671/); [PMCID PMC3449184](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3449184/); DOI `10.1186/1471-2393-12-45` |
| Design and population | Protocol for a planned pragmatic RCT of exercise plus usual care versus usual care in women with clinically defined postnatal depression. It contains no trial outcomes. |
| Finding | Defines a research question, intervention and outcome plan only. |
| State | `NOT-APPROVED-FOR-CLAIM` |
| Approved use | Trial-history and methodology context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot support any statement that exercise prevents, improves or treats postnatal depression. |

### STU-EXE-0023 — Resistance training in healthy postpartum women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | LeCheminant JD, et al. *Effect of resistance training on body composition, self-efficacy, depression, and activity in postpartum women.* Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2014;24:414–421. |
| Locator | [PMID 22738284](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22738284/); DOI `10.1111/j.1600-0838.2012.01490.x` |
| Design and population | RCT in 60 healthy postpartum women; 18 weeks of twice-weekly resistance training was compared with flexibility training, with 44 completers. |
| Finding | Resistance training produced greater strength, exercise self-efficacy and favourable activity changes. Body composition did not differ; depressive symptoms fell within the resistance group, but the between-group interaction was not significant. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Twice-weekly resistance training can improve strength and may support exercise confidence and activity in appropriately screened healthy postpartum women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove treatment of postnatal depression, superior body-composition change, readiness immediately after birth or safety for symptomatic or clinically complicated postpartum women. |

### STU-MND-0002 — Older exercise and postnatal-depression synthesis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Daley A, Jolly K, MacArthur C. *The effectiveness of exercise in the management of post-natal depression: systematic review and meta-analysis.* Fam Pract. 2009;26:154–162. |
| Locator | [PMID 19126829](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19126829/); DOI `10.1093/fampra/cmn101`; [DARE appraisal](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK76962/) |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of five small RCTs or quasi-RCTs in women with postnatal depression. |
| Finding | The pooled estimate favoured exercise but was heterogeneous; removing a study that combined exercise with social support reduced the effect and made it non-significant. The authors concluded that effectiveness was uncertain. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Historical evidence timeline and an example of why small heterogeneous early literatures require caution. |
| Prohibited overreach | Not a current primary citation that exercise treats postnatal depression, and not evidence for resistance training specifically. |
| Supersession | STU-MND-0003 controls the current bounded physical-activity statement. |

### STU-NUT-0003 — Multi-ingredient supplement timing in trained men

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cribb PJ, Hayes A. *Effects of supplement timing and resistance exercise on skeletal muscle hypertrophy.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2006;38:1918–1925. |
| Locator | [PMID 17095924](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17095924/); DOI `10.1249/01.mss.0000233790.08788.3e` |
| Design and population | Small single-blind randomised study in resistance-trained young men; analysed groups consumed the same large multi-ingredient protein, glucose and creatine supplement either immediately around training or more than five hours away from training during a 10-week programme. |
| Finding | The peri-workout group showed larger changes in several strength, lean-mass, fibre and intramuscular creatine/glycogen outcomes. The design cannot isolate protein timing from carbohydrate, creatine, dose or timing context. |
| State | `NOT-APPROVED-FOR-CLAIM` |
| Approved use | Historical discovery source showing that multi-ingredient peri-workout timing has been experimentally studied in trained men. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot support a universal anabolic window, mandatory pre/post supplements, claims about ordinary food timing, women, beginners, pregnancy, postpartum or menopause, or a standard-coaching supplement prescription. Explicit individual fuelling and supplement advice remains Megan 1:1 or clinical care as applicable. |

### STU-NUT-0004 — Dietary creatine and reported female reproductive-health history

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ostojic SM, et al. *Association between dietary intake of creatine and female reproductive health: Evidence from NHANES 2017–2020.* Food Sci Nutr. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39055234](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39055234/); [PMCID PMC11266896](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11266896/); DOI `10.1002/fsn3.4135` |
| Design and population | Cross-sectional secondary analysis of 4,522 US female NHANES respondents aged 12 years and older. Food-derived creatine was estimated from self-reported dietary data; reproductive outcomes were largely self-reported histories. Supplements were excluded. |
| Finding | Intake at or above the authors' 13 mg/kg/day threshold was associated with lower odds of reported irregular periods. Intake below the threshold was associated with higher odds of several historical obstetric, pelvic-surgery and hormone-therapy outcomes. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Hypothesis-generating evidence that food-derived creatine intake correlates with selected reproductive-health histories and deserves prospective research. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that creatine prevents irregular periods, infection, fetal macrosomia, hysterectomy, oophorectomy or need for menopausal hormone therapy; does not test creatine supplementation; does not establish a female dietary requirement; and does not support pregnancy, fertility or hormone-treatment advice. |
| Material limitations | Cross-sectional design, self-reported exposure and outcomes, no tissue or circulating creatine measure, residual confounding and reverse causation, a threshold the authors describe as somewhat arbitrary, heterogeneous age/life-stage pooling, and declared creatine-industry research support to the lead author. |
| Linked authority | Nutrition doctrine evidence boundary; supplements remain Megan 1:1 or clinical care where relevant. |

### STU-NUT-0005 — Meat consumption, APOE genotype and cognitive ageing

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Norgren J, et al. *Meat Consumption and Cognitive Health by APOE Genotype.* JAMA Netw Open. 2026;9:e266489. |
| Locator | [PMID 41854609](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41854609/); [PMCID PMC13003371](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13003371/); DOI `10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2026.6489` |
| Design and population | Prospective population cohort using the Swedish SNAC-K study: 2,157 dementia-free adults aged 60 or older, predominantly of Northern European ancestry, followed for up to 15 years; 569 had APOE ε3/ε4 or ε4/ε4 genotypes. Meat intake was assessed with food-frequency questionnaires. |
| Finding | Within the APOE ε3/ε4 or ε4/ε4 subgroup, the highest versus lowest total-meat quintile was associated with a better 10-year cognitive trajectory and lower dementia incidence (sHR 0.45, 95% CI 0.21–0.95). The genotype interaction was supported for global cognition and episodic memory, but not statistically established for dementia incidence (interaction P=0.10). A higher processed-to-total-meat ratio was unfavourably associated with dementia, without genotype interaction. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Emerging observational evidence that APOE genotype may modify associations between dietary patterns containing meat and cognitive ageing; processed and unprocessed meat should not be collapsed into one exposure. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that meat prevents dementia, establish two servings daily as treatment, justify genetic testing or genotype-specific dietary prescription in coaching, show that red meat is superior to poultry, or override cardiovascular, cancer-prevention, cultural and whole-diet considerations. |
| Material limitations | Single observational cohort, self-reported diet, possible survival and residual-confounding bias, limited ancestry generalisability, multiple subgroup and post hoc analyses, and a borderline within-subgroup dementia estimate without a significant genotype interaction for that endpoint. |
| Linked authority | Nutrition doctrine; cognition evidence pack; APOE testing and dementia prevention remain clinical care. |

## Current reconciliation anchors added during review

### STU-EXE-0024 — Current pregnancy resistance-training outcomes synthesis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Prevett C, et al. *Resistance training in pregnancy: systematic review and meta-analysis of pregnancy, delivery, fetal and pelvic floor outcomes and call to action.* Br J Sports Med. 2025;59:1173–1182. |
| Locator | [PMID 40610191](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40610191/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2024-109123` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 50 studies involving 47,619 participants; 45 were RCTs, but 90% used multicomponent programmes and resistance-training dose and progression were poorly reported. |
| Finding | Strength-containing interventions were associated with lower odds of several maternal and fetal outcomes without evidence of harm in the reviewed populations, but the contribution of resistance training alone and the optimal prescription remain unclear. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Appropriately screened pregnant women without contraindications can include suitably modified resistance training within a broader evidence-based activity programme. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that resistance training alone caused every pooled benefit, establish high-load or maximal training safety, remove individual contraindications, or permit a generic programme for complicated pregnancy. |
| Social-claim reconciliation | The reviewed carousel's four odds-reduction figures reflect pooled associations, but `I²=0%` does not mean every population or trial showed the benefit. Because 90% of programmes were multicomponent and resistance dose was poorly reported, it is speculative—not an evidence result—to say effects are necessarily underestimated, that the review establishes a higher unseen “ceiling,” or that prescribing lighter training is itself risky. |
| Linked authority | Controls STU-EXE-0012 through STU-EXE-0015 and preserves the standard-coaching versus specialist/clinical boundary. |

### STU-EXE-0025 — Heavy resistance training at retirement age and four-year strength preservation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bloch-Ibenfeldt M, et al. *Heavy resistance training at retirement age induces 4-year lasting beneficial effects in muscle strength: a long-term follow-up of an RCT.* BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med. 2024;10:e001899. |
| Locator | [PMID 38911477](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38911477/); [PMCID PMC11191791](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11191791/); DOI `10.1136/bmjsem-2024-001899` |
| Design and population | Long-term follow-up of the Danish LISA parallel-group RCT. At baseline, 451 well-functioning adults around retirement age were randomised to one year of supervised heavy resistance training, moderate-intensity training or no prescribed exercise. At year four, 369 attended follow-up; their mean age was 71 and 61% were women. |
| Finding | Three years after the supervised intervention ended, the heavy-training group had maintained baseline isometric quadriceps strength while the moderate and control groups declined. The primary outcome, leg extensor power, did not show the same lasting group advantage. Total lean mass was broadly maintained in the heavy group, but differences were small; handgrip strength and leg lean mass did not show a persistent training effect. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | A sustained, supervised period of appropriately heavy resistance training around retirement age can produce durable preservation of specific lower-body strength measures in healthy, functioning older adults. It supports progressive strength development before and through later life rather than merely advising people to remain generally active. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say that one hard year guarantees three exercise-free years of benefit, that all strength or muscle outcomes were preserved, that heavy training was superior for the primary leg-power outcome, or that the protocol transfers unchanged to frail, untrained, symptomatic or clinically complex members. “Heavy” remains individual, progressive and technique- and readiness-dependent. |
| Material limitations | The sustained result was a secondary outcome; the cohort was unusually active and well functioning, 82 participants did not attend year-four testing, and post-intervention activity was not experimentally controlled. |
| Linked authority | Strength and progressive-overload doctrine; later-life exercise and standard-coaching readiness boundary. |

### STU-MND-0003 — Current overview of physical activity and postpartum-depression symptoms

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Campos-Marin Á, et al. *What Interventions Focused on Physical Activity Could Improve Postpartum Depression Symptoms? An Overview of Systematic Reviews with Meta-Analysis.* Healthcare. 2025;13:1419. |
| Locator | [PMID 40565446](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40565446/); [PMCID PMC12192973](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12192973/); DOI `10.3390/healthcare13121419` |
| Design and population | Overview of eight systematic reviews with meta-analysis, searched to February 2025; interventions and clinical purposes varied, and prevention and treatment were not consistently separated. |
| Finding | Aerobic exercise, walking, yoga and multimodal activity may improve postpartum-depression symptoms, with important methodological and clinical limitations. It does not establish a resistance-training-specific treatment effect. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Physical activity can be presented as a potentially helpful adjunct for postpartum wellbeing and depressive symptoms when appropriately supported. |
| Prohibited overreach | Exercise is not a substitute for screening, diagnosis, psychotherapy, medication or crisis care. Trainers must not diagnose or treat postnatal depression, and resistance training cannot claim a modality-specific effect from this overview. |
| Linked authority | DOC-MND-0009 and the clinical-care boundary. |

## Gestational-diabetes, muscle-health and pregnancy-lifestyle records

### STU-GDM-0001 — Mid-pregnancy handgrip strength and gestational hyperglycaemia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zu P, et al. *The Association of Handgrip Strength in the Second Trimester with Gestational Hyperglycemia.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2026;58:1664–1673. |
| Locator | [PMID 41917766](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41917766/); DOI `10.1249/MSS.0000000000003985` |
| Design and population | Prospective cohort of 1,904 pregnant women in Hefei, China; handgrip was measured at 16–23 gestational weeks and glucose outcomes were assessed later. |
| Finding | Higher grip strength was associated with lower GDM prevalence and more favourable glucose, insulin and HOMA-IR measures. Adding grip strength improved the study's prediction model. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Mid-pregnancy grip strength may be a useful observational marker of metabolic health and GDM risk in some populations. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that strength training prevents GDM, establish causality, validate a universal 24.1 kg cutoff, or authorise grip testing to replace Australian clinical screening. |
| Source correction | The carousel's separate “Wang et al., Med Sci Sports Exerc, 2026” entry appears to duplicate this paper through co-author Peng Wang; it is not banked twice. |

### STU-MET-0001 — CT-defined myosteatosis and insulin resistance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kim MJ, et al. *Association Between Insulin Resistance and Myosteatosis Measured by Abdominal Computed Tomography.* J Clin Endocrinol Metab. 2023;108:3100–3110. |
| Locator | [PMID 37401630](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37401630/); DOI `10.1210/clinem/dgad382` |
| Design and population | Cross-sectional health-screening study of 18,251 nonpregnant Korean adults using abdominal CT muscle measures and HOMA-IR. |
| Finding | Greater insulin resistance was associated with CT measures consistent with myosteatosis. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Mechanistic and population context that muscle composition and insulin resistance are associated in adults. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish temporality or causality, apply directly to pregnancy, prove that myosteatosis causes GDM, or support CT screening in coaching. |

### STU-MET-0002 — Muscle ultrasound echo intensity and insulin resistance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Soliman SB, et al. *Muscle Ultrasound Echo Intensity Detects Insulin Resistance Before Changes in HbA1c or Fasting Glucose, Independent of BMI: A Novel Tool for Early Risk Stratification.* J Ultrasound Med. 2026;45:937–946. |
| Locator | [PMID 41250958](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41250958/); [PMCID PMC12658964](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12658964/); DOI `10.1002/jum.70126` |
| Design and population | Small cross-sectional proof-of-concept study in 20 nonpregnant adults with obesity and eight lean controls; muscle ultrasound, OGTT-derived insulin sensitivity and DXA were assessed. |
| Finding | Muscle echo intensity was associated with insulin-resistance measures despite normal conventional glycaemic markers in the sample. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Emerging method-development context for the relationship between muscle quality and insulin sensitivity. |
| Prohibited overreach | The cross-sectional design cannot show that ultrasound detects deterioration “before” blood markers over time. It is not pregnancy evidence, a validated clinical screen, or a coaching assessment. |

### STU-GDM-0002 — Early-pregnancy body composition and GDM under different prepregnancy weights

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Li X, et al. *Correlation of body composition in early pregnancy on gestational diabetes mellitus under different body weights before pregnancy.* Front Endocrinol. 2022;13:916883. |
| Locator | [PMCID PMC9649916](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9649916/); DOI `10.3389/fendo.2022.916883` |
| Design and population | Retrospective study of 6,698 pregnant women, including 1,109 with GDM; first-trimester body composition was estimated using bioelectrical impedance. |
| Finding | Body-size and composition measures differed between groups, but associations changed materially with adjustment. In the fullest overall model, waist-to-hip ratio remained the clearest positive correlate; early gestational weight gain was the strongest reported predictor among the compared measures. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Early-pregnancy adiposity distribution and weight trajectory may add information to GDM-risk assessment in the studied population. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that more muscle independently prevents GDM, that body composition outperforms BMI generally, or that BIA values are diagnostic or transportable to Australian members. |

### STU-GDM-0003 — Pregnancy lifestyle interventions for GDM prevention

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Allotey J, et al. *Effects of lifestyle interventions in pregnancy on gestational diabetes: individual participant data and network meta-analysis.* BMJ. 2026;392:e084159. |
| Locator | [PMID 41494781](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41494781/); [PMCID PMC12771886](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12771886/); DOI `10.1136/bmj-2025-084159` |
| Design and population | Individual-participant-data and network meta-analysis of 104 randomised trials involving 35,993 pregnant women; interventions included diet, physical activity and mixed lifestyle programmes. |
| Finding | Across IPD and aggregate data, lifestyle intervention modestly reduced GDM overall (OR 0.80, 95% CI 0.73–0.88; about 26 fewer cases per 1,000), with heterogeneity by intervention and diagnostic definition. The IPD-only and NICE-defined estimates were less conclusive. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Appropriately designed pregnancy lifestyle programmes can modestly reduce GDM risk at population level; the effect is not uniform across all programme types or diagnostic definitions. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not isolate resistance training, muscle gain or “muscle quality” as the mechanism; guarantee prevention; replace antenatal care; or support a single universal diet or exercise prescription. |

### STU-GDM-0004 — Diet and exercise interventions after GDM diagnosis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhang L, et al. *Effects of Dietary Approaches and Exercise Interventions on Gestational Diabetes Mellitus: A Systematic Review and Bayesian Network Meta-analysis.* Adv Nutr. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39481539](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39481539/); [PMCID PMC11629230](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11629230/) |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of 39 randomised trials reported in 41 papers, involving 2,712 pregnant women already diagnosed with GDM and 15 intervention types. |
| Finding | Some dietary approaches, particularly DASH and low-glycaemic-index patterns, improved selected glucose outcomes; comparative certainty was generally low or very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Diet and exercise can be components of clinician-led GDM management, with intervention choice requiring individual and clinical context. |
| Prohibited overreach | This is treatment evidence after diagnosis, not prevention evidence. It does not show that strength or muscle prevents GDM and does not authorise standard trainers to prescribe GDM treatment. |

### GUI-GDM-0001 — ADA Standards of Care in pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | American Diabetes Association Professional Practice Committee. *Management of Diabetes in Pregnancy: Standards of Care in Diabetes—2026.* Diabetes Care. 2026. |
| Locator | [Official ADA section](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/49/Supplement_1/S321/163918/15-Management-of-Diabetes-in-Pregnancy-Standards); [PMID 41358885](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41358885/); DOI `10.2337/dc26-S015` |
| Design and population | US professional clinical-practice standard covering pre-existing diabetes and GDM in pregnancy. |
| Finding | Sets clinical screening, management, monitoring, nutrition and physical-activity recommendations for diabetes in pregnancy. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Current international clinical context and corroboration for physical activity as part of pregnancy diabetes care. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not make Evolved a clinical provider, does not validate social-media screening tests, and does not displace Australian diagnostic and referral authority. |
| Supersession | GUI-GDM-0002 controls the Australian screening boundary. |

### STU-GDM-0005 — Early-pregnancy fat and fat-free mass associations with GDM

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Li X, et al. *Association between body composition in early pregnancy and the risk of gestational diabetes mellitus.* Front Nutr. 2025;12:1565986. |
| Locator | [PMID 40469674](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40469674/); [PMCID PMC12133464](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12133464/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2025.1565986` |
| Design and population | Retrospective cohort of 3,159 pregnant women in China; first-trimester body composition was estimated by bioelectrical impedance and 280 women developed GDM. |
| Finding | Percentage body fat and fat mass remained positively associated with GDM after multivariable adjustment including BMI. Adjusted tertile and per-standard-deviation estimates for fat-free and lean mass were not clearly protective, although the authors' spline analyses reported inverse patterns. Study-derived turning points were exploratory. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Early-pregnancy body composition may contain risk information beyond total body weight in the studied population. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that muscle “predicts GDM better than the scale,” measure muscle metabolic quality directly, establish causality, or validate its BIA thresholds for Australian screening or coaching. |

### GUI-GDM-0002 — Australian gestational-diabetes screening boundary

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Sweeting A, et al. *Australasian Diabetes in Pregnancy Society (ADIPS) 2025 consensus recommendations for the screening, diagnosis and classification of gestational diabetes.* Med J Aust. 2025;223:161–167. |
| Locator | [PMCID PMC12318493](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12318493/) |
| Design and population | Australian and New Zealand consensus recommendations for detection and diagnosis of hyperglycaemia in pregnancy. |
| Finding | Women without diabetes detected earlier in pregnancy should be offered a 75 g two-hour pregnancy oral glucose-tolerance test at 24–28 weeks, with risk-based earlier assessment where indicated. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Controls Evolved's Australian clinical boundary: GDM risk, testing, diagnosis and management belong in antenatal clinical care; trainers reinforce attendance and appropriate referral. |
| Prohibited overreach | Grip strength, body composition, ultrasound, appearance, symptoms or coaching observations must not be presented as a replacement for the recommended clinical pathway. |

## PubMed evidence expansion — 30 new records

These records were selected in five coherent six-paper batches after deduplication against every stable locator already in this bank. Selection deliberately included corroborating, boundary-setting and disconfirming evidence. A search result is not approval: only the bounded use recorded below may cascade.

### STU-EXE-0026 — Resistance-training volume in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Radaelli R, et al. *Effects of Resistance Training Volume on Physical Function, Lean Body Mass and Lower-Body Muscle Hypertrophy and Strength in Older Adults: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis of 151 Randomised Trials.* Sports Med. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 39405023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39405023/) |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis; 151 supervised-training trials and 6,306 adults aged at least 60. Findings were mainly driven by physically healthy older adults. |
| Finding | Low-volume training improved several functional, lean-mass and hypertrophy outcomes; moderate or high volume ranked better for lower-limb strength. Indirect ranking and programme categorisation limit precision. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | A low-volume resistance programme can be a useful effective entry dose for healthy older adults; more volume may be useful when maximising strength is the priority and recovery permits. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish one universal dose, prove that low volume is always optimal, or transfer directly to frail, impaired or clinically complex members. |

### STU-EXE-0027 — Single versus multiple sets in middle and older age

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Marques DL, et al. *Manipulating the Resistance Training Volume in Middle-Aged and Older Adults: A Systematic Review with Meta-Analysis.* Sports Med. 2023;53:503–518. |
| Locator | [PMID 36307745](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36307745/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-022-01769-x` |
| Design and population | Fifteen controlled studies; 430 adults aged 50 or older, 93% women. |
| Finding | Multiple sets produced small additional lower-limb strength and muscle-quality gains, with no clear advantage for upper-limb strength, muscle size or function. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Multiple sets can be progressed when lower-body strength is a priority, while a single-set minimum can remain legitimate when time, confidence or recovery constrains dose. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not make a one-set programme ineffective or establish that more sets are always better. |

### STU-EXE-0028 — Power training versus traditional strength in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Balachandran A, et al. *Comparison of Power Training vs Traditional Strength Training on Physical Function in Older Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* JAMA Netw Open. 2022;5:e2211623. |
| Locator | [PMID 35544136](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35544136/); [PMCID PMC9096601](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9096601/); DOI `10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2022.11623` |
| Design and population | Twenty randomised trials; 566 older adults, about 65% women. |
| Finding | Power training had a modest physical-function advantage over traditional strength training, but certainty was low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Suitably screened, coached intent-to-move-quickly work can be considered for function after movement competency and load control are established. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not require explosive lifting for every older member or override pain, balance, bone, cardiovascular or clinical constraints. |

### STU-EXE-0029 — Resistance training and mortality associations

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Shailendra P, et al. *Resistance Training and Mortality Risk: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Am J Prev Med. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 35599175](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35599175/); DOI `10.1016/j.amepre.2022.03.020` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of ten prospective observational studies. |
| Finding | Participation in resistance training was associated with lower all-cause, cardiovascular and cancer mortality; the dose relationship was nonlinear. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training belongs within a longevity-supportive activity pattern. |
| Prohibited overreach | Observational association is not proof that lifting itself causes longer life, and the apparent dose optimum is not an individual prescription ceiling. |

### STU-EXE-0030 — Resistance training in frail and pre-frail older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Nagata CA, et al. *Are dose-response relationships of resistance training reliable to improve functional performance in frail and pre-frail older adults?* Ageing Res Rev. 2023;91:102079. |
| Locator | [PMID 37774931](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37774931/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2023.102079` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis and meta-regression of ten randomised trials involving 1,303 frail or pre-frail adults aged at least 65. |
| Finding | Resistance training improved sit-to-stand time and SPPB scores, but not every functional outcome; institutional setting and dose relationships varied. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Frailty does not automatically exclude resistance training, but prescription and supervision need population-specific clinical context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not authorise standard coaching to assess or treat frailty or to use the reported meta-regression as a universal programme formula. |

### STU-EXE-0031 — Resistance training, quality of life and depression in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kashi SK, et al. *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Resistance Training on Quality of Life, Depression, Muscle Strength, and Functional Exercise Capacity in Older Adults Aged 60 Years or More.* Biol Res Nurs. 2023;25:88–106. |
| Locator | [PMID 35968662](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35968662/); DOI `10.1177/10998004221120945` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of intervention studies in adults aged at least 60. |
| Finding | Resistance training improved strength and selected function, quality-of-life and depressive-symptom outcomes, with heterogeneity across measures and programmes. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Strength training may contribute to wellbeing as part of a broader support system. |
| Prohibited overreach | Exercise is not psychotherapy, does not treat a diagnosed mental-health condition by itself and cannot guarantee mood improvement. |

### STU-EXE-0032 — Exercise and menopause symptoms overview

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Money M, et al. *The effectiveness of exercise and physical activity programmes on menopausal symptoms: an overview of reviews.* BMC Womens Health. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39003439](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39003439/); [PMCID PMC11245773](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11245773/); DOI `10.1186/s12905-024-03243-4` |
| Design and population | Overview of 17 reviews representing 80 primary studies and 8,983 participants. |
| Finding | Yoga showed some benefit for selected symptom groupings; evidence for vasomotor and psychological symptoms and for aerobic exercise was inconclusive. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Exercise can be supported for health and function through menopause without promising that a particular modality will resolve menopause symptoms. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not justify hormonal treatment claims, symptom cures or superiority of strength training for hot flushes, sleep or mood. |

### STU-EXE-0033 — Postpartum pelvic-floor training and abdominal exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Beamish NF, et al. *Impact of postpartum exercise on pelvic floor disorders and diastasis recti abdominis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Br J Sports Med. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 39694630](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39694630/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2024-108619` |
| Design and population | Sixty-five studies involving 21,334 postpartum participants. |
| Finding | Pelvic-floor muscle training reduced urinary-incontinence odds; evidence for prolapse relied on much less data. Abdominal training reduced inter-rectus distance, but the clinical meaning was uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Postpartum return-to-training can include appropriately selected pelvic-floor and abdominal work with symptom monitoring and referral when indicated. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not support a generic “close the gap” programme, guarantee prevention or treatment, or replace pelvic-health physiotherapy. |

### STU-EXE-0034 — Conservative exercise for postnatal diastasis recti

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Benjamin DR, et al. *Conservative interventions may have little effect on reducing diastasis of the rectus abdominis in postnatal women: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Physiotherapy. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 36934466](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36934466/); DOI `10.1016/j.physio.2023.02.002` |
| Design and population | Sixteen trials involving 698 postnatal women; only three studies were at low risk of bias. |
| Finding | Abdominal exercise produced a small average reduction in inter-rectus distance that the authors did not consider clinically significant. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Programme goals should prioritise function, confidence, symptoms and progressive capacity rather than cosmetic gap closure. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not mean exercise is useless; it rejects guaranteed or clinically meaningful “repair” claims based only on gap width. |

### STU-EXE-0035 — Exercise and body composition after menopause

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Khalafi M, et al. *The effects of exercise training on body composition in postmenopausal women: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37388207](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37388207/); [PMCID PMC10306117](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10306117/) |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 101 studies involving 5,697 postmenopausal women. |
| Finding | Exercise improved fat and fat-free-mass outcomes on average; aerobic training contributed more to fat outcomes and resistance training more to muscle outcomes. Heterogeneity and measurement variation were substantial. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | A combined activity strategy can serve body-composition and function goals after menopause, with resistance training central to muscle preservation or gain. |
| Prohibited overreach | Average effects do not predict an individual result or establish that one modality solves every body-composition goal. |

### STU-EXE-0036 — Resistance-training protocols and bone density after menopause

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang L, et al. *Comparative efficacy of resistance training protocols on bone mineral density in postmenopausal women: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* Front Physiol. 2023;14:1105303. |
| Locator | [PMID 36824476](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36824476/); [PMCID PMC9941565](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9941565/); DOI `10.3389/fphys.2023.1105303` |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of 19 studies involving 919 postmenopausal women. |
| Finding | Some moderate-intensity, higher-frequency prescriptions ranked well for lumbar-spine and femoral-neck BMD, while several hip-site findings were not significant. Rankings relied partly on indirect evidence. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Progressive resistance training can form part of bone-health activity, with site, load, impact exposure and clinical status considered. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish a universal three-day prescription, guarantee BMD gain or replace osteoporosis assessment and treatment. |

### STU-EXE-0037 — Power-training intensity in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Orssatto LBR, et al. *Chronic Effects of Different Intensities of Power Training on Neuromuscular Parameters in Older People: A Systematic Review with Meta-analysis.* Sports Med Open. 2023;9:98. |
| Locator | [PMID 37874417](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37874417/); [PMCID PMC10597949](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10597949/); DOI `10.1186/s40798-023-00646-9` |
| Design and population | Three randomised trials involving 179 older adults. |
| Finding | Low-to-moderate and high-intensity power training produced similar power gains; one high-intensity knee-extension strength comparison favoured higher intensity. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Power intent need not always require high external loads; load can be selected around skill, tolerance and goal. |
| Prohibited overreach | Three trials cannot establish exact loading rules or safety for every older or clinically complex member. |

### STU-EXE-0038 — Acute fetal response to high-intensity resistance exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Moolyk AN, et al. *Maternal and fetal responses to acute high-intensity resistance exercise during pregnancy.* Br J Sports Med. 2025;59:159–166. |
| Locator | [PMID 39694629](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39694629/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2024-108804` |
| Design and population | Acute experimental study of 10 healthy pregnant participants at 26.4 ± 3.2 weeks and 10 non-pregnant comparators. Participants performed 10 repetitions of squat, bench press and deadlift at 70%, 80% and 90% of 10RM, followed by 90% of 10RM with a Valsalva manoeuvre. |
| Finding and material limitation | No fetal bradycardia occurred, fetal heart rate did not change significantly from pre- to post-exercise and umbilical Doppler indices remained within normal ranges. One supervised session in 10 healthy participants cannot establish longitudinal, rare-event, maximal-load or universal pregnancy safety. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Direct physiological support that demanding compound resistance exercise can be acutely tolerated by selected healthy pregnant women under the studied conditions. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not prescribe Valsalva, 90% loading, supine exercise or heavy lifting generically; do not transfer the result to untrained women, other gestational stages, contraindicated pregnancies or long-term fetal outcomes. |
| Related authority and review | DOC-TRN-0001–0009 and TRN-OVR-PREG remain controlling. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for a larger longitudinal trial, adverse-event signal, guideline change or materially broader claim. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0039 — Heavy-load resistance exercise, supine position and fetal wellbeing

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dalhaug EM, et al. *Pushing limits: the acute effects of a heavy-load resistance protocol and supine exercise on fetal well-being.* BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med. 2025;11:e002639. |
| Locator | [PMID 40909193](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40909193/); [PMCID PMC12406897](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12406897/); DOI `10.1136/bmjsem-2025-002639` |
| Design and population | Acute laboratory study of 48 highly active pregnant athletes at 26–35 weeks. Participants completed three sets of eight with one repetition in reserve, approximately 76% 1RM, in sumo deadlift, bench press and incline bench press. |
| Finding and material limitation | Umbilical artery pulsatility did not change and fetal heart rate was generally within normal limits. One transient bradycardia episode after bench press resolved after about three minutes; several temporary tachycardia observations remained below the study's pathological threshold. Participants were experienced, healthy and highly active; measures were acute and not continuously captured throughout each set. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Emerging direct evidence against automatically reducing every experienced, uncomplicated pregnant lifter to very light loading solely because she is pregnant. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that heavy lifting or supine exercise is safe for every pregnant woman, authorise a generic protocol, remove symptom-led modification or replace obstetric contraindication screening. |
| Related authority and review | DOC-TRN-0001–0009 and TRN-OVR-PREG remain controlling. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for replication in diverse or previously untrained populations, programme-long exposure, rare adverse outcomes or guideline change. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0040 — Moderate-to-vigorous resistance training during healthy pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Petrov Fieril K, Glantz A, Fagevik Olsen M. *The efficacy of moderate-to-vigorous resistance exercise during pregnancy: a randomized controlled trial.* Acta Obstet Gynecol Scand. 2015;94:35–42. |
| Locator | [PMID 25287282](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25287282/); DOI `10.1111/aogs.12525` |
| Design and population | Randomised trial of 92 healthy pregnant women. The intervention used supervised resistance exercise twice weekly during gestational weeks 14–25; controls received general exercise recommendations and a home programme. |
| Finding and material limitation | Measured perinatal data were normal in both groups. Newborns were heavier before adjustment, but the between-group difference attenuated after adjustment for gestational age. The modest healthy-pregnancy sample and programme-specific exposure do not cover every exercise, trimester or pregnancy context. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support supervised moderate-to-vigorous resistance exercise as a feasible option for appropriately screened healthy pregnancies. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish fetal benefit, complicated-pregnancy safety, maximal lifting safety or one universal prenatal resistance prescription. |
| Related authority and review | Controlled by STU-EXE-0024, DOC-TRN-0001–0009 and TRN-OVR-PREG. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for a stronger resistance-only trial or broader claim. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0041 — Prenatal exercise type and neonatal outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kong C, Zhou S, Xin X, Zhang Y. *The effects of different types of prenatal exercise on neonatal outcomes: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* Front Physiol. 2026;17:1863589. |
| Locator | [PMID 42488609](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42488609/); [PMCID PMC13388151](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13388151/); DOI `10.3389/fphys.2026.1863589` |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of 61 studies involving 11,036 pregnant women, comparing aerobic, resistance, mind-body, combined exercise and no intervention for birthweight, gestational age and one- and five-minute Apgar scores. |
| Finding and material limitation | Effects varied by modality; the synthesis did not identify a resistance-exercise disadvantage for the neonatal outcomes examined. Confidence was mainly moderate to low, resistance comparisons were limited and these immediate neonatal measures do not establish later child development. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Corroborate that resistance exercise has not shown an adverse neonatal signal for these outcomes within the available comparative evidence. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not rank resistance training as the best prenatal modality, infer long-term child benefit or convert a network ranking into a personal programme. |
