| Related authority and review | STU-EXE-0024 remains the resistance-specific synthesis anchor. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for certainty changes, direct resistance comparisons or broader fetal/child claims. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0042 — Prenatal exercise and neurodevelopment at 18 months

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Hellenes OM, et al. *Regular moderate exercise during pregnancy does not have an adverse effect on the neurodevelopment of the child.* Acta Paediatr. 2015;104:285–291. |
| Locator | [PMID 25471255](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25471255/); DOI `10.1111/apa.12890` |
| Design and population | Follow-up of a randomised prenatal exercise trial: 188 intervention-group children and 148 controls were assessed at 18 months using Bayley-III cognitive, language and motor scales and the Ages and Stages Questionnaire. |
| Finding and material limitation | Primary neurodevelopmental outcomes did not differ significantly. A slightly lower motor composite and lower fine-motor score among intervention-group boys appeared in subgroup analyses and warrant cautious retention rather than dismissal. The intervention was general moderate exercise, not isolated resistance training. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Reassurance that the studied prenatal exercise programme did not produce an overall adverse neurodevelopmental signal at 18 months. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove cognitive benefit, erase the subgroup signal, establish resistance-specific effects or guarantee an individual child's development. |
| Related authority and review | Offspring evidence boundary under TRN-OVR-PREG. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for replicated motor findings, stronger child follow-up or a resistance-specific claim. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0043 — Prenatal exercise and neurodevelopment at seven years

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ellingsen MS, et al. *Neurodevelopmental outcome in 7-year-old children is not affected by exercise during pregnancy: follow up of a multicentre randomised controlled trial.* BJOG. 2020;127:508–517. |
| Locator | [PMID 31749301](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31749301/); DOI `10.1111/1471-0528.16024` |
| Design and population | Seven-year follow-up of an 855-participant multicentre prenatal-exercise RCT. Neurodevelopment was assessed in 279 children using the parent-reported Five-To-Fifteen questionnaire covering motor, executive, perception, memory, language, social and emotional-behavioural domains. |
| Finding and material limitation | No group differences were found in continuous scores or high-score proportions. Differential and substantial follow-up attrition, parent report and a moderate mixed-exercise intervention limit precision and resistance-specific transfer. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Reassurance that regular moderate prenatal exercise did not show an adverse neurodevelopmental signal at school age in this follow-up. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish enhanced cognition, prove equivalence with adequate power for every outcome or isolate strength training. |
| Related authority and review | Offspring evidence boundary under TRN-OVR-PREG. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for stronger objective follow-up, resistance-specific data or broader public claims. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0044 — Prenatal exercise and newborn EEG maturation signal

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Labonté-Lemoyne E, Curnier D, Ellemberg D. *Exercise during pregnancy enhances cerebral maturation in the newborn: A randomized controlled trial.* J Clin Exp Neuropsychol. 2017;39:347–354. |
| Locator | [PMID 27622854](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27622854/); DOI `10.1080/13803395.2016.1227427` |
| Design and population | Randomised mechanistic study of 18 mother-newborn pairs using an EEG auditory mismatch response as a marker associated with cerebral maturation. |
| Finding and material limitation | Exercise-exposed newborns showed a smaller slow positive mismatch response, interpreted by the authors as a more mature neuroelectric pattern. The sample was extremely small, the outcome was a surrogate and later functional development was not established. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Emerging mechanism and research-question context for prenatal exercise and fetal-brain development. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot support claims that prenatal exercise or strength training makes babies smarter, improves later cognition or prevents neurodevelopmental conditions. |
| Related authority and review | Non-doctrinal offspring mechanism signal. Reviewed 17 August 2026; promote only after larger replicated studies connect the marker to child-important outcomes. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0045 — Prenatal exercise and offspring metabolic health at 8–10 years

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Guo J, et al. *Long-Term Effects of Exercise During Pregnancy on the Metabolic Health of Mothers with Overweight/Obesity and Their Offspring: An 8- to 10-Year Follow-Up Study.* Adv Ther. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42283957](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42283957/); DOI `10.1007/s12325-026-03649-6` |
| Design and population | Long-term follow-up of a prenatal-exercise RCT in women with overweight or obesity, including 118 mothers and 110 offspring assessed 8–10 years later. |
| Finding and material limitation | Offspring in the exercise group had a lower measured prevalence of overweight/obesity; several continuous anthropometric and metabolic differences were directionally favourable but not statistically significant. Follow-up attrition, modest size, one maternal population and a general moderate-exercise intervention limit causal and resistance-specific interpretation. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Emerging long-term signal that prenatal exercise may influence offspring metabolic health, presented alongside neutral and conflicting follow-ups. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove prenatal strength training prevents childhood obesity or improves glucose regulation in every child. |
| Related authority and review | Controlled by STU-EXE-0046 and the offspring evidence boundary under TRN-OVR-PREG. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for replication or a larger pooled long-term synthesis. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0046 — Prenatal activity and childhood obesity outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Barakat R, et al. *Physical Activity during Pregnancy and Childhood Obesity: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* J Clin Med. 2024;13:3726. |
| Locator | [PMID 38999292](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38999292/); [PMCID PMC11242316](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11242316/); DOI `10.3390/jcm13133726` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of randomised trials covering 30 studies and 16,137 pregnancies, with meta-analyses of birthweight, ponderal index, macrosomia/LGA, childhood BMI, weight and skinfold thickness. |
| Finding and material limitation | No statistically significant pooled effects were found for the examined birth or childhood adiposity outcomes, and no overall adverse effect emerged. Exercise type, adherence and follow-up varied; the synthesis did not isolate resistance training. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Anchor the current conclusion that long-term offspring adiposity benefit from prenatal exercise is unproven while the available RCT evidence is not showing an overall adverse signal. |
| Prohibited overreach | A null pooled result does not prove no subgroup effect, equivalence for every programme or safety for contraindicated pregnancy. |
| Related authority and review | Controls STU-EXE-0045 and STU-EXE-0047 for broad childhood-adiposity claims. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for a stronger updated synthesis or resistance-specific long-term evidence. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-EXE-0047 — Prenatal cycling and mixed seven-year offspring adiposity findings

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Chiavaroli V, et al. *Exercise in pregnancy: 1-year and 7-year follow-ups of mothers and offspring after a randomized controlled trial.* Sci Rep. 2018;8:12915. |
| Locator | [PMID 30150651](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30150651/); [PMCID PMC6110723](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6110723/); DOI `10.1038/s41598-018-30925-5` |
| Design and population | Follow-up of an 84-participant prenatal stationary-cycling RCT; 61 mother-child pairs were available at one year and 57 at seven years. |
| Finding and material limitation | Outcomes were similar at one year. At seven years, exercise-exposed offspring had greater total, abdominal and gynoid adiposity in this small follow-up. Attrition was substantial, the result was unexpected and the intervention was cycling rather than resistance training. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Disconfirming evidence that prevents Evolved from presenting prenatal exercise as guaranteed to improve long-term offspring body composition. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish that prenatal exercise causes childhood adiposity or transfer to resistance training, other populations or other programmes. |
| Related authority and review | Interpreted under STU-EXE-0046. Reviewed 17 August 2026; reopen for replication, participant-level synthesis or broader causal claims. Source `SRC-PUBMED-PFO-001`. |

### STU-NUT-0006 — Energy deficit, resistance training and lean mass

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Murphy C, Koehler K. *Energy deficiency impairs resistance training gains in lean mass but not strength: a meta-analysis and meta-regression.* Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 34623696](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34623696/); DOI `10.1111/sms.14075` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis and meta-regression of resistance-training interventions with and without energy deficit. |
| Finding | Energy deficit attenuated lean-mass gain while strength gain remained broadly comparable; the model estimated that a deficit around 500 kcal/day prevented average lean-mass gain. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | When muscle gain or preservation is important, avoid unnecessarily aggressive or prolonged energy restriction and monitor response. |
| Prohibited overreach | The model estimate is not a universal personal threshold, and the study does not show that an energy deficit prevents fat loss or strength improvement. |

### STU-NUT-0007 — Protein supplementation with resistance training in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Vieira AF, et al. *Effects of Protein Supplementation Associated with Resistance Training on Body Composition and Muscle Strength in Older Adults: A Systematic Review of Systematic Reviews with Meta-analyses.* Sports Med. 2022;52:2511–2522. |
| Locator | [PMID 35689750](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35689750/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-022-01704-0` |
| Design and population | Overview of five moderate-quality systematic reviews of older adults undertaking resistance training. |
| Finding | Supplementation produced some additional lean or muscle-mass benefit, without a clear additional strength benefit; certainty ranged from moderate to very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Protein adequacy matters; a supplement can be a practical tool when food intake, appetite or convenience leaves a gap. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not require supplements for every older member or show that more protein always produces more strength. |

### STU-NUT-0008 — Creatine with resistance training in older women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dos Santos EEP, et al. *Efficacy of Creatine Supplementation Combined with Resistance Training on Muscle Strength and Muscle Mass in Older Females: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Nutrients. 2021;13:3757. |
| Locator | [PMID 34836013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34836013/); [PMCID PMC8619193](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8619193/); DOI `10.3390/nu13113757` |
| Design and population | Ten randomised trials involving 211 older women. |
| Finding | The pooled analysis favoured creatine for upper-body strength but not overall lower-body strength or muscle mass; longer-duration subgroups appeared more favourable. The evidence base was small and conflicts of interest require attention. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Creatine may be discussed as an optional adjunct to resistance training after contraindication, medication and clinical-context screening. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish guaranteed muscle, cognition or hormone effects, make creatine necessary, or authorise supplement advice outside scope. |

### STU-NUT-0009 — Protein timing and type network meta-analysis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhou H, et al. *Effects of Protein Supplementation Timing and Types of Protein Supplements on Muscle Mass, Strength, and Physical Performance in Adults: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis.* Int J Sport Nutr Exerc Metab. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38039960](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38039960/); DOI `10.1123/ijsnem.2023-0118` |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of 116 trials involving 4,711 adults. |
| Finding | Different timing and protein types ranked differently for lean mass and strength, but much of the comparison was indirect and physical-performance benefit was not established. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Timing may be optimised after total intake, food quality, tolerance and adherence are adequate. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove a mandatory anabolic window, a universal bedtime dose or superiority of supplements to adequate food intake. |

### STU-NUT-0010 — Protein supplementation and recovery from muscle damage

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Pearson AG, et al. *The effect of dietary protein supplementation on recovery from exercise-induced muscle damage: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Eur J Clin Nutr. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 36513777](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36513777/); [PMCID PMC10393778](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10393778/); DOI `10.1038/s41430-022-01250-y` |
| Design and population | Twenty-nine studies contributing 40 trials with divergent exercise, dose and outcome methods. |
| Finding | Protein sometimes preserved strength or influenced creatine kinase, but did not clearly reduce soreness. |

| Approved use | Protein adequacy supports recovery, but soreness and readiness require wider load, sleep and symptom context. |
