### STU-NUT-0011 — Nutrition and exercise near retirement age

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Eglseer D, et al. *Nutrition and Exercise Interventions to Improve Body Composition for Persons with Overweight or Obesity Near Retirement Age: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* Adv Nutr. 2023;14:516–538. |
| Locator | [PMID 37028708](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37028708/); [PMCID PMC10201832](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10201832/); DOI `10.1016/j.advnut.2023.04.001` |
| Design and population | Ninety-two studies; 66 trials and 4,957 participants aged 55–70 entered the network analysis. |
| Finding | Energy restriction combined with resistance or mixed exercise and higher protein generally ranked best across body-composition outcomes; restriction alone tended to reduce muscle mass. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Fat-loss work near retirement age should protect muscle through resistance training, adequate protein and a tolerable energy strategy. |
| Prohibited overreach | Network rankings do not create a universal diet, calorie deficit or protein target and do not authorise obesity treatment outside coaching scope. |

### STU-SLP-0001 — Acute sleep loss and physical performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Craven J, et al. *Effects of Acute Sleep Loss on Physical Performance: A Systematic and Meta-Analytical Review.* Sports Med. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 35708888](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35708888/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 69 publications and 227 performance outcomes; participants were predominantly male. |
| Finding | Acute sleep loss impaired average performance, but heterogeneity was extreme and effects varied by deprivation protocol, task and time of testing. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Poor sleep is a meaningful readiness input and may justify reducing complexity, volume or intensity using the member's symptoms and performance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not create an automatic class-cancellation rule, a precise forecast for women or proof of failed recovery from one short night. |

### STU-SLP-0002 — Sleep interventions and athletic performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cunha LA, et al. *The Impact of Sleep Interventions on Athletic Performance: A Systematic Review.* Sports Med Open. 2023;9:58. |
| Locator | [PMID 37462808](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37462808/); [PMCID PMC10354314](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10354314/); DOI `10.1186/s40798-023-00599-z` |
| Design and population | Twenty-five athlete intervention studies. |
| Finding | Sleep extension and naps were the most consistently promising; mindfulness and light interventions remained preliminary. Null sleep-hygiene findings came from few studies. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | When sleep opportunity is genuinely short, extending night sleep or using a planned nap may support performance and recovery. |
| Prohibited overreach | Athlete evidence does not prove the same effect in every member or make a nap a substitute for treating a sleep disorder. |

### STU-SLP-0003 — Exercise for sleep and insomnia outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Xie Y, et al. *Effects of Exercise on Sleep Quality and Insomnia in Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* Front Psychiatry. 2021;12:664499. |
| Locator | [PMID 34163383](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34163383/); [PMCID PMC8215288](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8215288/); DOI `10.3389/fpsyt.2021.664499` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 22 randomised trials across heterogeneous adult populations. |
| Finding | Exercise improved several subjective sleep measures, while objective sleep changes were fewer and less consistent. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Regular exercise may support perceived sleep quality as one part of a wider sleep strategy. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish exercise as stand-alone insomnia treatment or prove improvements in every objective sleep stage. |

### STU-SLP-0004 — Consumer wearables compared with polysomnography

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Agostinho M, et al. *Are Wearable Sleep-Tracking Devices Reliable Alternatives to Polysomnography? A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Behav Sleep Med. 2026:1–13. |
| Locator | [PMID 42175611](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42175611/); DOI `10.1080/15402002.2026.2673893` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of 16 validation studies, ten entering meta-analysis. |
| Finding | Devices tended to overestimate total sleep time and efficiency and underestimate wake after sleep onset, with material device and population variability. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Wearables may support longitudinal conversation about patterns when interpreted alongside symptoms and context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Consumer scores and sleep stages are not diagnostic, cannot identify insomnia or sleep apnoea and must not determine training without member context. |

### GUI-SLP-0001 — Behavioural treatment of chronic insomnia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Edinger JD, et al. *Behavioral and psychological treatments for chronic insomnia disorder in adults: an American Academy of Sleep Medicine clinical practice guideline.* J Clin Sleep Med. 2021;17:255–262. |
| Locator | [PMID 33164742](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33164742/); [PMCID PMC7853203](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7853203/); DOI `10.5664/jcsm.8986` |
| Design and population | Evidence-based clinical practice guideline for adults with chronic insomnia. |
| Finding | Multicomponent CBT-I received a strong recommendation; several components received conditional recommendations. Sleep hygiene alone was not recommended as the sole treatment. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Persistent insomnia symptoms require qualified assessment and evidence-based care; coaches can reinforce routines without presenting sleep hygiene as treatment. |
| Prohibited overreach | Trainers do not deliver CBT-I, sleep restriction therapy or diagnose insomnia. |

### STU-SLP-0005 — Sleep restriction, appetite and diet outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Soltanieh S, et al. *Sleep duration and dietary intake in adults: a systematic review of randomized controlled trials.* Clin Nutr ESPEN. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 34620371](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34620371/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnesp.2021.07.029` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of 50 randomised trials. |
| Finding | Sleep restriction generally increased energy intake, hunger or adverse food-choice outcomes, while leptin, ghrelin and cortisol findings were inconsistent. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Sleep opportunity can affect appetite-management difficulty and should be explored without blame when nutrition adherence changes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not support a simple cortisol mechanism, deterministic weight-gain claim or hormone diagnosis. |

### STU-BHV-0001 — Motivational interviewing and physical activity

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhu Y, et al. *Effectiveness of behavioural interventions with motivational interviewing on physical activity outcomes in adults: systematic review and meta-analysis.* BMJ. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38986547](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38986547/); [PMCID PMC11234249](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11234249/); DOI `10.1136/bmj-2023-078713` |
| Design and population | Ninety-seven randomised trials involving 27,811 adults. |
| Finding | Interventions containing motivational interviewing improved activity against lower-intensity controls, but not against comparators of similar intensity; effects waned and were absent beyond one year. Certainty was low or very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Collaborative, autonomy-supportive conversation is preferable to coercion, while ongoing environment and programme support still matter. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove that motivational interviewing itself caused the effect or provide a durable one-conversation adherence solution. |

### STU-BHV-0002 — Digital self-monitoring in weight-loss interventions

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Berry R, et al. *Systematic review and meta-analysis of the effectiveness of digital self-monitoring interventions for weight loss.* Obes Rev. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 34192411](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34192411/); DOI `10.1111/obr.13306` |
| Design and population | Twelve randomised trials of multicomponent digital self-monitoring interventions. |
| Finding | Interventions improved average weight, activity and energy-intake outcomes, with heterogeneity and stronger results in some tailored approaches. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Voluntary, purpose-limited tracking can support feedback and pattern recognition for some people. |
| Prohibited overreach | Tracking is not universally necessary or benign, and multicomponent trials do not isolate the effect of logging itself. |

### STU-BHV-0003 — Time required to form health habits

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Singh B, et al. *Time to Form a Habit: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Health Behaviour Habit Formation and Its Determinants.* Healthcare. 2024;12:2488. |
| Locator | [PMID 39685110](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39685110/); [PMCID PMC11641623](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11641623/); DOI `10.3390/healthcare12232488` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of heterogeneous prospective and intervention evidence, much of it self-reported. |
| Finding | Median estimates were around 59–66 days, means around 106–154 days and individual estimates ranged from 4 to 335 days. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Habit formation is variable; coaching should use repetition, stable cues and review rather than promise a fixed completion date. |
| Prohibited overreach | Rejects universal 21-day or 66-day habit claims and does not mean repetition alone guarantees automaticity. |

### STU-BHV-0004 — Interventions designed to build physical-activity habits

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ma H, et al. *Habit formation interventions for physical activity: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37700303](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37700303/); [PMCID PMC10498635](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10498635/); DOI `10.1186/s12966-023-01493-3` |
| Design and population | Ten randomised trials involving 2,349 participants. |
| Finding | Interventions produced a small-to-moderate improvement in habit strength, with substantial heterogeneity and weaker effects in longer interventions. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Action planning, stable cues, self-monitoring and repetition can be combined and tested around the individual. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not identify one required behaviour-change recipe or show that a habit score guarantees attendance or health outcomes. |

### STU-BHV-0005 — Self-compassion in weight management

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Brenton-Peters J, et al. *Self-compassion interventions and weight management: a systematic review.* J Psychosom Res. 2021;151:110617. |
| Locator | [PMID 34560404](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34560404/); DOI `10.1016/j.jpsychores.2021.110617` |
| Design and population | Twenty studies with heterogeneous designs and generally weak methods. |
| Finding | Self-compassion and several eating outcomes often improved; physical activity, weight and maintenance findings were less consistent. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Non-shaming language and recovery from lapses are psychologically safer coaching principles. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove superior weight loss or make self-compassion a treatment for eating disorders or mental illness. |

### STU-BHV-0006 — Self-compassion added to a commercial weight programme

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Brenton-Peters J, et al. *A randomized controlled trial of a self-compassion intervention for weight management.* Br J Health Psychol. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37544883](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37544883/); DOI `10.1111/bjhp.12686` |
| Design and population | Randomised trial of 249 adults comparing a commercial weight-management programme with the same programme plus a self-compassion intervention. |
| Finding | Adding self-compassion did not improve weight-management outcomes, although small changes in self-kindness and self-judgment occurred. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Preserve compassionate coaching because it supports dignity and lapse recovery, not because it has been shown to produce extra weight loss. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not market self-compassion as a causal weight-loss accelerator or use a null weight result to justify shame-based coaching. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — creatine and cognition

### STU-COG-0001 — Creatine and cognition in ageing

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Marshall S, et al. *Creatine and Cognition in Aging: A Systematic Review of Evidence in Older Adults.* Nutr Rev. 2026;84:333–344. |
| Locator | [PMID 40971619](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40971619/); [PMCID PMC12793482](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12793482/); DOI `10.1093/nutrit/nuaf135` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of six studies involving 1,542 adults aged at least 55; only two were double-blind supplementation studies and most participants were healthy. |
| Finding | Five studies reported a positive association, especially for memory or attention, but only one study was rated good quality and three were poor. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Creatine is a plausible optional cognition-support research candidate in generally healthy older adults. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish prevention or treatment of cognitive impairment, dementia or Alzheimer disease, or a cognitive dose for Evolved members. |

### STU-COG-0002 — Creatine and cognitive domains in adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Xu C, et al. *The effects of creatine supplementation on cognitive function in adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Front Nutr. 2024;11:1424972. |
| Locator | [PMID 39070254](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39070254/); [PMCID PMC11275561](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11275561/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2024.1424972`; wording correction [PMID 40034739](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40034739/) |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 16 RCTs involving 492 adults aged 20.8–76.4, including healthy and clinical populations. |
| Finding | Small benefits appeared for memory, attention scores and processing-speed scores; overall cognition and executive function did not improve. Certainty was moderate for memory and low for most other outcomes. The 2025 correction changed outcome wording, not the numerical results or conclusions. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Creatine may support selected cognitive tasks, with memory the more defensible domain. |
| Prohibited overreach | Mixed populations and small trials do not support a general brain-performance, dementia-prevention or universal female benefit claim. |

### STU-COG-0003 — Creatine and memory in healthy people

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Prokopidis K, et al. *Effects of creatine supplementation on memory in healthy individuals: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* Nutr Rev. 2023;81:416–427. |
| Locator | [PMID 35984306](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35984306/); [PMCID PMC9999677](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9999677/); DOI `10.1093/nutrit/nuac064` |
| Design and population | Ten RCTs in the systematic review; eight entered meta-analysis. |
| Finding | Memory improved modestly overall. The larger older-adult subgroup estimate came from adults aged 66–76 and had high heterogeneity. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Memory support is a plausible creatine benefit worth discussing as uncertain and secondary to established physical-performance evidence. |
| Prohibited overreach | The older subgroup does not demonstrate reduced dementia incidence or an assured effect for an individual. |

### STU-COG-0004 — Creatine pilot in Alzheimer disease

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Smith AN, et al. *Creatine monohydrate pilot in Alzheimer's: Feasibility, brain creatine, and cognition.* Alzheimers Dement (N Y). 2025;11:e70101. |
| Locator | [PMID 40395689](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40395689/); [PMCID PMC12089086](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12089086/); DOI `10.1002/trc2.70101` |
| Design and population | Eight-week, single-arm feasibility pilot; 20 people with Alzheimer disease received 20 g/day creatine. |
| Finding | Brain total creatine increased 11% and several cognitive test scores improved, but there was no control group and the study was not an efficacy trial. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Demonstrates feasibility and a live clinical research pathway for creatine in Alzheimer disease. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cannot show treatment efficacy, reverse causality, exclude practice effects or justify clinical dosing by Evolved. |

### STU-COG-0005 — Creatine and strength training in older women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Alves CR, et al. *Creatine supplementation associated or not with strength training upon emotional and cognitive measures in older women: a randomized double-blind study.* PLoS One. 2013;8:e76301. |
| Locator | [PMID 24098469](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24098469/); [PMCID PMC3789718](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3789718/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0076301` |
| Design and population | Twenty-four-week, four-group, double-blind RCT; 56 older women assigned creatine/placebo with or without strength training. |
| Finding | Neither creatine nor strength training altered the measured cognitive outcomes. Strength training improved strength and depressive-symptom scores irrespective of creatine. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Direct disconfirming evidence against promising a cognitive benefit from creatine or resistance training in every older woman. |
| Prohibited overreach | One small RCT does not prove that cognitive effects never occur or establish treatment for depression. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — resistance training and cognition

### STU-COG-0006 — Multi-session resistance exercise and cognition

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Molloy G, et al. *The effects of multi-session resistance exercise on cognitive function in healthy adults: A systematic research review and meta-analysis.* Ageing Res Rev. 2026;116:103277. |
| Locator | [PMID 42508500](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42508500/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2026.103277` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of repeated resistance-exercise interventions in healthy adults. |
| Finding | Global cognition, executive function, working memory and processing speed improved; episodic-memory retrieval did not show a consistent effect. Studies and measures were heterogeneous. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Repeated resistance training can support selected cognitive domains as part of healthy ageing. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish uniform cognitive improvement, dementia prevention or superiority to other exercise. |

### STU-COG-0007 — Resistance exercise and cognition in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wu J, Huang C. *A systematic review and meta-analysis of the effects of resistance exercise on cognitive function in older adults.* Front Psychiatry. 2025;16:1708244. |
| Locator | [PMID 41503279](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41503279/); [PMCID PMC12772445](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12772445/); DOI `10.3389/fpsyt.2025.1708244` |
| Design and population | Seventeen RCTs involving 739 adults aged at least 60. |
| Finding | Overall cognition, working memory, verbal learning and spatial memory improved; processing speed, executive function and attention did not clearly improve. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance exercise may selectively support memory and cognition in older adults. |
| Prohibited overreach | Domain-specific effects do not prove prevention of neurodegenerative disease or a precise cognitive training dose. |

### STU-COG-0008 — Resistance training across cognitive status

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Coelho-Junior H, et al. *Resistance training improves cognitive function in older adults with different cognitive status: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Aging Ment Health. 2022;26:213–224. |
| Locator | [PMID 33325273](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33325273/); DOI `10.1080/13607863.2020.1857691` |
| Design and population | Eighteen intervention studies in cognitively healthy and cognitively impaired adults aged at least 60. |
| Finding | Overall cognition improved in both groups; short-term memory improved only in the cognitively healthy group. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training is compatible with cognitive-health support across differing baseline cognitive status when appropriately supervised. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not authorise standard coaches to assess cognitive status or treat cognitive impairment. |

### STU-COG-0009 — Exercise in MCI or dementia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Huang X, et al. *Comparative efficacy of various exercise interventions on cognitive function in patients with mild cognitive impairment or dementia: A systematic review and network meta-analysis.* J Sport Health Sci. 2022;11:212–223. |
| Locator | [PMID 34004389](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34004389/); [PMCID PMC9068743](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9068743/); DOI `10.1016/j.jshs.2021.05.003` |
| Design and population | Network meta-analysis of 71 trials and 5,606 people with MCI or dementia. |
| Finding | Exercise generally maintained or improved cognition. Resistance exercise ranked highly overall; multicomponent exercise ranked highly in MCI subgroups. Rankings include indirect comparisons. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise belongs within clinician-aware supportive care for cognitive impairment, with resistance and multicomponent approaches both plausible. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove resistance training is the best treatment, prevent conversion to dementia or permit unsupervised clinical programming. |

### STU-COG-0010 — Exercise variables and cognition in healthy populations

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhang M, et al. *Effects of exercise interventions on cognitive functions in healthy populations: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Ageing Res Rev. 2023;92:102116. |
| Locator | [PMID 37924980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37924980/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2023.102116` |
| Design and population | Fifty-four RCTs across healthy children, adults and older adults; exercise FITT-VP variables and five cognitive domains were examined. |
| Finding | Exercise benefited cognition overall; aerobic exercise showed the largest global-cognition signal and resistance exercise the largest executive-function signal. Older adults showed the greatest average benefits. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Cognitive-health programming should be multimodal rather than presented as a uniquely strength-mediated effect. |
| Prohibited overreach | Subgroup rankings do not establish causal superiority or an exact Evolved cognitive prescription. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — neurobiology and BDNF

### STU-COG-0011 — Exercise and resting BDNF in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Gholami F, et al. *Exercise training alters resting brain-derived neurotrophic factor concentration in older adults: A systematic review with meta-analysis of randomized-controlled trials.* Exp Gerontol. 2025;200:112658. |
| Locator | [PMID 39674562](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39674562/); DOI `10.1016/j.exger.2024.112658` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 35 RCTs of exercise lasting at least four weeks in older adults. |
| Finding | Resting peripheral BDNF increased overall and in aerobic, resistance and combined-exercise subgroups. Measures were blood biomarkers, not direct brain outcomes. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise can influence a peripheral biomarker involved in neuroplasticity. |
| Prohibited overreach | A blood BDNF increase does not establish brain BDNF, cognitive improvement, dementia prevention or a causal mechanism for an individual. |

### STU-COG-0012 — Resistance exercise and neuroprotective factors

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Rodríguez-Gutiérrez E, et al. *Effects of Resistance Exercise on Neuroprotective Factors in Middle and Late Life: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Aging Dis. 2023;14:1171–1183. |
| Locator | [PMID 37163437](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37163437/); [PMCID PMC10389831](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10389831/); DOI `10.14336/AD.2022.1207` |
| Design and population | Thirty RCTs involving 1,247 adults aged 45–92; 27 entered meta-analysis. |
| Finding | Resistance exercise increased circulating IGF-1, but the pooled BDNF effect was not statistically clear; VEGF evidence was insufficient. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Directly constrains the claim that resistance training reliably raises BDNF; neurobiological effects are marker-specific. |
| Prohibited overreach | IGF-1 change does not prove neuroprotection, cognitive improvement or dementia prevention. |

### STU-COG-0013 — Resistance-training dose and neuroplasticity biomarkers

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Qipo O, et al. *Dose-response relationship of resistance training and the effects on circulating biomarkers of inflammation or neuroplasticity in older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Ageing Res Rev. 2026;113:102917. |
| Locator | [PMID 41086999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41086999/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2025.102917` |
| Design and population | Thirty-six RCTs of resistance training in older adults; pooled neuroplasticity and inflammatory biomarkers with exploratory dose-response analyses. |
| Finding | Pooled neuroplasticity biomarkers and anti-inflammatory markers increased and pro-inflammatory markers decreased. Larger effects appeared in some higher-dose categories. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training can influence circulating biological pathways relevant to healthy ageing. |
| Prohibited overreach | Composite biomarkers, subgroup meta-regressions and circulating measures cannot define a dementia-prevention dose or justify more training regardless of recovery. |

### STU-COG-0014 — Exercise and BDNF in neurodegenerative disorders

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ruiz-González D, et al. *Effects of physical exercise on plasma brain-derived neurotrophic factor in neurodegenerative disorders: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* Neurosci Biobehav Rev. 2021;128:394–405. |
| Locator | [PMID 34087277](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34087277/); DOI `10.1016/j.neubiorev.2021.05.025` |
| Design and population | Eighteen RCTs across multiple sclerosis, Parkinson disease, MCI and Alzheimer disease. |
| Finding | Plasma BDNF increased overall and in some disease groups; the four-study MCI estimate was a nonsignificant trend. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise–BDNF effects differ by clinical population and remain an active research area. |
| Prohibited overreach | Pooled neurodegenerative diagnoses cannot be transferred to dementia prevention or standard gym coaching. |

### STU-COG-0015 — Multicomponent exercise in MCI or dementia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang X, et al. *The neurocognitive and BDNF changes of multicomponent exercise for community-dwelling older adults with mild cognitive impairment or dementia: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Aging (Albany NY). 2020;12:4907–4917. |
| Locator | [PMID 32191630](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32191630/); [PMCID PMC7138588](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7138588/); DOI `10.18632/aging.102918` |
| Design and population | Eight studies of community group exercise in older adults with MCI or dementia. |
| Finding | Cognition, attention and executive function improved; memory did not clearly improve and BDNF reporting was too limited for a firm conclusion. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Multicomponent group exercise may support selected cognitive outcomes within appropriately governed care. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish a BDNF mechanism or make ordinary group fitness a dementia treatment. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — strength and longevity

### STU-LNG-0001 — Grip-strength ranges and mortality

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | López-Bueno R, et al. *Thresholds of handgrip strength for all-cause, cancer, and cardiovascular mortality: A systematic review with dose-response meta-analysis.* Ageing Res Rev. 2022;82:101778. |
| Locator | [PMID 36332759](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36332759/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2022.101778` |
| Design and population | Forty-eight prospective studies involving 3,135,473 adults; 49.6% women, ages 35–85. |
| Finding | Higher grip strength was associated with lower mortality. Apparent risk-reduction ranges differed by outcome: about 26–50 kg for all-cause, 24–40 kg for cardiovascular and 16–33 kg for cancer mortality. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Grip strength is a meaningful population-level health and longevity marker. |
| Prohibited overreach | These overlapping pooled ranges are not sex-, age- or body-size-specific Evolved targets, intervention thresholds or guarantees of longer life. |

### STU-LNG-0002 — Grip strength and health outcomes umbrella review

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Soysal P, et al. *Handgrip strength and health outcomes: Umbrella review of systematic reviews with meta-analyses of observational studies.* J Sport Health Sci. 2021;10:290–295. |
| Locator | [PMID 32565244](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32565244/); [PMCID PMC8167328](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8167328/); DOI `10.1016/j.jshs.2020.06.009` |
| Design and population | Umbrella review of eight systematic reviews and 11 health outcomes. |
| Finding | Higher grip had highly suggestive associations with lower all-cause mortality, cardiovascular mortality and disability; no outcome reached the review's most convincing evidence class. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Grip strength is a scalable sentinel measure of general functional health. |
| Prohibited overreach | A prognostic marker is not necessarily the causal mechanism or a sufficient assessment by itself. |

### STU-LNG-0003 — Upper- and lower-body strength and mortality

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | García-Hermoso A, et al. *Muscular Strength as a Predictor of All-Cause Mortality in an Apparently Healthy Population: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Data From Approximately 2 Million Men and Women.* Arch Phys Med Rehabil. 2018;99:2100–2113.e5. |
| Locator | [PMID 29425700](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29425700/); DOI `10.1016/j.apmr.2018.01.008` |
| Design and population | Thirty-eight prospective studies, 1,907,580 adults and 63,087 deaths. |
| Finding | Higher grip was associated with lower mortality, slightly more strongly in women; higher knee-extension strength was also associated with lower mortality. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Both upper- and lower-body strength are useful healthy-ageing markers; grip is not the entire thesis. |
| Prohibited overreach | Observational associations do not quantify how much mortality risk is changed by gaining a given amount of strength. |

### STU-LNG-0004 — Prognostic value of grip in adults over 60

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Rijk JM, et al. *Prognostic value of handgrip strength in people aged 60 years and older: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Geriatr Gerontol Int. 2016;16:5–20. |
| Locator | [PMID 26016893](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26016893/); DOI `10.1111/ggi.12508` |
| Design and population | Thirty-four longitudinal observational reports covering cognition, mobility, functional status, hospitalisation or mortality. |
| Finding | Higher baseline grip generally predicted better later cognition, mobility and function and lower mortality. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Grip can contribute to longitudinal functional-reserve monitoring across several domains. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not diagnose cognitive decline or establish a single pass/fail strength standard. |

### STU-LNG-0005 — Outcome-linked grip and gait cut-points

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cawthon PM, et al. *Putative Cut-Points in Sarcopenia Components and Incident Adverse Health Outcomes: An SDOC Analysis.* J Am Geriatr Soc. 2020;68:1429–1437. |
| Locator | [PMID 32633824](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32633824/); [PMCID PMC7508260](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7508260/); DOI `10.1111/jgs.16517` |
| Design and population | Sex-stratified pooled analysis of eight cohorts: 13,421 men and 4,828 women. |
| Finding | The combination of weakness—absolute grip below 35.5 kg in men or 20 kg in women—and gait below 0.8 m/s consistently identified higher adverse-outcome risk. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Clinical-research context showing why strength and slowness should be interpreted together. |
| Prohibited overreach | These sarcopenia-screening cut-points are not Evolved performance goals, longevity guarantees or suitable for younger healthy women without validation. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — functional reserve

### STU-LNG-0006 — Objective physical capability and mortality

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cooper R, et al. *Objectively measured physical capability levels and mortality: systematic review and meta-analysis.* BMJ. 2010;341:c4467. |
| Locator | [PMID 20829298](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20829298/); [PMCID PMC2938886](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2938886/); DOI `10.1136/bmj.c4467` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of community cohorts measuring grip, walking speed, chair rise and standing balance. |
| Finding | Worse performance in all four capacities was associated with higher mortality; the slowest gait quartile had a particularly large association. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Functional reserve is multidimensional and should include strength, locomotion, rising ability and balance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Relative cohort quartiles do not create universal coaching thresholds or show that improving one test causes longer life. |

### STU-LNG-0007 — Gait speed and mortality

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Veronese N, et al. *Association Between Gait Speed With Mortality, Cardiovascular Disease and Cancer: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Prospective Cohort Studies.* J Am Med Dir Assoc. 2018;19:981–988.e7. |
| Locator | [PMID 30056008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30056008/); DOI `10.1016/j.jamda.2018.06.007` |
| Design and population | Forty-eight cohorts and 101,945 participants; mean age 72.2, 55% women. |
| Finding | Each 0.1 m/s slower gait speed was associated with 12% higher mortality and 8% higher cardiovascular-disease risk; heterogeneity was high and no cancer association appeared. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Comfortable gait speed is a useful functional-health signal in older adults. |
| Prohibited overreach | The association is not a personal risk calculator, training target or cancer-screening measure. |

### STU-LNG-0008 — Muscle measures and later independence

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang DXM, et al. *Muscle mass, strength, and physical performance predicting activities of daily living: a meta-analysis.* J Cachexia Sarcopenia Muscle. 2020;11:3–25. |
| Locator | [PMID 31788969](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31788969/); [PMCID PMC7015244](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7015244/); DOI `10.1002/jcsm.12502` |
| Design and population | Eighty-three longitudinal studies and 108,428 adults aged at least 65; 45 studies entered meta-analysis. |
| Finding | Low mass, grip, gait, SPPB, balance, timed-up-and-go and chair-stand performance predicted later ADL or IADL dependence; physical-performance measures often had larger associations. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Independence is best protected and monitored through a battery spanning mass, strength and performance rather than appearance alone. |
| Prohibited overreach | Prognostic associations do not validate an unsupervised public “longevity test” or one universal cut-off. |

### GUI-LNG-0001 — Sarcopenia Definition and Outcomes Consortium position

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bhasin S, et al. *Sarcopenia Definition: The Position Statements of the Sarcopenia Definition and Outcomes Consortium.* J Am Geriatr Soc. 2020;68:1410–1418. |
| Locator | [PMID 32150289](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32150289/); [PMCID PMC12132920](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12132920/); DOI `10.1111/jgs.16372` |
| Design and population | Expert position statements informed by multiple cohort, trial and nationally representative datasets. |
| Finding | Weak grip and slow gait independently predicted meaningful adverse outcomes; DXA lean mass did not independently predict incident outcomes in the analysed community cohorts. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Strength and performance should carry more practical weight than DXA lean mass alone in functional-risk reasoning. |
| Prohibited overreach | This is a clinical sarcopenia framework, not authority for trainers to diagnose sarcopenia or deprioritise muscle preservation. |

### STU-LNG-0009 — No single functional test predicts falls reliably

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Beck Jepsen D, et al. *Predicting falls in older adults: an umbrella review of instruments assessing gait, balance, and functional mobility.* BMC Geriatr. 2022;22:615. |
| Locator | [PMID 35879666](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35879666/); [PMCID PMC9310405](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9310405/); DOI `10.1186/s12877-022-03271-5`; author-name correction [PMID 36199031](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36199031/) |
| Design and population | Umbrella review of 31 reviews; most reviews were low quality. |
| Finding | No single gait, balance or mobility assessment predicted falls with high certainty; gait speed had moderate supporting evidence within comprehensive assessment. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Functional testing should be a profile, not a single dramatic pass/fail challenge. |
| Prohibited overreach | A farmer's carry, hang, chair stand, gait or balance test alone cannot establish longevity, falls risk or clinical clearance. |

## Muscle–brain–longevity expansion — muscle mass, quality and power

### STU-MSQ-0001 — Muscle mass and later functional decline

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Visser M, et al. *The association between muscle mass and change in physical functioning in older adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis of prospective studies.* Eur Geriatr Med. 2025;16:1731–1750. |
| Locator | [PMID 40407980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40407980/); [PMCID PMC12528211](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12528211/); DOI `10.1007/s41999-025-01230-y` |
| Design and population | Seventy-two prospective studies; mean age at least 65 and average follow-up 46 months. |
| Finding | Low muscle mass was associated with 23% higher odds of functional decline, but heterogeneity was high and results depended on measurement and adjustment for body size. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Muscle mass contributes to functional reserve, especially when interpreted relative to body size and alongside performance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Absolute muscle size, appearance, a scan number or one body-composition method does not determine future independence. |

### STU-MSQ-0002 — Muscle wasting and mortality

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhou HH, et al. *Association of muscle wasting with mortality risk among adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis of prospective studies.* J Cachexia Sarcopenia Muscle. 2023;14:1596–1612. |
| Locator | [PMID 37209044](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37209044/); [PMCID PMC10401550](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10401550/); DOI `10.1002/jcsm.13263` |
| Design and population | Forty-nine prospective studies, 878,349 adults and 61,055 deaths. |
| Finding | The lowest muscle-mass category was associated with higher all-cause and several cause-specific mortality risks, but heterogeneity was extreme and measurement definitions varied. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Avoiding clinically meaningful muscle loss is part of healthy-ageing risk reduction. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that bodybuilding-level size extends life, establish a minimum visible-muscle standard or prove that adding mass changes mortality by the reported association. |

### STU-MSQ-0003 — Clinical usefulness of strength versus mass measures

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Lunt E, et al. *The clinical usefulness of muscle mass and strength measures in older people: a systematic review.* Age Ageing. 2021;50:88–95. |
| Locator | [PMID 32706848](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32706848/); [PMCID PMC7793605](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7793605/); DOI `10.1093/ageing/afaa123` |
| Design and population | Eighty-three studies in older clinical populations examining strength, size, mass and motor outcomes. |
| Finding | Grip had consistent associations with mobility, balance and ADL independence. Gold-standard longitudinal evidence for muscle mass, volume or thickness was sparse. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Prioritise repeatable strength and performance measures over muscle appearance or low-precision mass estimates when assessing function. |
| Prohibited overreach | Grip is not sufficient alone and clinical associations do not make it a diagnosis or universal programme target. |

### STU-MSQ-0004 — Muscle density, size and cognition after hip fracture

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ge Y, et al. *Muscle density, but not size, is independently associated with cognitive health in older adults with hip fractures.* JBMR Plus. 2024;8:ziae047. |
| Locator | [PMID 38665314](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38665314/); [PMCID PMC11044827](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11044827/); DOI `10.1093/jbmrpl/ziae047` |
| Design and population | Post hoc cross-sectional analysis of 679 adults over 65 with hip fracture; CT gluteal measures and MMSE classification. |
| Finding | Gluteus medius/minimus density, but not cross-sectional size, remained associated with cognitive impairment after adjustment. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Supports investigating muscle quality separately from size in older clinical populations. |
| Prohibited overreach | Cross-sectional hip-fracture data cannot show that training glutes improves cognition, quantify “23% lower odds” for healthy women or establish causality. |

### STU-MSQ-0005 — Leg power and ten-year cognitive ageing in female twins

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Steves CJ, et al. *Kicking Back Cognitive Ageing: Leg Power Predicts Cognitive Ageing after Ten Years in Older Female Twins.* Gerontology. 2016;62:138–149. |
| Locator | [PMID 26551663](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26551663/); [PMCID PMC4789972](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4789972/); DOI `10.1159/000441029` |
| Design and population | Longitudinal observational twin study; 324 healthy female twins, mean baseline age 55, followed for ten years; a smaller identical-twin subset received brain imaging. |
| Finding | Higher baseline leg power predicted less cognitive decline after extensive adjustment; discordant-twin and imaging analyses supported the signal but did not establish intervention causality. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Lower-body power is a promising functional-reserve marker linked with later cognitive health in women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that power training makes the brain 3.3 years younger, prevents dementia or that a particular leg-power target causes cognitive protection. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — general principles

### STU-PAI-0001 — Physical activity and exercise across chronic-pain conditions

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Geneen LJ, et al. *Physical activity and exercise for chronic pain in adults: an overview of Cochrane Reviews.* Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2017. |
| Locator | [PMID 28436583](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28436583/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD011279.pub3` |
| Design and population | Overview of 21 Cochrane reviews covering multiple chronic-pain diagnoses; evidence quality was generally low. |
| Finding | Exercise produced small-to-moderate, inconsistent improvements in pain and physical function. Reported serious harm was uncommon, but adverse-event reporting was incomplete. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Appropriately selected physical activity is usually part of persistent-pain care rather than something automatically stopped because pain exists. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not mean every activity, dose or pain response is safe; does not cover new trauma, undiagnosed pathology or clinical clearance. |

### STU-PAI-0002 — Pain-neuroscience education is an adjunct

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cuenca-Martínez F, et al. *Pain neuroscience education in patients with chronic musculoskeletal pain: an umbrella review.* Front Neurosci. 2023;17:1272068. |
| Locator | [PMID 38075271](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38075271/); DOI `10.3389/fnins.2023.1272068` |
| Design and population | Umbrella review of 16 reviews; high overlap, heterogeneous protocols and mostly critically low review quality. |
| Finding | Pain education alone was not consistently effective. It appeared more useful when added to exercise-based or multimodal care, especially for psychosocial outcomes. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Use validating, non-threatening pain explanations to support graded activity, not as a stand-alone cure. |
| Prohibited overreach | A trainer must not diagnose sensitisation, tell a member pain is “only in the brain,” or replace clinical care with education. |

### STU-PAI-0003 — Exercise and pain-processing measures

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Chen KK, et al. *Exercise-induced changes in central sensitization outcomes in individuals with chronic musculoskeletal pain: A systematic review with meta-analysis.* Eur J Pain. 2024;28:1431–1449. |
| Locator | [PMID 38662515](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38662515/); DOI `10.1002/ejp.2277` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of exercise studies measuring temporal summation or conditioned pain modulation in chronic musculoskeletal pain. |
| Finding | Exercise may alter selected laboratory pain-processing outcomes, but results varied by test, modality and condition and certainty was limited. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Mechanistic context for why persistent pain and exercise response are not explained by local tissue alone. |
| Prohibited overreach | Laboratory pain-processing measures do not diagnose an individual, prove tissue safety or prescribe a gym load. |

### STU-PAI-0004 — Exercise, brain measures and chronic pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Palmer KL, et al. *Exercise training augments brain function and reduces pain perception in adults with chronic pain: A systematic review of intervention studies.* Neurobiol Pain. 2023;13:100129. |
| Locator | [PMID 37206154](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37206154/); DOI `10.1016/j.ynpai.2023.100129` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of heterogeneous chronic-pain exercise interventions with neurobiological or pain outcomes. |
| Finding | The small evidence base showed favourable pain and selected brain-function signals, with substantial variation in conditions, exercise and measurement. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Supports a biopsychosocial explanation of persistent pain and graded exercise. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show that exercise reverses a specific brain abnormality, cures chronic pain or makes painful loading automatically safe. |

### STU-PAI-0005 — Resistance training in osteoarthritis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Lim J, et al. *The Effects of Resistance Training on Pain, Strength, and Function in Osteoarthritis: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* J Pers Med. 2024;14:1130. |
| Locator | [PMID 39728043](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39728043/); DOI `10.3390/jpm14121130` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of resistance-training trials in osteoarthritis. |
| Finding | Resistance training improved pain, strength and function on average, with variation across joints, programmes and study quality. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Progressive resistance training can be a useful component of osteoarthritis management under an appropriate clinical and coaching plan. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not diagnose osteoarthritis, regenerate cartilage, eliminate pain or establish one universal programme. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — low-back pain

### GUI-LBP-0001 — Physical-therapy low-back-pain guideline

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | George SZ, et al. *Interventions for the Management of Acute and Chronic Low Back Pain: Revision 2021.* J Orthop Sports Phys Ther. 2021;51:CPG1–CPG60. |
| Locator | [PMID 34719942](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34719942/); [PMCID PMC10508241](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10508241/); DOI `10.2519/jospt.2021.0304` |
| Design and population | Evidence-based clinical practice guideline for physical therapists. |
| Finding | Exercise recommendations differ by acute or chronic presentation and classification; active, individualised care is central, with clinical examination and referral boundaries. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Clinical authority for active low-back-pain care and the need to individualise rather than prescribe one corrective exercise. |
| Prohibited overreach | This clinician guideline does not authorise a fitness trainer to assess pathology, provide rehabilitation or clear neurological or traumatic presentations. |

### STU-LBP-0001 — Exercise therapy for chronic nonspecific low-back pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Hayden JA, et al. *Exercise therapy for chronic low back pain.* Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2021;9:CD009790. |
| Locator | [PMID 34580864](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34580864/); [PMCID PMC8477273](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8477273/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD009790.pub2` |
| Design and population | Cochrane review of 249 randomised trials; mean age 44 and about 59% women; most studies had risk-of-bias concerns. |
| Finding | Exercise improved pain by a clinically important amount versus no treatment/usual care/placebo at earliest follow-up; average functional improvement was smaller. No single exercise type solved all cases. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Persistent nonspecific low-back pain usually benefits from an individualised exercise programme and continued activity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show a plank deficit causes pain, that one posterior-chain exercise is curative, or that exercise is suitable before serious pathology is excluded. |

### STU-LBP-0002 — Posterior-chain resistance training

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Tataryn N, et al. *Posterior-Chain Resistance Training Compared to General Exercise and Walking Programmes for the Treatment of Chronic Low Back Pain in the General Population: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Sports Med Open. 2021;7:17. |
| Locator | [PMID 33683497](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33683497/); [PMCID PMC7940464](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7940464/); DOI `10.1186/s40798-021-00306-w` |
| Design and population | Eight studies, 408 participants with chronic low-back pain; heterogeneous comparators and programmes. |
| Finding | Posterior-chain programmes improved pain, disability and strength and sometimes outperformed general exercise, especially in longer programmes; evidence was limited and heterogeneous. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Loaded posterior-chain work can be one useful option within a graded low-back-pain programme. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove weak hamstrings or a weak core cause most back pain, or that deadlifts are required or appropriate for every presentation. |

### STU-LBP-0003 — External load versus unloaded exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ranzani M, et al. *Is resistance training with external loads superior to unloaded exercise in the management of chronic low back pain? A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Arch Physiother. 2025;15:297–313. |
| Locator | [PMID 41356326](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41356326/); [PMCID PMC12680015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12680015/); DOI `10.33393/aop.2025.3533` |
| Design and population | Thirteen randomised trials, 778 adults with chronic nonspecific low-back pain. |
| Finding | External load was safe and feasible and produced a small longer-term pain advantage, but disability and psychosocial outcomes were broadly comparable with unloaded exercise. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | A member need not avoid external load solely because persistent nonspecific back pain exists; load is progressed by tolerance, function and clinical context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Heavy loading is not inherently superior, and this does not clear a new, traumatic, radiating or neurologically changing presentation. |

### STU-LBP-0004 — Exercise therapy versus manual therapy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | González-Gómez L, et al. *Exercise Therapy Versus Manual Therapy for the Management of Pain Intensity, Disability, and Physical Function in People With Chronic Low Back Pain: A Systematic Review With Meta-Analysis and Meta-Regression.* Eur J Pain. 2025;29:e70090. |
| Locator | [PMID 40747709](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40747709/); DOI `10.1002/ejp.70090` |
| Design and population | Six randomised trials, 743 adults aged 18–54; certainty was very low. |
| Finding | Exercise had a small long-term disability advantage, but overall differences from manual therapy were inconclusive. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Active exercise belongs in care; passive treatment is not a prerequisite for every return to training. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not make trainers manual therapists, establish exercise-only care for all cases or justify dismissing clinician-led treatment. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — knee pain

### STU-KNE-0001 — Resistance training and knee osteoarthritis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Xu T, et al. *The effect of resistance training on patients with knee osteoarthritis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Res Sports Med. 2025;33:78–91. |
| Locator | [PMID 38980284](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38980284/); DOI `10.1080/15438627.2024.2377087` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of resistance-training studies in knee osteoarthritis. |
| Finding | Resistance training improved WOMAC pain, stiffness and function and visual-analogue pain, but not six-minute walk distance. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Knee osteoarthritis is compatible with tailored progressive resistance training that targets strength and function. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish one movement, eliminate symptoms, reverse joint structure or allow diagnosis by a trainer. |

### STU-KNE-0002 — Resistance-exercise dose in knee and hip osteoarthritis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Marriott KA, et al. *Are the Effects of Resistance Exercise on Pain and Function in Knee and Hip Osteoarthritis Dependent on Exercise Volume, Duration, and Adherence?* Arthritis Care Res. 2024;76:821–830. |
| Locator | [PMID 38317328](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38317328/); DOI `10.1002/acr.25313` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of resistance exercise in knee and hip osteoarthritis. |
| Finding | Programmes lasting three to six months improved pain and function; no rigid volume or adherence threshold explained the benefits. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Consistent, tolerable resistance exercise matters more than claiming a single perfect dose. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not imply dose is irrelevant for an individual or that missed sessions never affect results. |

### STU-KNE-0003 — Dose-response claims in knee osteoarthritis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang H, et al. *Dose-Response Relationships of Resistance Training in Adults With Knee Osteoarthritis: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* J Geriatr Phys Ther. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37774094](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37774094/); DOI `10.1519/JPT.0000000000000394` |
| Design and population | Forty-six randomised trials, 4,289 adults with knee osteoarthritis, mean age at least 50. |
| Finding | Resistance training improved symptoms, function and lower-limb strength. A four-to-eight-week subgroup ranked favourably, but subgroup and meta-regression results do not establish a universal optimum. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Clinically governed resistance training can improve knee-OA outcomes within weeks and continue to build capacity over time. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise a four-to-eight-week cure or use the subgroup as a fixed programme length. |

### STU-KNE-0004 — Hip and knee exercise for patellofemoral pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Manojlović D, et al. *Trunk, Hip and Knee Exercise Programs for Pain Relief, Functional Performance and Muscle Strength in Patellofemoral Pain: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* J Pain Res. 2021;14:1431–1449. |
| Locator | [PMID 34079359](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34079359/); [PMCID PMC8165213](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8165213/); DOI `10.2147/JPR.S301448` |
| Design and population | Twenty-one studies, thirteen meta-analysed, involving people with patellofemoral pain. |
| Finding | Hip-plus-knee and hip-focused programmes improved pain and function; heterogeneity was high and between-programme superiority was uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Anterior knee pain often benefits from a broader progressive hip-and-knee programme rather than avoiding knee loading. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show one ATG split squat prevents or resolves all knee pain or that a visible movement pattern identifies the cause. |

### STU-KNE-0005 — Movement feedback in patellofemoral pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Nunes GS, et al. *Effectiveness of Interventions Aimed at Changing Movement Patterns in People With Patellofemoral Pain: A Systematic Review With Network Meta-analysis.* J Orthop Sports Phys Ther. 2023;53:748–760. |
| Locator | [PMID 37707784](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37707784/); DOI `10.2519/jospt.2023.11956` |
| Design and population | Thirty-seven randomised trials, 1,235 participants with patellofemoral pain. |
| Finding | Hip/knee exercise plus internal feedback changed selected task kinematics; whether changing those movements mediates pain recovery was not established. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Feedback may help a symptomatic person explore a more tolerable movement strategy. |
| Prohibited overreach | Knee valgus, tracking or technique appearance must not be presented as a diagnosis or proof of damage. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — shoulder and neck pain

### STU-SHN-0001 — Exercise prescription for rotator-cuff-related shoulder pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Lafrance S, et al. *The Efficacy of Exercise Therapy for Rotator Cuff-Related Shoulder Pain According to the FITT Principle: A Systematic Review With Meta-analyses.* J Orthop Sports Phys Ther. 2024;54:499–512. |
| Locator | [PMID 38848304](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38848304/); DOI `10.2519/jospt.2024.12453` |
| Design and population | Twenty-two randomised trials, 1,281 adults with rotator-cuff-related shoulder pain. |
| Finding | Motor-control programmes were probably slightly better than nonspecific exercise for disability, not short-term pain; optimal intensity, frequency and duration remained uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Tailored progressive shoulder exercise is appropriate in clinically identified rotator-cuff-related pain, without claiming one ideal dose. |
| Prohibited overreach | A trainer cannot diagnose a rotator-cuff condition or infer a tear from pain, weakness or range alone. |

### STU-SHN-0002 — Manual therapy added to shoulder exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Paraskevopoulos E, et al. *Effectiveness of Combined Program of Manual Therapy and Exercise Vs Exercise Only in Patients With Rotator Cuff-related Shoulder Pain: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* Sports Health. 2023;15:727–735. |
| Locator | [PMID 36517977](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36517977/); [PMCID PMC10467476](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10467476/); DOI `10.1177/19417381221136104` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of randomised trials comparing exercise with or without manual therapy. |
| Finding | Adding manual therapy did not improve pain with movement or shoulder function over exercise alone. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Active exercise can be a primary component of shoulder-pain care; passive treatment is not automatically required. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not show manual therapy never helps an individual or permit a trainer to provide clinical shoulder rehabilitation. |

### STU-SHN-0003 — Exercise types in chronic neck pain

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Rasmussen-Barr E, et al. *Summarizing the effects of different exercise types in chronic neck pain: a systematic review and meta-analysis of systematic reviews.* BMC Musculoskelet Disord. 2023;24:806. |
| Locator | [PMID 37828488](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37828488/); DOI `10.1186/s12891-023-06930-9` |
| Design and population | Umbrella review of 25 reviews, reporting 17,321 participants before overlap adjustment. |
| Finding | Several exercise types improved short-term pain or disability versus no exercise; no clearly optimal type emerged. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Chronic nonspecific neck pain is compatible with individually acceptable resistance, motor-control, Pilates, yoga or related exercise. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not identify the cause of neck pain or support a single posture-correction narrative. |

### STU-SHN-0004 — Resistance, motor control and mind-body neck exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mueller J, et al. *Resistance, Motor Control, and Mindfulness-Based Exercises Are Effective for Treating Chronic Nonspecific Neck Pain.* J Orthop Sports Phys Ther. 2023;53:420–459. |
| Locator | [PMID 37339388](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37339388/); DOI `10.2519/jospt.2023.11820` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 68 trials; heterogeneity was very high and certainty ranged from very low to moderate. |
| Finding | Multiple exercise approaches reduced pain and disability versus true controls; dose-response findings were programme-specific. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Select neck-pain exercise by goals, preference, symptom response and clinician guidance rather than brand allegiance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Pooled rankings do not establish one best exercise or justify aggressive neck loading. |

### STU-SHN-0005 — Network comparison of chronic neck-pain exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | de Zoete RM, et al. *Comparative effectiveness of physical exercise interventions for chronic non-specific neck pain: a systematic review with network meta-analysis of 40 randomised controlled trials.* Br J Sports Med. 2020. |
| Locator | [PMID 33139256](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33139256/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2020-102664` |
| Design and population | Forty randomised trials; 3,151 participants in the pain network and 2,336 in the disability network; evidence was very low quality. |
| Finding | Strengthening, motor-control and mind-body exercise all showed benefit versus no treatment, without a clearly superior category. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Multiple active routes can help chronic nonspecific neck pain. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not validate a universal posture, mobility or muscle-imbalance explanation. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — tendinopathy

### STU-TEN-0001 — Resistance-exercise dose across tendinopathies

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Pavlova AV, et al. *Effect of resistance exercise dose components for tendinopathy management: a systematic review with meta-analysis.* Br J Sports Med. 2023;57:1327–1334. |
| Locator | [PMID 37169370](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37169370/); [PMCID PMC10579176](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10579176/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2022-105754` |
| Design and population | One hundred ten studies and 3,953 participants across rotator-cuff, Achilles, elbow, patellar and gluteal tendinopathy. |
| Finding | Higher external resistance and less-than-daily frequency were associated with better average outcomes; exercise-dose reporting was poor and volume findings inconsistent. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Tendon rehabilitation generally uses progressive resistance with recovery, not endless light daily loading. |
| Prohibited overreach | Meta-regression cannot prescribe an individual dose or authorise gym-led rehabilitation without diagnosis and clinician coordination. |

### STU-TEN-0002 — Time course of Achilles loading programmes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Murphy M, et al. *Rate of Improvement of Pain and Function in Mid-Portion Achilles Tendinopathy with Loading Protocols.* Sports Med. 2018;48:1875–1891. |
| Locator | [PMID 29766442](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29766442/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-018-0932-2` |
| Design and population | Longitudinal meta-analysis of 31 cohorts from 24 studies; follow-up two weeks to six months. |
| Finding | Pain and function improved as early as two weeks and appeared to peak around twelve weeks on average. Early clinical change preceded expected structural adaptation. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Tendon symptoms and function can improve during a progressive loading programme even before imaging structure changes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not prove a two- or twelve-week cure, permit diagnosis, or show pain is irrelevant to load decisions. |

### STU-TEN-0003 — Achilles loading-programme comparisons

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Head J, et al. *The efficacy of loading programmes for improving patient-reported outcomes in chronic midportion Achilles tendinopathy: A systematic review.* Musculoskeletal Care. 2019;17:283–299. |
| Locator | [PMID 31763774](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31763774/); DOI `10.1002/msc.1428` |
| Design and population | Seven controlled studies comparing loading programmes for chronic midportion Achilles tendinopathy. |
| Finding | Evidence did not show isolated eccentric loading was consistently superior; do-as-tolerated modification may improve short-term function. Certainty was low or very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Loading can be adapted to the person; one branded eccentric protocol is not mandatory. |
| Prohibited overreach | “As tolerated” is not permission to ignore worsening symptoms, next-day response or clinical advice. |

### STU-TEN-0004 — Higher versus lower dose in rotator-cuff tendinopathy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Malliaras P, et al. *The Efficacy of Higher Versus Lower Dose Exercise in Rotator Cuff Tendinopathy.* Arch Phys Med Rehabil. 2020;101:1822–1834. |
| Locator | [PMID 32682937](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32682937/); DOI `10.1016/j.apmr.2020.06.013` |
| Design and population | Three randomised trials, 283 participants; certainty low to very low. |
| Finding | Higher load or volume sometimes improved function, but findings conflicted and adverse-event certainty was inadequate. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Shoulder-tendon loading should be progressed, but the evidence does not justify assuming more is always better. |
| Prohibited overreach | No universal load, volume or pain allowance can be copied into group coaching. |

### STU-TEN-0005 — Isometric exercise is not uniquely curative

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Clifford C, et al. *Effectiveness of isometric exercise in the management of tendinopathy: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials.* BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med. 2020;6:e000760. |
| Locator | [PMID 32818059](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32818059/); [PMCID PMC7406028](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7406028/); DOI `10.1136/bmjsem-2020-000760` |
| Design and population | Ten studies across patellar, rotator-cuff, elbow, Achilles and gluteal tendinopathy; most were poor quality. |
| Finding | Isometrics were not superior to isotonic exercise and immediate analgesic response varied. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Isometrics are one tolerable loading option, not a diagnostic test or required pain-relief technique. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise immediate analgesia or use lack of pain relief to infer tendon damage. |

## Training with injury and persistent pain expansion — return to training

### CON-RTT-0001 — Training load and injury risk in athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Soligard T, et al. *How much is too much? (Part 1) International Olympic Committee consensus statement on load in sport and risk of injury.* Br J Sports Med. 2016;50:1030–1041. |
| Locator | [PMID 27535989](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27535989/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2016-096581` |
| Design and population | International expert consensus focused on competitive athletes and heterogeneous load evidence. |
| Finding | Poorly managed load can contribute to injury risk, but internal and external load, wellbeing and context should be considered together. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Avoid abrupt, unexplained increases and monitor the member's response when rebuilding capacity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Does not establish a universal acute:chronic ratio, pain threshold or injury-prediction formula for general-population coaching. |

### CON-RTT-0002 — Return is a continuum, not one clearance test

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ardern CL, et al. *2016 Consensus statement on return to sport from the First World Congress in Sports Physical Therapy, Bern.* Br J Sports Med. 2016;50:853–864. |
| Locator | [PMID 27226389](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27226389/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2016-096278` |
| Design and population | Consensus of 17 expert sports clinicians. |
| Finding | Return to participation, return to sport and return to performance form a continuum; decisions are multifactorial risk management and ideally collaborative. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Evolved should distinguish “can participate with modification” from “fully restored to previous loading.” |
| Prohibited overreach | Fitness staff do not provide medical clearance or independently own return after diagnosed injury or surgery. |

### CON-RTT-0003 — Hamstring return-to-play criteria

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | van der Horst N, et al. *Return to play after hamstring injuries in football: a worldwide Delphi procedure.* Br J Sports Med. 2017;51:1583–1591. |
| Locator | [PMID 28360143](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28360143/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2016-097206` |
| Design and population | Four-round Delphi involving 58 football injury experts. |
| Finding | Consensus included medical clearance, pain response, strength/flexibility, field performance and psychological readiness in shared decisions. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Demonstrates that return after a diagnosed muscle injury uses multiple criteria and clinician ownership. |
| Prohibited overreach | Elite-football criteria do not become a gym checklist or allow a trainer to clear a hamstring injury. |

### STU-RTT-0001 — Evidence gap in ankle-sprain return criteria

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Tassignon B, et al. *Criteria-Based Return to Sport Decision-Making Following Lateral Ankle Sprain Injury: a Systematic Review and Narrative Synthesis.* Sports Med. 2019;49:601–619. |
| Locator | [PMID 30747379](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30747379/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-019-01071-3` |
| Design and population | Systematic review seeking prospective criteria-based return decisions after lateral ankle sprain. |
| Finding | No eligible prospective studies had applied an evidence-based criteria process; the authors proposed candidate domains rather than validated clearance rules. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Be explicit about uncertainty; no single balance, hop, pain or time test clears return. |
| Prohibited overreach | Candidate tests are not validated Evolved pass/fail thresholds. |

### CON-RTT-0004 — Hamstring rehabilitation and return consensus

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Paton BM, et al. *London International Consensus and Delphi study on hamstring injuries part 3: rehabilitation, running and return to sport.* Br J Sports Med. 2023;57:278–291. |
| Locator | [PMID 36650032](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36650032/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2021-105384` |
| Design and population | International Delphi consensus on athletic hamstring injury. |
| Finding | Experts supported individualisation, progressive loading, running exposure and criteria-based return; several precise strength and flexibility benchmarks did not reach consensus. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Return plans must progress the actual demands of training and acknowledge uncertainty rather than rely on elapsed time alone. |
| Prohibited overreach | This is sports-clinical consensus, not a standard-coaching rehabilitation protocol. |

## Four-extension expansion — cardiometabolic, bone and aerobic health

### STU-CMB-0001 — Effect of aerobic training versus resistance training for improving cardiorespiratory fitness and body composition in middle-aged to older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | An J, et al. *Effect of aerobic training versus resistance training for improving cardiorespiratory fitness and body composition in middle-aged to older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* Arch Gerontol Geriatr. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38878596](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38878596/); DOI `10.1016/j.archger.2024.105530` |
| Design and population | Journal Article; Meta-Analysis; Systematic Review; Research Support, Non-U.S. Gov't — Middle-aged and older adults, with female- and menopause-specific syntheses prioritised. |
| Finding and material limitation | Exercise improves aerobic capacity and related fitness outcomes; mode comparisons and certainty vary, and both aerobic and resistance work contribute different benefits. This record is approved only for its indexed population, intervention and outcomes; pooled findings do not establish individual response. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support maintaining aerobic capacity alongside strength in a complete women’s-health programme. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim one interval format is superior for every woman or that VO2max change proves longevity. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Immediate review on retraction/correction, newer major synthesis or guideline, materially broader claim, or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-CMB-001`. |

### STU-NWM-0011 — Calcium plus vitamin D supplementation and risk of fractures: an updated meta-analysis from the National Osteoporosis Foundation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Weaver CM, et al. *Calcium plus vitamin D supplementation and risk of fractures: an updated meta-analysis from the National Osteoporosis Foundation.* Osteoporos Int. 2016. |
| Locator | [PMID 26510847](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26510847/); DOI `10.1007/s00198-015-3386-5`; numerical/method correction [PMID 27401092](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27401092/) |
| Design and population | Journal Article; Meta-Analysis; Research Support, Non-U.S. Gov't — Premenopausal and postmenopausal women; BMD, fracture, strength and mobility outcomes. |
| Finding and material limitation | Benefits depend on baseline status, age and risk; routine supplementation in healthy low-risk women is not automatically beneficial, and food, loading and clinical treatment remain distinct. The correction changed pooled total-fracture RR from 0.85 to 0.86 and hip-fracture RR from 0.70 to 0.61 and clarified methods; the authors reported no conclusion change. This record is approved only for its indexed population, intervention and outcomes; pooled findings do not establish individual response. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support food-first adequacy, loading and risk-based clinical assessment rather than automatic dosing. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not prescribe a universal supplement dose or claim supplementation prevents fractures in every woman. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Immediate review on retraction/correction, newer major synthesis or guideline, materially broader claim, or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NWM-001`. |
