### STU-MSC-0023 — Comparison of dietary and physical activity behaviors in women with and without polycystic ovary syndrome: a systematic review and meta-analysis of 39 471 women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kazemi M, et al. *Comparison of dietary and physical activity behaviors in women with and without polycystic ovary syndrome: a systematic review and meta-analysis of 39 471 women.* Hum Reprod Update. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 35639552](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35639552/); DOI `10.1093/humupd/dmac023`; confidence-interval correction [PMID 36326693](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36326693/) |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 54 observational studies involving 39,471 women aged 18–50: 8,736 with PCOS and 30,735 controls; pregnancy and menopause were excluded. |
| Finding and material limitation | Women with PCOS had lower pooled total physical activity and selected dietary differences, while energy and macronutrient intakes were similar; diet-quality reporting was heterogeneous and largely narrative. The correction repaired three typographical confidence intervals, including alcohol intake and total physical activity. Observational group differences do not establish causation or a unique PCOS prescription. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support ordinary healthy-eating and physical-activity foundations while recognising that PCOS-specific precision lifestyle evidence remains limited and care may require clinical coordination. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer that women with PCOS uniformly eat or move poorly, diagnose PCOS from behaviour, or prescribe a unique PCOS diet from these observational comparisons. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Immediate review on retraction/correction, newer major synthesis or guideline, materially broader claim, or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-MSC-001`. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Furukawa Y, et al. *Components and Delivery Formats of Cognitive Behavioral Therapy for Chronic Insomnia in Adults: A Systematic Review and Component Network Meta-Analysis.* JAMA Psychiatry. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38231522](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38231522/); DOI `10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2023.5060` |
| Design and population | Systematic Review; Journal Article; Network Meta-Analysis — Adults with insomnia, with female life-stage and sex-difference relevance; CBT-I, sleep restriction and sleep-hygiene evidence. |
| Finding and material limitation | CBT-I and structured sleep-restriction components have stronger support than sleep-hygiene education alone; insomnia is a clinical disorder, not simply poor habits. Approved only for the indexed population, intervention and outcomes; pooled or observational findings do not establish individual response. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Provide general sleep support while referring persistent insomnia for evidence-based clinical treatment. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not present sleep hygiene as CBT-I or deliver clinical sleep restriction outside competence. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Immediate review on retraction/correction, newer major synthesis or guideline, materially broader claim, or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-SCF-001`. |

### STU-FTM-0006 — Evidence for an Upper Threshold for Resistance Training Volume in Trained Women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Barbalho M, et al. *Evidence for an Upper Threshold for Resistance Training Volume in Trained Women.* Med Sci Sports Exerc. 2019. |
| Locator | [PMID 30779716](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30779716/); DOI `10.1249/MSS.0000000000001818`; [retraction PMID 33986236](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33986236/); retraction DOI `10.1249/MSS.0000000000002672` |
| Design and population | Retracted randomized controlled publication concerning trained women and resistance-training volume. Retained only as a citation-safety record. |
| Finding and material limitation | The original result is not available for evidence use. The journal first issued an expression of concern and then retracted the publication. The Crossref Retraction Watch record classifies the reasons as concerns about data, unreliable data, third-party investigation and upgrade of the prior notice. The public record does not establish that this exact paper was retracted for a formally proven fabrication finding. |
| State | `RETRACTED-EXCLUDED` |
| Approved use | Provenance, retraction surveillance and teaching why a retracted publication must not support a coaching or public claim. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not use its results, numerical thresholds, group comparisons or conclusions as evidence. Do not describe the exact paper as proven fabricated unless a competent authority establishes that finding. |
| Review | Authority withdrawn 18 August 2026 after live surveillance detected the retraction relationship. Corpus trace found no direct downstream citation outside this bank record and surveillance control documents. Removing this paper does not alter DOC-TRN-0002 or the Evolved 6–8-working-set session standard, which remain grounded in separate synthesis/trial evidence and explicit Evolved delivery judgement. Source `SRC-PUBMED-FTM-001`. |

| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

| Prohibited overreach | Do not guarantee fracture prevention or use BMD findings as unscreened clearance for impact. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Immediate review on retraction/correction, newer major synthesis or guideline, materially broader claim, or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-FTM-001`. |

## Focused expansion — ageing, anabolic resistance and protein

### STU-ARP-0001 — Current meta-analysis of age-related anabolic resistance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kristiansen JB, et al. *Age-related anabolic resistance and post-absorptive muscle protein synthesis: integrative evidence from a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Front Physiol. 2026;17:1740284. |
| Locator | [PMID 42326998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42326998/); DOI `10.3389/fphys.2026.1740284` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 46 stable-isotope studies involving 1,280 healthy adults: 700 older adults aged at least 60 and 580 younger adults aged 18–35. |
| Finding and material limitation | Older adults had modestly lower postabsorptive MPS and lower postprandial MPS, although postprandial heterogeneity was high. Most post-exercise and combined feeding-plus-exercise comparisons did not show a significant age difference. Sex-specific analyses and protocol standardisation remain limited. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Support the bounded position that ageing is associated with modest basal and nutrient-related anabolic resistance while meaningful exercise responsiveness is substantially retained. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say older muscle cannot build, that resistance exercise stops working, or that this mechanism proves one personal protein target or supplement requirement. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`; immediate review on major contrary synthesis, correction, retraction or personal-target use. |

### STU-ARP-0002 — Exercise and nutrition may overcome much apparent anabolic resistance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Shad BJ, et al. *Does the muscle protein synthetic response to exercise and amino acid-based nutrition diminish with advancing age? A systematic review.* Am J Physiol Endocrinol Metab. 2016;311:E803–E817. |
| Locator | [PMID 27555299](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27555299/); DOI `10.1152/ajpendo.00213.2016` |
| Design and population | Systematic review of direct younger-versus-older acute MPS comparisons after exercise, amino-acid nutrition or their combination. |
| Finding and material limitation | Evidence of age-related resistance appeared in 8/17 exercise-only and 8/21 nutrition-only arms, but only 2/10 combined exercise-plus-nutrition arms. The review uses acute mechanistic outcomes and cannot establish long-term muscle, strength or functional effects. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Explain why adequate protein and progressive resistance training work together and why ageing does not remove anabolic potential. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not convert an unspecified threshold into a universal meal dose or imply protein alone compensates for insufficient training. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0003 — Greater relative meal dose in older versus younger men

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Moore DR, et al. *Protein ingestion to stimulate myofibrillar protein synthesis requires greater relative protein intakes in healthy older versus younger men.* J Gerontol A Biol Sci Med Sci. 2015;70:57–62. |
| Locator | [PMID 25056502](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25056502/); DOI `10.1093/gerona/glu103` |
| Design and population | Retrospective pooled laboratory dose-response analysis of healthy men around age 22 or 71 after 0–40 g single boluses of high-quality protein. |
| Finding and material limitation | Older men were less responsive to lower relative doses; estimated MPS plateaus were about 0.40 versus 0.24 g/kg body mass. The older estimate was imprecise, the between-group body-mass breakpoint comparison was borderline, and the study measured acute MPS in men rather than long-term outcomes in women. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support the mechanism that an older adult may need a larger relative protein exposure than a younger adult to maximise an acute post-meal MPS response. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not prescribe 0.40 g/kg per meal universally, transfer the estimate directly to women, or promise muscle gain from an acute MPS breakpoint. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0004 — PROT-AGE older-adult protein position

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bauer J, et al. *Evidence-based recommendations for optimal dietary protein intake in older people: a position paper from the PROT-AGE Study Group.* J Am Med Dir Assoc. 2013;14:542–559. |
| Locator | [PMID 23867520](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23867520/); DOI `10.1016/j.jamda.2013.05.021` |
| Design and population | Multiorganisation expert position for adults older than 65, including healthy, active and clinically unwell groups. |
| Finding and material limitation | Recommended 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day for older adults and at least 1.2 g/kg/day for active older adults, with higher clinical ranges in illness and an explicit severe-kidney-disease exception. It is an older expert position, not an individual assessment or female-specific outcome trial. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Support the population-level conclusion that protein needs and the importance of adequacy can increase in older age, particularly with activity or illness. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not turn population ranges into an automatic Evolved personal prescription or use clinical ranges without a doctor or APD. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`; reopen for updated geriatric guideline. |

### STU-ARP-0005 — ESPEN protein-and-exercise recommendations for ageing

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Deutz NE, et al. *Protein intake and exercise for optimal muscle function with aging: recommendations from the ESPEN Expert Group.* Clin Nutr. 2014;33:929–936. |
| Locator | [PMID 24814383](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24814383/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnu.2014.04.007` |
| Design and population | ESPEN expert recommendations for healthy older people and older adults with malnutrition or illness risk. |
| Finding and material limitation | Recommended at least 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day for healthy older adults alongside ongoing resistance and aerobic exercise; clinical ranges were higher. The paper is professional guidance rather than a trial and clinical recommendations are not standard-coaching targets. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Anchor the combined message that adequate protein and exercise are complementary protections for ageing muscle. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim protein replaces training or use illness/malnutrition targets without clinical ownership. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`; reopen for updated ESPEN guidance. |

### STU-ARP-0006 — Protein supplementation and muscle mass in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Hettiarachchi J, et al. *The effect of dose, frequency, and timing of protein supplementation on muscle mass in older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Ageing Res Rev. 2024;99:102325. |
| Locator | [PMID 38830547](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38830547/); DOI `10.1016/j.arr.2024.102325` |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 38 RCT reports in adults aged at least 65; community-dwelling evidence included 3,204 participants and was 62.8% female. |
| Finding and material limitation | Supplementation produced a small positive muscle-mass effect in community-dwelling older adults. Effects did not significantly differ by the studied dose, frequency or timing subgroups; hospital and institutional data were limited. Supplementation trials do not establish an optimal whole-food diet. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Support the position that closing a protein gap can modestly support muscle mass in older adults without making timing or supplement dose the main story. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim supplements are necessary, that more is always better, or that the small mass effect guarantees strength or function. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0007 — Whey supplementation outcomes in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Al-Rawhani AH, et al. *Effectiveness of whey protein supplementation on muscle strength and physical performance of older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized clinical trials.* Clin Nutr. 2024;43:2412–2426. |
| Locator | [PMID 39303495](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39303495/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnu.2024.08.033` |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 30 studies involving 2,105 adults aged 60 or older. |
| Finding and material limitation | Whey did not significantly improve most pooled strength, physical-performance or body-composition outcomes. With resistance exercise it produced a small lower-body-strength benefit; appendicular-mass evidence came from two heterogeneous studies. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Explain that a convenient protein supplement may help close an intake gap and may modestly complement resistance training, but food adequacy and training remain primary. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not market whey as necessary, broadly function-enhancing or independently protective against sarcopenia. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0008 — Acute protein synthesis in postmenopausal women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | McKenna CF, et al. *Postabsorptive and postprandial myofibrillar protein synthesis rates at rest and after resistance exercise in women with postmenopause.* J Appl Physiol. 2024;136:1388–1399. |
| Locator | [PMID 38385186](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38385186/); DOI `10.1152/japplphysiol.00886.2023` |
| Design and population | Acute stable-isotope and biopsy study in 16 postmenopausal women, with a young-women postabsorptive comparison. |
| Finding and material limitation | Exercise and whey responses varied across the four-hour window, without greater cumulative four-hour MPS from exercise in either condition. The small acute study indicates postmenopause can alter MPS regulation but does not yield a simple higher-protein prescription or long-term outcome. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Establish that female-specific anabolic physiology is being studied and that postmenopausal responses cannot be inferred perfectly from young men. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim this study proves postmenopausal women need a particular dose, have uniformly impaired muscle building, or require supplements. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0009 — Resistance training, adequate protein and leucine in pre-frail older women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Jacob KJ, et al. *Resistance training, but not leucine, increased basal muscle protein synthesis and reversed frailty in older women consuming optimized protein intake.* Geroscience. 2026;48:3971–3982. |
| Locator | [PMID 40926079](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40926079/); DOI `10.1007/s11357-025-01877-2` |
| Design and population | Twelve-week double-blind leucine-versus-alanine trial in 19 pre-frail women, mean age 77.5, all undertaking resistance training and consuming about 1.2 g/kg/day protein. |
| Finding and material limitation | Across the intervention, MPS, fibre size, lean mass, strength, function and frailty criteria improved; leucine added no benefit. Without a no-training/no-optimised-protein control, the study cannot isolate the contribution of each shared component. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support resistance training plus adequate protein as the main pairing and resist supplement-first leucine claims. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim 1.2 g/kg or resistance training alone reversed frailty, or generalise a 19-person pre-frail cohort to all women. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0010 — Whey after resistance exercise in healthy older women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mori H, Tokuda Y. *Effect of whey protein supplementation after resistance exercise on the muscle mass and physical function of healthy older women: A randomized controlled trial.* Geriatr Gerontol Int. 2018;18:1398–1404. |
| Locator | [PMID 30113122](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30113122/); DOI `10.1111/ggi.13499` |
| Design and population | Twenty-four-week three-arm RCT in 81 healthy Japanese women aged 65–80: resistance exercise plus whey, exercise only or whey only. Intake in all groups was adjusted to at least 1.2 g/kg/day. |
| Finding and material limitation | Exercise plus post-exercise whey improved skeletal-muscle-mass index more than either component alone and improved grip and gait versus whey alone. The setting, intake adjustment, twice-weekly exercise and relatively small groups limit broad prescription. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support a practical protein source after training when it helps an older woman meet overall adequacy. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim whey timing is universally superior, necessary, or sufficient without progressive training. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0011 — Higher protein did not add lean mass in a short postmenopausal training trial

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Rossato LT, et al. *Higher Protein Intake Does Not Improve Lean Mass Gain When Compared with RDA Recommendation in Postmenopausal Women Following Resistance Exercise Protocol: A Randomized Clinical Trial.* Nutrients. 2017;9:1007. |
| Locator | [PMID 28895933](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28895933/); DOI `10.3390/nu9091007` |
| Design and population | Ten-week RCT in 23 postmenopausal women comparing about 1.2 with 0.8 g/kg/day during progressive resistance training three times weekly. |
| Finding and material limitation | Both groups gained lean mass with no between-group difference. The trial was small and short, so it cannot establish equivalence or a lifetime requirement. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Preserve the boundary that higher intake does not automatically add benefit when resistance training and baseline intake are already sufficient. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not use the null result to claim older women never benefit from protein above 0.8 g/kg/day or that protein adequacy is unimportant. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

### STU-ARP-0012 — Twice-RDA protein did not improve outcomes in late postmenopausal women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Silva TR, et al. *Effects of high protein, low-glycemic index diet on lean body mass, strength, and physical performance in late postmenopausal women: a randomized controlled trial.* Menopause. 2021;28:307–317. |
| Locator | [PMID 33201025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33201025/); DOI `10.1097/GME.0000000000001692` |
| Design and population | Six-month RCT in 26 apparently healthy women aged at least 65 comparing 0.8 with 1.6 g/kg/day within low-glycaemic-index diets. |
| Finding and material limitation | Twice-RDA protein did not produce greater lean mass, grip strength or gait-speed change. The small sample, dietary co-intervention and absence of a defined progressive resistance-training comparison limit transfer. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Show why the correct position is age-sensitive protein adequacy plus training, not unlimited protein escalation. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim 0.8 g/kg is optimal for every older woman or that higher protein can never help an under-consuming, dieting, ill or highly active woman. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ARP-001`. |

## Age-related muscle-loss verification

### STU-AML-0001 — Quantitative trajectories of muscle mass and strength with age

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mitchell WK, et al. *Sarcopenia, dynapenia, and the impact of advancing age on human skeletal muscle size and strength; a quantitative review.* Front Physiol. 2012;3:260. |
| Locator | [PMID 22934016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22934016/); [PMCID PMC3429036](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3429036/); DOI `10.3389/fphys.2012.00260` |
| Design and population | Quantitative review of cross-sectional and longitudinal human studies. Methods, populations and muscle measures varied substantially. |
| Finding and material limitation | The median reported muscle-mass loss across studies was 0.37% per year in women. At age 75, longitudinal estimates were approximately 0.64–0.70% per year for muscle mass and 2.5–3% per year for strength in women; studies measuring both found strength declined two to five times faster than mass. These medians do not define an age-30 onset or a sedentary woman's fixed kilogram loss. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Ageing creates a meaningful population-level risk to both muscle and strength; strength may deteriorate much faster than measured mass, and the rate varies by age and method. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not convert the review into a guaranteed 2.5 kg-per-decade forecast, apply the age-75 rate from age 30, or treat muscle mass and strength as the same outcome. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-AML-001`. |

### STU-AML-0002 — Six-year regional lean-tissue change in women aged 40–79

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kitamura I, et al. *Six-year longitudinal changes in body composition of middle-aged and elderly Japanese: age and sex differences in appendicular skeletal muscle mass.* Geriatr Gerontol Int. 2014;14:354–361. |
| Locator | [PMID 23809775](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23809775/); DOI `10.1111/ggi.12109` |
| Design and population | Six-year DXA follow-up of 1,454 community-dwelling Japanese adults aged 40–79. |
| Finding and material limitation | Women's leg lean tissue decreased significantly in every decade group studied: approximately 2.0% in the 40s, 2.8% in the 50s, 2.4% in the 60s and 3.9% in the 70s over six years. Arm trajectories differed. DXA regional lean tissue is not identical to total skeletal muscle, and this cohort was not a sedentary-only sample. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Women can experience measurable, age-varying loss of lower-body lean tissue from midlife onward; regional and age differences matter. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim every woman loses the same percentage or 2.5 kg per decade, or transfer Japanese cohort estimates unchanged to an individual Australian woman. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-AML-001`. |

### STU-AML-0003 — Menopausal transition, physical activity and muscle maintenance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Juppi HK, et al. *Role of Menopausal Transition and Physical Activity in Loss of Lean and Muscle Mass: A Follow-Up Study in Middle-Aged Finnish Women.* J Clin Med. 2020;9:1588. |
| Locator | [PMID 32456169](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32456169/); [PMCID PMC7290663](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7290663/); DOI `10.3390/jcm9051588` |
| Design and population | Follow-up of 234 Finnish women aged 47–55 from perimenopause to early postmenopause using DXA, CT, activity measures and muscle biopsy. |
| Finding and material limitation | Multiple lean- and muscle-mass measures decreased across the transition. Menopausal status predicted the tested muscle measures, while physical activity made an additional beneficial contribution to several maintenance outcomes. This observational contribution does not isolate resistance training or prove a precise loss rate for sedentary women. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Midlife and the menopausal transition are relevant risk periods, and remaining physically active is beneficial to muscle maintenance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say menopause causes one inevitable decline rate, activity fully prevents loss, or the study validates a 2.5 kg-per-decade rule. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-AML-001`. |

### STU-AML-0004 — Nonlinear five-year body-composition change in Australian women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wong JCH, et al. *A 5-year longitudinal study of changes in body composition in women in the perimenopause and beyond.* Maturitas. 2020;132:49–56. |
| Locator | [PMID 31883663](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31883663/); DOI `10.1016/j.maturitas.2019.12.001` |
| Design and population | Five-year observational follow-up of 489 Australian women aged 40–79 using DXA body-composition measures. |
| Finding and material limitation | Total and regional lean-mass changes differed by age: total and leg lean mass increased in the youngest group, while decline emerged in different regions and later age bands. The observational design and survivor/cohort effects limit causal interpretation. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Female muscle-related trajectories are not linear or identical; age, body region, starting point and measurement method change the estimate. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not present a fixed decline beginning at 30 as established for every woman, or use lean mass as an exact synonym for skeletal muscle. |
| Review | Reviewed 16 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-AML-001`. |

## Women's athletic and cognitive frontier expansion

### Female athletic performance

### STU-FAP-0001 — Current female-athlete programme-design position

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dabbs NC, et al. *National Strength and Conditioning Association Position Statement on Strength and Conditioning of Female Athletes. Part II: Training Adaptations and Program Design.* J Strength Cond Res. 2026;40:895–908. |
| Locator | [PMID 42283375](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42283375/); DOI `10.1519/JSC.0000000000005493` |
| Design and finding | Current NSCA position statement covering neuroendocrine, neuromuscular and metabolic adaptation and programme design. Core training principles, including progressive overload, remain applicable; female opportunity is often limited by provision, resourcing and delivery rather than biology. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Programme women with the same serious progressive principles used in effective strength and conditioning, while individualising for the athlete, health status and context. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not presume that every woman needs cycle-phased programming, attribute every performance difference to ovarian hormones or treat the statement as one universal prescription. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0002 — Resistance training in elite female athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zouita A, et al. *The Effects of Resistance Training on Muscular Fitness, Muscle Morphology, and Body Composition in Elite Female Athletes: A Systematic Review.* Sports Med. 2023;53:1969–1986. |
| Locator | [PMID 37289331](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37289331/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-023-01859-4` |
| Design and finding | Systematic review of 33 studies, usually about 12 weeks. Most reported improvements in strength, power, speed or jumping, but programming varied and the optimal female-athlete dose remains unresolved. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Well-programmed resistance training supports multiple performance qualities in trained and elite women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not guarantee every outcome, infer one optimal intensity or volume, or transfer elite effect sizes directly to general members. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0003 — Plyometric performance in female team-sport athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zeng Z, et al. *Effects of plyometric training on strength, explosive performance, and agility in female team-sport athletes: a systematic review and three-level meta-analysis.* Front Physiol. 2026;17:1837615. |
| Locator | [PMID 42164067](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42164067/); DOI `10.3389/fphys.2026.1837615` |
| Design and finding | Thirty-six studies with 921 participants. Plyometric training improved jumping, sprinting, throwing and agility; strength findings were outcome-specific and evidence certainty ranged from very low to moderate. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Appropriately progressed plyometrics can develop explosive and change-of-direction qualities in female team-sport athletes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer a universal contact count, frequency or readiness threshold, or substitute plyometrics for foundational strength and landing competence. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0004 — Women's-football physical-performance benchmarks

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Compton HR, et al. *Benchmarking the Physical Performance Qualities in Women's Football: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis Across the Performance Scale.* Sports Med. 2026;56(Suppl 1):127–155. |
| Locator | [PMID 40887564](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40887564/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-025-02251-0`; benchmark-label/unit correction [PMID 41160240](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41160240/) |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 1,855 estimates from 982 groups across 288 studies. Intermittent fitness, sprinting and lower-limb power differentiated performance tiers, with substantial protocol and population heterogeneity. The correction confirms 288 studies, changes Tier 2 from `>3 sessions/week` to `≤3 sessions/week`, and fixes acceleration-time units from centimetres to seconds. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Female athletic assessment should include qualities that matter to the sport and use sex- and level-relevant reference data where available. |
| Prohibited overreach | Normative distributions are not minimum health standards, talent destiny or proof that one test causes success. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0005 — Nutrition evidence in female athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Larrosa M, et al. *Nutritional Strategies for Optimizing Health, Sports Performance, and Recovery for Female Athletes and Other Physically Active Women: A Systematic Review.* Nutr Rev. 2025;83:478–513. |
| Locator | [PMID 38994896](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38994896/); DOI `10.1093/nutrit/nuae082` |
| Design and finding | Systematic review of 71 studies and 1,654 women; most intervention evidence concerned supplements and risk of bias was commonly moderate. Carbohydrate availability, adequate protein and correction of genuine deficiencies may matter, but female-specific evidence remains sparse. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Athletic fuelling should protect energy and nutrient adequacy and be matched to training demands. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not import the review's meal number or supplement doses as universal Evolved rules; individual targets and deficiency treatment remain within the Megan 1:1 or clinical boundary. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0006 — Iron deficiency and female athletic performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Pengelly M, et al. *Iron deficiency, supplementation, and sports performance in female athletes: A systematic review.* J Sport Health Sci. 2025;14:101009. |
| Locator | [PMID 39536912](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39536912/); DOI `10.1016/j.jshs.2024.101009` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-three studies involving 669 high-level iron-deficient female athletes. Deficiency was associated with poorer endurance, while supplementation in deficient athletes often improved aerobic outcomes; most samples were small and other performance outcomes varied. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Unexplained fatigue or performance deterioration can justify clinical iron assessment, especially in female athletes at risk. |
| Prohibited overreach | Trainers do not diagnose iron deficiency, use symptoms alone, recommend therapeutic iron or generalise benefit to iron-replete women. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0007 — Elite-performance consensus and technology boundary

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bangsbo J, et al. *Consensus Statements: Optimizing Performance of the Elite Athlete.* Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2025;35:e70112. |
| Locator | [PMID 40781883](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40781883/); [PMCID PMC12334928](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12334928/); DOI `10.1111/sms.70112` |
| Design and finding | Multidisciplinary consensus covering training, nutrition, female athletes, injury and emerging technology. Individualised sport-specific practice is supported; wearables and multi-omics are promising but require validation. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Use measurement and technology when it answers a clear coaching question and has adequate validity, without displacing athlete feedback or professional judgement. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not treat consumer-device precision, biomarker novelty or algorithmic personalisation as proven outcome improvement. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0008 — Research-quality standard for elite women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Elliott-Sale KJ, et al. *Methodological Considerations for Studies in Sport and Exercise Science with Women as Participants Part II: Guidance for Applied Studies in Elite Female Athletes.* Sports Med. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42108373](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42108373/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-026-02442-3` |
| Design and finding | Methodological guidance covering the broad range of ovarian-hormone profiles across puberty, contraception, menstrual dysfunction, pregnancy, postpartum and menopause. It calls for explicit questions, appropriate designs, validated methods and transparent reporting. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Downgrade female-performance studies that poorly verify or report the hormonal state central to their claim. |
| Prohibited overreach | Methodological quality does not imply that hormones must dominate every female athlete's programme or that imperfect studies have no informational value. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0009 — Acute caffeine in female team sports

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Li H, et al. *Effects of Acute Caffeine Supplementation on Physical, Sport-Specific, Physiological, Perceptual, and Cognitive Outcomes in Female Team-Sport Athletes: A Three-Level Meta-Analysis.* Nutrients. 2026;18:2429. |
| Locator | [PMID 42588052](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42588052/); DOI `10.3390/nu18152429` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-six randomised blinded crossover trials. Small outcome-specific physical benefits and lower perceived exertion were observed; sport-skill and cognitive evidence was uncertain, and dose or timing moderators were not established. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Caffeine can modestly support some acute performance outcomes for some female athletes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise cognitive improvement, prescribe a universal dose or ignore sleep, anxiety, pregnancy, medication, tolerance and individual-response boundaries. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0010 — Menstrual phase and elite endurance performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Britton II, Duncan WC. *The effects of hormone changes over the menstrual cycle on elite athlete performance: meta-analysis and suggested standardised protocol.* Reprod Fertil. 2026;7. |
| Locator | [PMID 42455790](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42455790/); DOI `10.1530/RAF-25-0131` |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of five studies that hormonally confirmed phase in elite or well-trained athletes. Maximal oxygen uptake did not differ significantly between early follicular and mid-luteal phases; small individual effects remain possible. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Population evidence does not justify routine menstrual-phase programming; individual symptoms and repeated personal responses may still inform adjustments. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim the cycle never matters, or that one phase reliably improves or impairs all performance qualities. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0011 — Lower-body strength transfer to sprint performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Seitz LB, Reyes A, Tran TT, Saez de Villarreal E, Haff GG. *Increases in lower-body strength transfer positively to sprint performance: a systematic review with meta-analysis.* Sports Med. 2014;44:1693–1702. |
| Locator | [PMID 25059334](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25059334/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-014-0227-1` |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 15 studies, 510 participants and 85 nested effect sizes. Increases in back-squat strength were strongly associated with sprint improvement, with an average reported sprint improvement of 3.11%; transfer varied with subject and programme characteristics and the effect-size base was uneven. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Increasing lower-body strength can transfer positively to sprint performance and supports strength as a high-leverage general athletic intervention when force capacity is limiting. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim strength alone produces sprint skill, every strength gain improves speed by 3.11%, the strongest athlete is always fastest or the finding specifies one universal programme. |
| Review | Reviewed 18 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-EXD-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0012 — Resistance training and elite sport-specific performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Makaruk H, Starzak M, Tarkowski P, Sadowski J, Winchester J. *The Effects of Resistance Training on Sport-Specific Performance of Elite Athletes: A Systematic Review with Meta-Analysis.* J Hum Kinet. 2024;91:135–155. |
| Locator | [PMID 38689584](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38689584/); [PMCID PMC11057612](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11057612/); DOI `10.5114/jhk/185877` |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 35 studies and 777 elite athletes found a large overall improvement in sport-specific performance, but heterogeneity was substantial (I² 84%). Effects varied by competitive level and outcome; the female subgroup estimate was large but not statistically significant, probably because female participant numbers were limited. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training can improve sport-specific performance, while programme design and transfer must be individualised to the athlete, event and current limiting quality. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not call the pooled effect universal, infer that strength replaces sport practice, claim a proven female-specific effect magnitude or ignore the high heterogeneity and non-significant subgroup findings. |
| Review | Reviewed 18 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-EXD-001`. |

### STU-FAP-0013 — Compatibility of concurrent aerobic and strength training

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Schumann M, Feuerbacher JF, Sünkeler M, Freitag N, Rønnestad BR, Doma K, Lundberg TR. *Compatibility of Concurrent Aerobic and Strength Training for Skeletal Muscle Size and Function: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Sports Med. 2022;52:601–612. |
| Locator | [PMID 34757594](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34757594/); [PMCID PMC8891239](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8891239/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-021-01587-7` |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 43 studies comparing concurrent aerobic and strength training with the same strength prescription alone. Concurrent training did not materially compromise maximal strength or hypertrophy, but explosive-strength gains were modestly attenuated, especially when aerobic and strength work occurred in the same session. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Strength and general conditioning can be developed concurrently; order, separation and fatigue should reflect the priority adaptation, particularly when explosive performance matters. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim aerobic work must wait until strength is complete, that concurrent training never creates trade-offs, or that one session order is universally optimal across sports, volumes and athletes. |
| Review | Reviewed 18 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-EXD-001`. |

### Female athlete health and injury

### STU-FAH-0001 — Current female-athlete lifespan and health position

| Field | Record |

| Citation | Dabbs NC, et al. *National Strength and Conditioning Association Position Statement on Strength and Conditioning of Female Athletes. Part I: Lifespan, Injury, and Health Considerations.* J Strength Cond Res. 2026;40:875–894. |

### STU-FAH-0002 — IOC FAIR injury-prevention consensus

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |

### STU-FAH-0003 — Neuromuscular training and lower-extremity injury

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bullock GS, et al. *Prevention strategies for lower extremity injury: a systematic review and meta-analyses for the FAIR consensus.* Br J Sports Med. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40645751](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40645751/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2025-109910` |
| Design and finding | Eighty-two studies with 154,561 participants. Neuromuscular programmes incorporating balance, strength, agility and change of direction reduced ACL injuries substantially; broader lower-limb and ankle estimates were less precise. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |

### STU-FAH-0004 — Limits of lower-extremity risk prediction

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Whittaker JL, et al. *Modifiable risk factors for lower-extremity injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis for the FAIR consensus.* Br J Sports Med. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40908104](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40908104/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2025-109902` |
| Design and finding | Review of 195 studies and 66 risk-factor categories. Female-specific evidence was sparse, heterogeneous and frequently biased; pooled strength measures were not associated with the combined injury outcomes examined. |

### STU-FAH-0009 — Updated Triad screening and return-to-play guidance

| Design and finding | Clinical consensus moves away from one energy-availability threshold and updates screening, treatment, clearance and return-to-play guidance for adolescents and adults. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Suspected energy-deficiency, menstrual, eating or bone-stress concerns require qualified multidisciplinary assessment and clearance decisions. |
| Prohibited overreach | Evolved does not prescribe therapeutic intake, diagnose functional hypothalamic amenorrhoea or clear an athlete to return. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-FAH-0010 — Athlete-centred menstrual tracking

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Verhagen E, et al. *UEFA consensus statement on menstrual cycle tracking in women's football.* BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med. 2025;11:e002769. |
| Locator | [PMID 41001255](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41001255/); DOI `10.1136/bmjsem-2025-002769` |
| Design and finding | Multidisciplinary RAND-UCLA consensus. Phase links with performance or injury remain inconclusive; tracking can support symptom recognition, health, education and athlete autonomy when privacy and culture are respected. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Voluntary tracking may help an athlete identify symptoms and request appropriate adjustment or care. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not mandate tracking, infer ovulation from an app, expose reproductive data or prescribe cycle-synchronised training as settled science. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### Female cognitive and brain performance

### STU-COG-0016 — Menopause creatine-formulation pilot

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Korovljev D, et al. *The Effects of 8-Week Creatine Hydrochloride and Creatine Ethyl Ester Supplementation on Cognition, Clinical Outcomes, and Brain Creatine Levels in Perimenopausal and Menopausal Women: A Randomized Controlled Trial.* J Nutr Health Aging. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 40854087](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40854087/); DOI `10.1080/27697061.2025.2551184` |
| Design and finding | Small four-arm double-blind RCT in 36 women. One creatine-hydrochloride dose improved selected reaction-time and brain-creatine measures over eight weeks; multiplicity, sample size and use of less-established formulations limit inference. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Direct female menopause evidence is emerging and supports further study of brain creatine and cognition. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim creatine treats menopausal brain fog, prevents dementia or that these formulations outperform creatine monohydrate. Supplement advice remains outside standard coaching. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0017 — Acute creatine during sleep deprivation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Gordji-Nejad A, et al. *Single-Dose Creatine Reduces Sleep Deprivation-Induced Deterioration in Cognitive Performance.* Nutrients. 2026;18:1192. |
| Locator | [PMID 42075005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42075005/); DOI `10.3390/nu18081192` |
| Design and finding | Randomised crossover study in 29 healthy adults during 21 hours of sleep deprivation. A single 0.2 g/kg dose mitigated deterioration in selected tasks; female subgroup effects were exploratory. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Creatine has a plausible acute cognitive-resilience signal under severe sleep loss. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not use this protocol as routine supplementation advice, a sleep substitute, female-specific proof or dementia evidence. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0018 — Bodyweight strength training and cognition

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Levín Catrilao Á, et al. *Effects of Body Weight Strength Training on Cognitive Function and Quality of Life in Healthy Older People: A Systematic Review of Randomized Controlled Trials.* Life. 2025;15:1698. |
| Locator | [PMID 41302123](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41302123/); DOI `10.3390/life15111698` |
| Design and finding | Nine RCTs with 682 older adults, 85% women. Individual trials reported gains in several cognitive domains, but certainty was insufficient for definitive recommendations. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Accessible strength exercise may contribute to cognitive as well as physical function in older adults. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim bodyweight training prevents dementia, is superior to loaded training or produces uniform cognitive change. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0019 — Postmenopausal dual-task performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Gözütok NH, Eyuboglu F. *Effects of a high-intensity functional exercise program on menopausal symptoms, physical performance, and dual-task performance in postmenopausal women: a randomized controlled trial.* BMC Womens Health. 2026;26. |
| Locator | [PMID 42226147](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42226147/); DOI `10.1186/s12905-026-04572-2` |
| Design and finding | Eight-week RCT in 40 postmenopausal women. Functional exercise improved physical and cognitive-motor dual-task measures versus control; the small short trial needs replication. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Training that integrates strength, movement and attention can improve measured dual-task function in postmenopausal women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not generalise to dementia prevention, all menopausal symptoms or one branded high-intensity format. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0020 — Short multicomponent exercise and executive function

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ramadhana DR, et al. *Short-term multicomponent exercise training improves executive function in postmenopausal women.* PLoS One. 2024;19:e0307812. |
| Locator | [PMID 39141601](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39141601/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0307812` |
| Design and finding | Controlled two-week study in 30 postmenopausal women using frequent combined aerobic, strength, balance and flexibility sessions. Selected Stroop and global-cognition measures improved. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Multicomponent exercise can produce short-term cognitive-performance changes in postmenopausal women. |
| Prohibited overreach | The design cannot isolate strength training, establish durability or demonstrate neuroprotection. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0021 — Napping after sleep restriction in female athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | BenSalem S, et al. *Enhanced physical performance, attention, and mood states after a nap opportunity following a sleep restriction night in female athletes: A randomized controlled trial.* J Sports Sci. 2025;43:477–489. |
| Locator | [PMID 39965883](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39965883/); DOI `10.1080/02640414.2025.2464440` |
| Design and finding | Crossover trial in 14 trained female boxers. Forty- and 90-minute nap opportunities improved selected physical, attention, mood and recovery outcomes after normal or restricted sleep, with larger effects for 90 minutes. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | A planned nap can be a useful recovery option when night sleep has been restricted. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not prescribe 90 minutes universally, promise performance improvement or treat napping as a repair for chronic inadequate sleep or a sleep disorder. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0022 — Sleep, sex and concussion cognitive testing

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kalagara R, et al. *Analysis of the interaction between sleep quantity and sex in neurocognitive performance testing for sports-related concussion.* J Neurosurg Pediatr. 2025;36:193–200. |
| Locator | [PMID 40344767](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40344767/); DOI `10.3171/2025.2.PEDS24208` |
| Design and finding | Retrospective analysis of 11,563 high-school athletes. Self-reported prior-night sleep and sex interacted with selected baseline and post-injury test scores; the study is observational and test-specific. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Sleep context can affect interpretation of neurocognitive testing and should be documented by clinicians. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not diagnose concussion, infer recovery or make sex-specific treatment decisions from this association. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0023 — Resistance training, cognition and cortical thickness

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kušleikienė S, et al. *Cognitive gains and cortical thickness changes after 12 weeks of resistance training in older adults with low and high risk of mild cognitive impairment: Findings from a randomized controlled trial.* Brain Res Bull. 2025;222:111249. |
| Locator | [PMID 39954817](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39954817/); DOI `10.1016/j.brainresbull.2025.111249` |
| Design and finding | RCT in 70 adults aged 60–85, 38 women; 50 supplied complete data. Lower-limb resistance training affected a response-inhibition measure and parahippocampal cortical thickness, with some subgroup findings only uncorrected or exploratory. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Resistance training has promising cognitive and structural brain signals in older adults, including those at higher MCI risk. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim neuronal loss was prevented, dementia risk was reduced or that the result is female-specific. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0024 — Long-term resistance training in older women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Best JR, et al. *Long-Term Effects of Resistance Exercise Training on Cognition and Brain Volume in Older Women: Results from a Randomized Controlled Trial.* J Int Neuropsychol Soc. 2015;21:745–756. |
| Locator | [PMID 26581787](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26581787/); DOI `10.1017/S1355617715000673` |
| Design and finding | RCT in 155 older women comparing one year of once- or twice-weekly resistance training with balance and toning. At two years, both resistance groups had better executive function; twice weekly also benefited memory, white-matter volume and peak power. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Regular resistance training can have durable cognitive and brain-volume benefits in older women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim it prevents dementia, establish a universal frequency standard or assume the imaging and cognitive effects share one mechanism. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

### STU-COG-0025 — Midlife subjective cognitive decline and brain energetics

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mosconi L, et al. *Neurophysiological correlates of subjective cognitive decline in perimenopausal and postmenopausal midlife women at risk for Alzheimer's disease.* Menopause. 2025;32:433–442. |
| Locator | [PMID 40067757](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40067757/); DOI `10.1097/GME.0000000000002512` |
| Design and finding | Cross-sectional study of 156 cognitively normal midlife women with family-history or APOE risk. Subjective decline was associated with selected cognitive differences, menopausal symptoms and altered regional phosphocreatine-to-ATP ratios, without overall volume or blood-flow differences. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Brain bioenergetics is a credible emerging research direction for menopausal cognition. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not interpret subjective brain fog as Alzheimer's disease, use a biomarker association as diagnosis or claim exercise or creatine corrects the observed pattern. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-WAC-001`. |

## Nutrition cohort expansion — dietary pattern, plant-based adequacy, performance, behaviour and access

### Dietary pattern, fibre and food processing

### STU-NDP-0001 — Ultra-processed-food interventions

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Aramburu A, et al. *Ultra-processed foods consumption and health-related outcomes: a systematic review of randomized controlled trials.* Front Nutr. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38988861](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38988861/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2024.1421728` |
| Design and finding | Four trials, 455 participants. Reducing ultra-processed foods improved selected intake, weight, waist and lipid outcomes, but 30 of 42 outcomes were null and certainty was mostly low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support a practical shift toward less-processed, nutrient-dense foods without presenting processing level as a complete diet-quality score. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim avoiding all ultra-processed foods independently prevents disease or that every processed convenience food is harmful. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0002 — Ultra-processed foods in pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Akyakar B, et al. *Ultra-processed Food Intake During Pregnancy and its Impact on Maternal Diet Quality and Weight Change: A Systematic Review of Observational Studies.* Curr Nutr Rep. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39370494](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39370494/); DOI `10.1007/s13668-024-00580-6` |
| Design and finding | Twelve observational studies linked higher intake with poorer dietary-pattern scores and gestational-weight differences. Exposure definitions and confounding prevent causal inference. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support pregnancy conversations about overall dietary pattern and access. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not attribute a pregnancy outcome to one processing category or prescribe a restrictive antenatal diet. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0003 — Fibre and chronic-disease associations

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Veronese N, et al. *The impact of dietary fiber consumption on human health: An umbrella review of evidence from 17,155,277 individuals.* Clin Nutr. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40651334](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40651334/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnu.2025.06.021` |
| Design and finding | Umbrella review of 33 meta-analyses found the strongest association evidence for cardiovascular mortality, pancreatic cancer and diverticular disease; much of the evidence remained observational and varied in quality. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support fibre-rich food variety as a dietary-pattern priority. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not turn observational risk associations into an individual prevention guarantee or universal supplement dose. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0004 — Fibre supplements for chronic constipation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | van der Schoot A, et al. *The Effect of Fiber Supplementation on Chronic Constipation in Adults: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials.* Am J Clin Nutr. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 35816465](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35816465/); DOI `10.1093/ajcn/nqac184` |
| Design and finding | Sixteen trials found improved response, frequency and consistency, especially with psyllium or pectin, alongside high heterogeneity and increased flatulence. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Establish that fibre type, dose, tolerance and clinical context matter. |
| Prohibited overreach | Standard coaches do not treat chronic constipation or prescribe a therapeutic fibre protocol. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0005 — Ultra-processed foods and incident cardiometabolic risk

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Mambrini SP, et al. *Ultra-Processed Food Consumption and Incidence of Obesity and Cardiometabolic Risk Factors in Adults: A Systematic Review of Prospective Studies.* Nutrients. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37299546](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37299546/); DOI `10.3390/nu15112583` |
| Design and finding | Seventeen prospective studies associated higher intake with incident obesity and selected cardiometabolic risks; residual confounding and changing diet quality remain material. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support pattern-level attention to food quality and displacement. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not present the association as proof that processing itself caused an individual's condition. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0006 — Ultra-processed foods and nutrient displacement

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Martini D, et al. *Ultra-Processed Foods and Nutritional Dietary Profile: A Meta-Analysis of Nationally Representative Samples.* Nutrients. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 34684391](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34684391/); DOI `10.3390/nu13103390` |
| Design and finding | National survey syntheses linked greater intake with more free sugar, fat and saturated fat and lower fibre, protein and several micronutrients. Aggregated cross-sectional data cannot isolate processing from food composition. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Explain displacement: frequent convenience foods can crowd out protein, plants and micronutrient-rich foods. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not classify foods morally or require complete avoidance. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0007 — Nutrient adequacy across plant-based and meat-containing diets

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Neufingerl N, et al. *Nutrient Intake and Status in Adults Consuming Plant-Based Diets Compared to Meat-Eaters: A Systematic Review.* Nutrients. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 35010904](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35010904/); DOI `10.3390/nu14010029` |
| Design and finding | Across 141 studies, plant-based diets were higher in several beneficial nutrients but lower in B12, vitamin D, iron, zinc, iodine, calcium and long-chain omega-3 measures; inadequacies also occurred in meat eaters. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support diet-quality and nutrient-adequacy review regardless of dietary identity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim plant-based or omnivorous eating is inherently adequate, superior or deficient for every person. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NDP-0008 — Vegan-diet adequacy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bakaloudi DR, et al. *Intake and adequacy of the vegan diet: a systematic review of the evidence.* Clin Nutr. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 33341313](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33341313/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnu.2020.11.035` |
| Design and finding | Forty-eight mostly observational European studies identified recurrent low intakes of B12, zinc, calcium, selenium and other nutrients, without proving clinical deficiency in every vegan. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support planned food selection and appropriate qualified assessment in restrictive dietary patterns. |
| Prohibited overreach | Trainers do not diagnose deficiency or prescribe supplements from dietary identity alone. |

### STU-NDP-0009 — Plant-based patterns and chronic-disease associations

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Capodici A, et al. *Cardiovascular health and cancer risk associated with plant based diets: An umbrella review.* PLoS One. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38748667](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38748667/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0300711` |
| Design and finding | Plant-based patterns were associated with favourable cardiometabolic markers and some lower disease risks, but heterogeneity, lifestyle confounding and average review quality limit causal claims. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support varied plant-rich dietary patterns without demanding animal-food exclusion. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise cancer or cardiovascular prevention or equate plant-based with healthy by definition. |

### STU-NDP-0010 — Plant-based diets and osteoporosis association

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zheng S, et al. *Plant-based diet and risk of osteoporosis: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Clin Nutr. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40494032](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40494032/); DOI `10.1016/j.clnu.2025.05.023` |
| Design and finding | Twenty observational studies associated plant-based diets with higher lumbar-spine osteoporosis odds, with extreme heterogeneity and design-driven differences. |

### STU-NPF-0001 — Plant-based diets and physical performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Damasceno YO, et al. *Plant-based diets benefit aerobic performance and do not compromise strength/power performance: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Br J Nutr. 2024. |

### STU-NPF-0002 — Vegan pregnancy evidence

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Meulenbroeks D, et al. *The Association of a Vegan Diet during Pregnancy with Maternal and Child Outcomes: A Systematic Review.* Nutrients. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39408296](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39408296/); DOI `10.3390/nu16193329` |
| Design and finding | Only six heterogeneous, underpowered studies were eligible. Several nutrient intakes and birth weight were lower, while evidence was insufficient for firm outcome conclusions. |

### STU-NPF-0003 — Healthy versus unhealthy plant-based patterns

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang Y, et al. *Associations between plant-based dietary patterns and risks of type 2 diabetes, cardiovascular disease, cancer, and mortality: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Nutr J. 2023. |
| Locator | [PMID 37789346](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37789346/); DOI `10.1186/s12937-023-00877-2`; cancer-analysis correction [PMID 38178231](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38178231/) |
| Design and finding | Seventy-six prospective publications associated healthier plant-rich patterns with lower chronic-disease risks, while unhealthy plant-based patterns were associated with worse outcomes. Heterogeneity was substantial. A data-extraction correction revised cancer estimates, including the pooled cancer RR from 0.91 to 0.88 and a now-significant prostate-cancer association; the broad dietary-quality conclusion was unchanged. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Dietary quality matters more than the plant-based label alone. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer causality or promise disease prevention. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NPF-0004 — Carbohydrate–fat cardiometabolic trade-offs

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Nikitara K, et al. *Cardiometabolic trade-offs of carbohydrate- and fat-focused diets: a network meta-analysis of randomised clinical trials.* Eur J Nutr. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42418007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42418007/); DOI `10.1007/s00394-026-04028-1` |
| Design and finding | Forty-seven trials found differing lipid and adiposity trade-offs; no macronutrient distribution was uniformly favourable across outcomes, and food quality was incompletely represented. |

| Design and finding | Narrative review identifies recurring energy and micronutrient concerns and emphasises multidisciplinary therapeutic care. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support early recognition and referral when low-energy-availability, menstrual or bone concerns cluster. |
| Prohibited overreach | Trainers do not diagnose or treat REDs or infer it from one symptom or food log. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NPF-0010 — Nutrition and menstrual symptoms

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Brown N, et al. *Nutritional practices to manage menstrual cycle related symptoms: a systematic review.* Nutr Res Rev. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 37746736](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37746736/); DOI `10.1017/S0954422423000227` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-eight studies used 21 interventions and 18 symptom measures; inconsistency prevented confident food or supplement recommendations despite selected positive signals. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |

| Prohibited overreach | Do not sell a menstrual symptom diet or supplement stack. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### Eating behaviour, adolescents and safeguarding

### STU-NEB-0001 — Menstrual phase and resting metabolism

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Benton MJ, et al. *Effect of menstrual cycle on resting metabolism: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* PLoS One. 2020. |
| Locator | [PMID 32658929](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32658929/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0236025` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-six studies suggested a small luteal-phase increase, but the effect was attenuated and non-significant in newer studies; samples and cycle verification were weak. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Explain why small population effects do not create automatic nutrition changes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not prescribe cycle-phase calories or claim metabolism meaningfully resets each month. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NEB-0002 — Plant-based athletic performance review

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Presti N, et al. *The Impact Plant-Based Diets Have on Athletic Performance and Body Composition: A Systematic Review.* J Am Nutr Assoc. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38913935](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38913935/); DOI `10.1080/27697061.2024.2365755` |
| Design and finding | Eight studies found no evidence of performance harm and selected possible benefits; long-term professional-athlete evidence was absent. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Corroborate compatibility of well-planned plant-based eating with training. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim performance superiority or ignore protein and micronutrient adequacy. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NEB-0003 — School eating-disorder prevention

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Berry SL, et al. *A systematic review and meta-analysis of eating disorder preventative interventions in schools.* Eur Eat Disord Rev. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 39545807](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39545807/); DOI `10.1002/erv.3149` |
| Design and finding | Twelve school trials found some short-term benefit from media-literacy and dissonance programmes, without reliable persistence across groups and time. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support body-neutral, non-diet messaging and specialist prevention pathways for adolescents. |
| Prohibited overreach | Gym coaching is not an eating-disorder prevention or treatment programme. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NEB-0004 — Eating disorders and disordered eating in athletes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Nasser SN, et al. *Prevalence of eating disorders and disordered eating in athletes: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Br J Sports Med. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42392846](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42392846/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2025-110678` |
| Design and finding | Across 190 studies and 58,335 athletes, pooled ED/DE prevalence was 20%; estimates varied substantially by instrument and rose with greater female representation. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Justify safeguarding, validated specialist screening and caution with physique dieting. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not diagnose from appearance, calorie tracking, training volume or one questionnaire. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NEB-0005 — Intuitive eating and psychological correlates

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Linardon J, et al. *Intuitive eating and its psychological correlates: a meta-analysis.* Int J Eat Disord. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 33786858](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33786858/); DOI `10.1002/eat.23509` |
| Design and finding | Ninety-seven studies linked intuitive eating with more positive body image and wellbeing and less eating pathology; 89% were cross-sectional. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support autonomy and a less adversarial relationship with food. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim intuitive eating causes psychological health or suits every clinical presentation. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

### STU-NEB-0006 — Mindful/intuitive eating and dietary intake

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Grider HS, et al. *The Influence of Mindful Eating and/or Intuitive Eating Approaches on Dietary Intake: A Systematic Review.* J Acad Nutr Diet. 2021. |
| Locator | [PMID 33279464](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33279464/); DOI `10.1016/j.jand.2020.10.019` |
| Design and finding | Thirteen randomised studies provided little evidence of improved energy intake or diet quality and were generally low quality. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Treat mindful eating as an optional awareness skill, not a metabolic intervention. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise appetite regulation, weight loss or diet-quality improvement. |
| Review | Reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-PUBMED-NCE-001`. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |

| Locator | [PMID 36274010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36274010/); DOI `10.1016/j.jneb.2022.08.008` |
| Design and finding | Fourteen interventions reported neutral or favourable diet-quality outcomes, but heterogeneous designs and narrative synthesis prevent a universal effect estimate. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Intuitive principles need not undermine dietary quality. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim a specific delivery format is proven superior or replace eating-disorder care. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Eaton M, et al. *A systematic review of observational studies exploring the relationship between health and non-weight-centric eating behaviours.* Appetite. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38643903](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38643903/); DOI `10.1016/j.appet.2024.107361` |
| Design and finding | Eighty-six observational studies associated intuitive, mindful and competent eating with selected physical, behavioural and psychological outcomes; causality and population diversity were limited. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |

### STU-NEB-0009 — Food insecurity and mental health

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Pourmotabbed A, et al. *Food insecurity and mental health: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Public Health Nutr. 2020. |
| Locator | [PMID 32174292](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32174292/); DOI `10.1017/S136898001900435X`; affiliation correction [PMID 32364104](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32364104/) |
| Design and finding | Nineteen observational studies associated food insecurity with depression and stress; directionality and unmeasured social determinants remain unresolved. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |

### STU-NEB-0010 — Food insecurity and micronutrient status

| Field | Record |

| Citation | Lopes SO, et al. *Food Insecurity and Micronutrient Deficiency in Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Nutrients. 2023. |

| Design and finding | Eighteen studies, mostly involving women, associated food insecurity with anaemia, low ferritin and other micronutrient concerns; evidence was observational. |

### Access, pregnancy equity and implementation

### STU-NAX-0001 — Maternal food insecurity and low birth weight

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | de Freitas Rocha AR, et al. *Maternal food insecurity increases the risk of low birth weight babies: Systematic review and meta-analysis.* Acta Paediatr. 2024. |

### STU-NAX-0002 — Healthcare food-access interventions in pregnancy

| --- | --- |
| Citation | Merchant T, et al. *Healthcare-based interventions to address food insecurity during pregnancy: a systematic review.* Am J Obstet Gynecol MFM. 2023. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |

| Design and finding | Twenty-five studies linked food insecurity with stress, mood disorder and gestational diabetes but not consistently with several birth outcomes; most evidence came from North America. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Litvin K, et al. *How Do Social and Behavioral Change Interventions Respond to Social Norms to Improve Women's Diets in Low- and Middle-Income Countries? A Scoping Review.* Curr Dev Nutr. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38948109](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38948109/); DOI `10.1016/j.cdnut.2024.103772` |

### STU-NAX-0005 — Randomised food-insecurity interventions

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Williams Sites KE, et al. *Addressing Food Insecurity in the United States: A Systematic Review of Interventions from Randomized Controlled Trials.* Nutr Rev. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41653442](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41653442/); DOI `10.1093/nutrit/nuag002` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-five US trials produced mixed results; only 44% improved food-security outcomes and no single intervention was consistently effective. |

### STU-NAX-0006 — Behaviour-change techniques for weight outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Preuhs K, et al. *Effective Behavior Change Techniques for Successful Weight Loss and Weight Loss Maintenance Among Adults With Overweight and Obesity: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Obes Rev. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42432828](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42432828/); DOI `10.1111/obr.70195` |
| Design and finding | Sixty-five trials found a small additional long-term weight effect, no significant maintenance effect after initial loss, and selected promising techniques without a reliable dose-by-component rule. |

### STU-NAX-0007 — Exercise preserves fat-free mass during energy restriction

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Deller M, et al. *Effects of Calorie Restriction With and Without Strength, Endurance or Mixed Training on Fat-Free and Skeletal Muscle Mass in Overweight or Obese Individuals.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42144246](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42144246/); DOI `10.1111/dom.70873` |
| Design and finding | Thirty-four trials found exercise preserved about 0.87 kg more fat-free mass than calorie restriction alone; mixed and strength modes had favourable point estimates, while skeletal-muscle reporting was sparse. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
