### STU-NAX-0008 — Ketogenic versus higher-carbohydrate diets under comparable prescriptions

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Żurawski T, et al. *Do Ketogenic Diets Produce Greater Weight Loss than Higher-Carbohydrate Diets Under Comparable Prescribed Energy Conditions?* Nutrients. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42588148](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42588148/); DOI `10.3390/nu18152525` |
| Design and finding | Six meta-analysed trials found about 1.5 kg greater short-term loss, but evidence was low certainty and achieved energy/protein intake, body composition and durability were uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim ketogenic eating bypasses energy balance or is universally superior. |

### STU-NAX-0009 — High-protein diets and renal/body-composition outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Teixeira Silva PR, et al. *Effects of High-Protein Diets on Renal Function and Body Composition in Adults Without Chronic Kidney Disease.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42289790](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42289790/); DOI `10.1111/dom.70996` |
| Design and finding | Twenty-two short trials found increased creatinine-estimated filtration without consistent biochemical injury, modest fat reduction and no clear fat-free-mass gain; long-term renal implications remain uncertain. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | More protein is not automatically more muscle, and clinical context matters. |

### STU-NAX-0010 — Fat-free mass and mortality association

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Haas VL, et al. *Association Between Fat-Free Mass and Mortality: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* J Cachexia Sarcopenia Muscle. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42430205](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42430205/); DOI `10.1002/jcsm.70331` |
| Design and finding | Forty-nine cohorts associated low fat-free mass with higher mortality; the association was smaller in women and varied materially by measurement method. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |

| Field | Record |

## Supplied emerging study — resistance exercise and brain-age biomarker

### STU-COG-0026 — Resistance exercise and functional brain-age clocks

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Gonzalez-Gomez R, et al. *Randomized controlled trial of resistance exercise and brain aging clocks.* GeroScience. 2026;48(3):3251–3262. |
| Locator | [PMID 41665740](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41665740/); [DOI 10.1007/s11357-026-02141-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11357-026-02141-x) |
| Design and population | Secondary longitudinal neuroimaging analysis of 309 cognitively healthy adults aged 62–70 from the Danish LISA randomised trial; 39–41% of each group was female. Heavy training used three supervised weekly sessions. Moderate training used one supervised session plus two home sessions. Both programmes combined resistance and functional training; control participants maintained habitual low activity. |
| Finding | After a significant time-by-group interaction, within-group functional-connectivity brain-age gaps decreased in heavy training by 1.42 modelled years at one year and 1.85 at two years, and in moderate training by 1.39 and 2.26 years. Control changes were not significant. Heavy training also increased selected prefrontal connectivity relative to control. The biomarker–leg-strength association was small overall and inconsistent by group. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support the emerging proposition that sustained structured resistance-containing exercise may favourably alter distributed functional-connectivity markers associated with brain ageing in healthy older adults. Use as a brain-biomarker finding alongside stronger cognition, function and longitudinal evidence. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say lifting makes a brain biologically two years younger, prevents dementia, proves cognitive improvement, predicts longevity, establishes a women-specific effect or shows heavy training is superior to the moderate programme. Do not treat a model-derived brain-age gap as a clinical diagnosis or direct measure of neurodegeneration. |
| Transfer limits | Healthy older adults from a high-income European setting; mixed-sex sample; substantial medical, neurological and musculoskeletal exclusions; resistance and functional training were combined; clinical meaning and longer-term outcome relevance remain untested. |
| Review | Full open-access paper reviewed 17 August 2026. Source `SRC-SPRINGER-RBC-001`. |

## Sleep/Recovery pillar expansion — performance, monitoring, modalities, shift work and pain

### STU-SRP-0001 — Exercise during experimental sleep loss

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Faria VS, et al. *Effects of acute exercise or short-term exercise interventions on metabolic markers during experimentally-induced sleep loss in humans: A systematic literature review.* J Sport Health Sci. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40280326](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40280326/); [DOI 10.1016/j.jshs.2025.101044](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2025.101044) |
| Design and finding | Systematic review of 12 studies and 177 adults. Exercise sometimes mitigated adverse glucose, insulin and mitochondrial markers during short experimental sleep loss; protocols and outcomes were heterogeneous and short term. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise can remain useful during some sleep-loss contexts when demand is adjusted to capacity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim exercise cancels sleep loss, repairs metabolism or makes hard training appropriate when someone is dangerously sleepy or impaired. |

### STU-SRP-0002 — Sleep loss and muscular strength

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Easow J, et al. *Implications of sleep loss or sleep deprivation on muscle strength: a systematic review.* Sleep Breath. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40663194](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40663194/); [DOI 10.1007/s11325-025-03413-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11325-025-03413-0) |
| Design and finding | Thirteen controlled studies in healthy adults produced mixed results, with some strength, power, endurance, neuromuscular and fatigue measures worsening after acute or repeated sleep loss. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Treat reduced sleep as relevant capacity information for a strength session. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say one poor night always reduces strength, requires cancellation or predicts adaptation failure. |

### STU-SRP-0003 — Acute sleep deprivation and athlete performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Gong M, et al. *Effects of Acute Sleep Deprivation on Sporting Performance in Athletes: A Comprehensive Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* Nat Sci Sleep. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 39006249](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39006249/); [DOI 10.2147/NSS.S467531](https://doi.org/10.2147/NSS.S467531) |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 27 studies found average impairment across several athlete-performance domains after acute sleep deprivation, with effects varying by deprivation pattern, task and testing time. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support avoiding unnecessary severe sleep loss before demanding or safety-critical performance. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not transfer pooled athlete effects into a guaranteed individual decrement or a universal session rule. |

### STU-SRP-0004 — Endurance overreaching and sleep

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Murphy C, et al. *Does overreaching from endurance-based training impair sleep: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* PLoS One. 2024. |
| Locator | [PMID 38809828](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38809828/); [DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0303748](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0303748) |
| Design and finding | Fourteen endurance-overreaching studies suggested a small reduction in actigraphy-derived sleep efficiency without a clear pooled subjective-sleep effect; no included study had low risk of bias. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Repeated training strain and sleep trends can be reviewed together across time. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not diagnose overtraining from poor sleep or transfer endurance overreaching protocols directly to general members. |

### STU-SRP-0005 — Acute sleep deprivation and brain connectivity

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Hosang S, et al. *Brain connectivity changes associated with acute sleep deprivation: a systematic review.* Neurol Sci. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 41082128](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41082128/); [DOI 10.1007/s10072-025-08517-9](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10072-025-08517-9) |
| Design and finding | Review of 109 heterogeneous neuroimaging studies found recurring connectivity changes after acute sleep deprivation, especially in default-mode networks. Clinical meaning was not established. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support the proposition that severe sleep loss can affect brain function and should not be dismissed as a character issue. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not diagnose brain injury, cognitive decline or a disorder from sleep loss or a scan-based group finding. |

### STU-SRP-0006 — Non-drug sleep interventions after age 50

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wang J, et al. *Non-pharmacological interventions for improving sleep quality in adults aged 50 years and older: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* Arch Public Health. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41981687](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41981687/); [DOI 10.1186/s13690-026-01892-5](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13690-026-01892-5) |
| Design and finding | Network meta-analysis of 132 trials and 10,872 adults aged 50+ found several non-drug interventions improved subjective sleep; combined aerobic and resistance exercise ranked highly, but indirect comparisons and clinical populations limit transfer. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise may support sleep in later life while retaining sleep-disorder referral and ordinary training progression. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim exercise treats every later-life sleep problem or prescribe a ranked clinical intervention from network estimates. |

### STU-SRP-0007 — Physical activity dose in diagnosed insomnia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Huang Z, et al. *Dose-response relationship between physical activity and subjective sleep quality in patients with insomnia: a systematic review and Bayesian meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41965648](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41965648/); [DOI 10.1186/s12966-026-01905-0](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12966-026-01905-0) |
| Design and finding | Twenty-four trials in 1,591 people with insomnia suggested subjective improvement across exercise types with diminishing returns; certainty was low and the population had a clinical disorder. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Exercise can be a useful adjunct when clinically appropriate. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not use pooled MET-minute estimates as an Evolved insomnia prescription or substitute exercise coaching for CBT-I or medical care. |

### STU-SRP-0008 — Exercise modalities in insomnia

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bu ZJ, et al. *Effects of various exercise interventions in insomnia patients: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* BMJ Evid Based Med. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 40664502](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40664502/); [DOI 10.1136/bmjebm-2024-113512](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmjebm-2024-113512) |
| Design and finding | Twenty-two RCTs covering several exercise and mind-body modes reported improvements in selected subjective and objective sleep outcomes, mostly with low or moderate certainty and substantial indirect comparison. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Support exercise as a possible adjunct within appropriate insomnia care. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not rank one class as a cure, promise large sleep gains or deliver clinical insomnia treatment through standard coaching. |

### STU-SRP-0009 — Exercise effects depend on baseline sleep

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Liang Z, et al. *Determinants and interrelations of exercise effects on sleep quality: A multimethod meta-analysis.* Sleep Med Rev. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41650690](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41650690/); [DOI 10.1016/j.smrv.2026.102239](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2026.102239) |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of 200 trials and 23,523 adults found small objective and larger subjective sleep-quality effects, with greater benefit among poorer sleepers; certainty was very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Explain that exercise–sleep effects vary and are often larger when there is room to improve. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise a fixed programme duration, universal sleep improvement or insomnia treatment. |

### STU-SRP-0010 — Menopause exercise and sleep

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Choudhary A, Bansal K. *Menopause and movement: exercise for better sleep and psychological well-being-a systematic review.* Menopause. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 40694785](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40694785/); [DOI 10.1097/GME.0000000000002610](https://doi.org/10.1097/GME.0000000000002610) |

| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

### STU-SRP-0011 — Monitoring as decision support

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Rebelo A, et al. *Monitoring Training Effects in Athletes: A Multidimensional Framework for Decision-Making.* Sports Med. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41824225](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41824225/); [DOI 10.1007/s40279-026-02417-4](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-026-02417-4) |
| Design and finding | Narrative framework distinguishes repeated monitoring from isolated assessment and recommends minimal, adequate and accurate measures interpreted longitudinally and in context. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Support monitoring as one input to judgement and tool selection around a defined question. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not treat this framework as intervention efficacy or allow a metric to determine a session by itself. |

### STU-SRP-0012 — Subjective and wearable recovery signals

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Spetz L, et al. *Validating Subjective Ratings with Wearable Data for a Nuanced Understanding of Load-Recovery Status in Elite Endurance Athletes.* Sports Med Open. 2025. |
| Locator | [PMID 41369808](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41369808/); [DOI 10.1186/s40798-025-00958-y](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-025-00958-y) |
| Design and finding | One-year observational study of 20 elite endurance athletes found associations between subjective and device metrics alongside substantial individual variability. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Combine the person's experience with longitudinal context rather than privileging one device score. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not transfer elite-athlete correlations into a readiness algorithm for general women or infer causality. |

### STU-SRP-0013 — Consumer wearable sleep accuracy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Schyvens AM, et al. *Accuracy of Fitbit Charge 4, Garmin Vivosmart 4, and WHOOP Versus Polysomnography: Systematic Review.* JMIR Mhealth Uhealth. 2024. |

| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Wearables may provide optional trend information when uncertainty is explained. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not treat a consumer sleep stage or recovery score as polysomnography, diagnosis or exact readiness. |

### STU-SRP-0014 — Wearable estimation of sleep onset

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Scott H, et al. *A systematic review of the accuracy of sleep wearable devices for estimating sleep onset.* Sleep Med Rev. 2020. |
| Locator | [PMID 31901524](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31901524/); [DOI 10.1016/j.smrv.2019.101227](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2019.101227) |
| Design and finding | Seventy-one comparisons with polysomnography found average agreement could obscure substantial individual variability; EEG-based tools were generally more accurate. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Explain why sleep-onset estimates are approximate and should be interpreted across context and time. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not use wearable latency to diagnose insomnia or prescribe sleep restriction. |

### STU-SRP-0015 — Overtraining syndrome evidence gap

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Weakley J, et al. *Overtraining Syndrome Symptoms and Diagnosis in Athletes: Where Is the Research? A Systematic Review.* Int J Sports Physiol Perform. 2022. |
| Locator | [PMID 35320774](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35320774/); [DOI 10.1123/ijspp.2021-0448](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijspp.2021-0448) |
| Design and finding | No included study prospectively documented the required transition from healthy performance and mood into prolonged overtraining syndrome with adequate objective evidence. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Preserve uncertainty and use repeated performance, symptoms and context for referral-aware load decisions. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not diagnose overtraining syndrome, HPA-axis dysfunction or a cortisol condition from fatigue, sleep or training frequency. |

### STU-SRP-0016 — Hot and cold water after exercise

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Bustos Carvajal J, et al. *Effects of post-exercise hot and cold-water immersion on performance recovery and physiological markers in athletes: a systematic review.* Front Sports Act Living. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42491492](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42491492/); [DOI 10.3389/fspor.2026.1794029](https://doi.org/10.3389/fspor.2026.1794029) |
| Design and finding | Eight studies found inconsistent perceptual and physiological effects; hot water showed selected benefits while cold impaired immediate power in some contexts. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Water immersion is an optional context-dependent adjunct selected around the next performance demand. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not market hot or cold immersion as necessary recovery, hormonal optimisation or a guaranteed adaptation enhancer. |

### STU-SRP-0017 — Cold-water immersion over time

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Zhu Y, et al. *Temporal dynamics of muscle strength recovery following acute cold-water immersion: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* PeerJ. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42473449](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42473449/); [DOI 10.7717/peerj.21537](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj.21537) |
| Design and finding | Twenty-two RCTs found no meaningful overall isometric-strength benefit, transient immediate jump impairment and possible soreness reduction with low or very-low certainty. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Cold water may be chosen for short-term soreness when immediate power is not the priority. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim cold water speeds all recovery, improves strength adaptation or is harmless before another explosive task. |

| --- | --- |

| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

### STU-SRP-0019 — Physical therapies for muscle soreness

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |

### STU-SRP-0021 — Exercise interventions for shift workers

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Algharbi FH, et al. *Exercise interventions for sleep and cognitive dysfunction in shift workers: a systematic review of randomized trials.* Front Public Health. 2026. |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Aemmi SZ, et al. *The effectiveness of bright light exposure in shift-worker nurses: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Sleep Sci. 2020. |

### STU-SRP-0024 — Sleep hygiene in shift workers

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Shriane AE, et al. *Sleep hygiene in shift workers: A systematic literature review.* Sleep Med Rev. 2020. |

### STU-SRP-0025 — Napping during night shifts

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Dutheil F, et al. *Napping and cognitive performance during night shifts: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Sleep. 2020. |

| --- | --- |

| --- | --- |

## Thermal-recovery and female-transfer records

### STU-THR-0001 — Cold-water protocols vary by exercise mode

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Yu T, et al. *Cold water immersion protocol optimization across exercise modalities: a systematic review and network meta-analysis of resistance training, endurance exercise, and team sport applications.* BMC Sports Sci Med Rehabil. 2026;18. |
| Locator | [PMID 41845491](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41845491/); [DOI 10.1186/s13102-026-01653-5](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-026-01653-5) |
| Design and finding | Network meta-analysis of 87 studies and 2,313 participants. Passive recovery ranked most favourably for resistance-training strength; other effects varied by exercise mode and protocol. Much of the network was indirect, certainty was moderate to low and approximately 85% of participants were male. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Routine cold immediately after strength training is not the default when adaptation is the priority. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not call cold uniquely harmful to women or prescribe an “optimal” protocol from indirect rankings. |

### STU-THR-0002 — Repeated cold and training adaptation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Malta ES, et al. *The Effects of Regular Cold-Water Immersion Use on Training-Induced Changes in Strength and Endurance Performance: A Systematic Review with Meta-Analysis.* Sports Med. 2021;51:161–174. |
| Locator | [PMID 33146851](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33146851/); [DOI 10.1007/s40279-020-01362-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-020-01362-0) |
| Design and finding | Meta-analysis of eight controlled studies found smaller strength-related adaptations when cold-water immersion was used regularly with resistance training; endurance outcomes were not materially changed. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Separate repeated post-lifting cold from occasional symptom or competition use. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not transfer the pooled result into a female-specific effect or claim every exposure erases gains. |

### STU-THR-0003 — Repeated cold after strength training in men

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Roberts LA, et al. *Post-exercise cold water immersion attenuates acute anabolic signalling and long-term adaptations in muscle to strength training.* J Physiol. 2015;593:4285–4301. |
| Locator | [PMID 26174323](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26174323/); [DOI 10.1113/JP270570](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP270570) |
| Design and finding | In 21 active men training for 12 weeks, active recovery produced greater muscle and selected strength adaptations than ten minutes of cold after every session. A second nine-man experiment found differences in acute anabolic signalling. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Direct long-term evidence that repeated immediate cold may compete with hypertrophy-focused training. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not call this a women-specific result or use acute signalling as proof of a clinical outcome. |

### STU-THR-0004 — Cold blunted hypertrophy but not maximal strength

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Fyfe JJ, et al. *Cold water immersion attenuates anabolic signaling and skeletal muscle fiber hypertrophy, but not strength gain, following whole-body resistance training.* J Appl Physiol. 2019;127:1403–1418. |
| Locator | [PMID 31513450](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31513450/); [DOI 10.1152/japplphysiol.00127.2019](https://doi.org/10.1152/japplphysiol.00127.2019) |
| Design and finding | In 16 young men training for seven weeks, cold after every session blunted type-II fibre hypertrophy and anabolic signalling, while maximal-strength gains were similar. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Distinguish muscle-growth outcomes from strength outcomes when discussing cold. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say cold prevented all adaptation or assume the magnitude applies to women. |

### STU-THR-0005 — Cooling and muscle-protein synthesis

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Fuchs CJ, et al. *Postexercise cooling impairs muscle protein synthesis rates in recreational athletes.* J Physiol. 2020;598:755–772. |
| Locator | [PMID 31788800](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31788800/); [DOI 10.1113/JP278996](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP278996) |
| Design and finding | In 12 young men, the cooled leg showed lower dietary amino-acid incorporation and lower myofibrillar protein-synthesis rates than thermoneutral immersion acutely and across two weeks. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Explain one plausible pathway behind the repeated-cold adaptation concern. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not equate protein-synthesis measurements with realised female hypertrophy or long-term performance. |

### STU-THR-0006 — Direct hot-versus-cold recovery in women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Wellauer V, et al. *No acceleration of recovery from exercise-induced muscle damage after cold or hot water immersion in women: A randomised controlled trial.* PLoS One. 2025;20:e0322416. |
| Locator | [PMID 40333546](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40333546/); [PMCID PMC12057877](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12057877/); [DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0322416](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0322416) |
| Design and finding | Randomised trial in 30 healthy young women. Cold and hot immersion produced different temperature and oxygenation responses, but neither accelerated subjective or objective recovery over 72 hours versus control. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Reject universal claims that either hot or cold water is inherently superior for women's muscle-damage recovery. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not generalise one damaging-exercise model to all training, sauna formats or long-term adaptation. |

### STU-THR-0007 — Repeated cold after hamstring damage in women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Huang YC, Chen HT. *Effect of cold-water immersion treatment on recovery from exercise-induced muscle damage in the hamstring.* Eur J Sport Sci. 2025;25:e12235. |
| Locator | [PMID 39665595](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39665595/); [DOI 10.1002/ejsc.12235](https://doi.org/10.1002/ejsc.12235) |
| Design and finding | Small randomised trial in 18 women. Five daily cold immersions produced better changes in strength, soreness, flexibility and myoglobin than passive control after a severe eccentric protocol. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Preserve that cold may help selected short-term recovery outcomes in women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not treat nine participants per arm, unusual repeated dosing or biomarker changes as universal recovery proof. |

### STU-THR-0008 — Cold reduced soreness without restoring function in women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Hohenauer E, et al. *Partial-body cryotherapy (-135°C) and cold-water immersion (10°C) after muscle damage in females.* Scand J Med Sci Sports. 2020;30:485–495. |
| Locator | [PMID 31677292](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31677292/); [DOI 10.1111/sms.13593](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.13593) |
| Design and finding | Randomised trial in 28 women found that cold and partial-body cryotherapy reduced soreness versus control but did not improve swelling, isometric force or jump performance. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Separate perceived soreness relief from restored functional capacity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not call reduced soreness faster healing or improved adaptation. |

### STU-THR-0009 — Infrared sauna during female strength and power training

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Ahokas EK, et al. *Effects of repeated use of post-exercise infrared sauna on neuromuscular performance and muscle hypertrophy.* Front Sports Act Living. 2025;7:1462901. |
| Locator | [PMID 40104529](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40104529/); [DOI 10.3389/fspor.2025.1462901](https://doi.org/10.3389/fspor.2025.1462901) |
| Design and finding | Pair-matched six-week study in 40 female team-sport athletes. Both groups improved; infrared sauna added no hypertrophy benefit and produced only a tentative loaded-jump power signal. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Treat post-training infrared sauna as promising for selected power outcomes, not as a muscle-growth intervention. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say sauna increases female hypertrophy, is superior to cold or guarantees recovery. |

### STU-THR-0010 — Sex and post-exercise sauna adaptation

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kirby NV, et al. *Sex differences in adaptation to intermittent post-exercise sauna bathing in trained middle-distance runners.* Sports Med Open. 2021;7:51. |
| Locator | [PMID 34297227](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34297227/); [DOI 10.1186/s40798-021-00342-6](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-021-00342-6) |
| Design and finding | Three-week study in 26 trained runners, including 16 women. Women and men showed similar heat-tolerance and aerobic-performance improvements, with different thermoeffector adaptations. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Sauna or passive heat may be a heat-acclimation tool for trained women. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not relabel heat acclimation as recovery, hypertrophy, longevity or universal female benefit. |

### STU-THR-0011 — Heat adaptation in females

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Kelly MK, et al. *Heat Adaptation for Females: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Physiological Adaptations and Exercise Performance in the Heat.* Sports Med. 2023;53:1395–1421. |
| Locator | [PMID 37222863](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37222863/); [DOI 10.1007/s40279-023-01831-2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-023-01831-2) |
| Design and finding | Thirty-study systematic review, with 22 studies meta-analysed, found beneficial thermoregulatory adaptations and improved performance testing in the heat among females. |
| State | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| Approved use | Establish that women can adapt beneficially to structured heat exposure for heat-performance purposes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer that ordinary sauna accelerates strength recovery or is superior to cold. |

### STU-THR-0012 — Post-exercise heat and endurance performance

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Solomon TPJ, et al. *The effect of post-exercise heat exposure (passive heat acclimation) on endurance exercise performance: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* BMC Sports Sci Med Rehabil. 2025;17:4. |
| Locator | [PMID 39762944](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39762944/); [DOI 10.1186/s13102-024-01038-6](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-024-01038-6) |
| Design and finding | Ten-study meta-analysis included 199 participants, only 43 women. Performance effects were uncertain and evidence certainty was low to very low. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Keep post-exercise heat optional and goal-specific despite plausible adaptation mechanisms. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not promise aerobic improvement or claim a female-specific effect from pooled mixed-sex data. |

### STU-THR-0013 — Sauna can add fatigue before the next demand

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Skorski S, et al. *Effects of Postexercise Sauna Bathing on Recovery of Swim Performance.* Int J Sports Physiol Perform. 2020;15:934–940. |
| Locator | [PMID 31869820](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31869820/); [DOI 10.1123/ijspp.2019-0333](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijspp.2019-0333) |
| Design and finding | Randomised crossover in 20 competitive swimmers and triathletes, only three women, found worse next-morning repeated sprint-swim performance and greater perceived stress after a demanding sauna protocol. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Heat is an added dose and may be unsuitable before a near-term high-intensity demand. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not generalise this protocol to moderate sauna use, women specifically or long-term adaptation. |

### STU-THR-0014 — Acute modalities in female basketball players

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | Cabarkapa D, et al. *Acute Effects of Three Recovery Interventions on Post-Practice Vertical Jump Force-Time Metrics in Female Basketball Players.* J Funct Morphol Kinesiol. 2026;11. |
| Locator | [PMID 41718172](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41718172/); [DOI 10.3390/jfmk11010044](https://doi.org/10.3390/jfmk11010044) |
| Design and finding | In 18 adolescent female basketball players, cold water, cryotherapy and pneumatic compression were no more effective than passive recovery for post-practice lower-body force and power measures. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Avoid assuming a recovery modality restores acute female neuromuscular capacity. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not generalise an adolescent acute study to soreness, long-term adaptation or adult clinical care. |

## Recovery-myth and measurement records

These compact records are expanded in `reference/educational-intelligence/recovery-myth-translation-bank.md`. Approval remains limited to the exact use and boundary recorded below.

| ID | Study and locator | Evidence finding | Approved use and prohibited overreach | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-RMY-0001 | Schwane et al. 1983, *Is Lactic Acid Related to Delayed-Onset Muscle Soreness?* [PMID 27409551](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27409551/) | Experimental running study dissociated blood lactate from delayed soreness. | Lactate does not cause DOMS. Do not reduce all post-exercise pain to benign DOMS. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0002 | Baird et al. 2012, *Creatine-kinase- and exercise-related muscle damage implications for muscle performance and recovery.* [PMID 22288008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22288008/) | CK is indirect and highly variable across people and contexts. | Keep CK separate from soreness, function, healing and readiness; no single-value diagnosis. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0003 | Afonso et al. 2021, post-exercise stretching meta-analysis. [PMID 34025459](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34025459/) | Ten meta-analysed RCTs found no clear strength-recovery or DOMS advantage over passive recovery; certainty was very low. | Stretching may serve preference or mobility, not a promised recovery treatment. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0004 | Herbert et al. 2011, Cochrane stretching review. [PMID 21735398](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21735398/) | Twelve trials found no clinically important reduction in DOMS from stretching before or after exercise. | Reject stretching as required soreness prevention; this is not a rehabilitation conclusion. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0005 | Zhang et al. 2025, post-exercise stretching meta-analysis. [PMID 41103301](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41103301/) | Fifteen studies found no significant standalone effect on soreness, strength, performance, flexibility or pain threshold. | Post-session stretching is optional; a pooled null does not remove personal preference. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0006 | Van Hooren and Peake 2018, active cool-down review. [PMID 29663142](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29663142/) | Cool-downs cleared blood lactate faster but did not consistently improve soreness, muscle-damage markers, next-day performance or injury risk. | Transition and lactate clearance are not proof of accelerated recovery. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0007 | Ortiz et al. 2019, active-recovery systematic review. [PMID 29742750](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29742750/) | Twenty-six heterogeneous athlete studies gave weak performance evidence; lactate clearance was an unreliable recovery proxy. | Active recovery is optional and purpose-specific; movement does not always beat rest. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0008 | Torres et al. 2012, physical-therapy recovery meta-analysis. [PMID 22498151](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22498151/) | Thirty-five trials found a small massage effect and little or inconclusive evidence for several other modalities. | Historical boundary only; newer syntheses control current use. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-RMY-0009 | Dupuy et al. 2018, recovery-technique meta-analysis. [PMID 29755363](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29755363/) | Several modalities changed DOMS or perceived fatigue, with outcome-specific effects. | Preserve symptom-relief choices; never translate soreness, fatigue or biomarkers into healing or adaptation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0010 | Davis et al. 2020, sports-massage meta-analysis. [PMID 32426160](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32426160/); corrected Figure 3 [PMID 33981450](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33981450/) | No strength, jump, sprint, endurance or fatigue benefit; small flexibility and DOMS benefits. Corrected Figure 3 retained null sprint, endurance and fatigue results. | Massage may help comfort or flexibility but is not performance restoration. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0011 | Guo et al. 2017, massage and DOMS meta-analysis. [PMID 29021762](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29021762/) | Massage reduced soreness across 24–72 hours in the pooled studies. | Acknowledge possible DOMS relief without calling it repaired tissue or readiness. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0012 | Wiewelhove et al. 2019, foam-rolling meta-analysis. [PMID 31024339](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31024339/) | Mostly small or trivial performance, flexibility and soreness effects. | Optional comfort or short-term mobility tool; no fascia-release, detox or repair claim. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0013 | Hendricks et al. 2020, foam-rolling systematic review. [PMID 32507141](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32507141/) | Forty-nine studies supported short-term ROM and possible DOMS effects, with protocol and control limitations. | No universal rolling dose and no rehabilitation claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0014 | Brown et al. 2017, compression-garment meta-analysis. [PMID 28434152](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28434152/) | Earlier synthesis suggested small overall and selected strength-recovery benefits. | Preserve plausibility, but newer outcome-specific evidence prevents a reliable restoration claim. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-RMY-0015 | Négyesi et al. 2022, compression and strength meta-analysis. [PMID 35476183](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35476183/) | Nineteen RCTs found no strength-sparing effect across assessed recovery windows. | Compression is not a reliable way to restore post-exercise strength. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0016 | O'Riordan et al. 2023, compression and peripheral blood-flow meta-analysis. [PMID 36622554](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36622554/) | Selected circulation measures changed by timing and vessel type; recovery-phase arterial flow did not improve. | Blood flow is not healing, toxin removal or restored performance. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0017 | Maia et al. 2024, pneumatic-compression meta-analysis. [PMID 39416507](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39416507/) | Seventeen studies showed trivial-to-small functional effects, possible soreness effects and variable biomarkers. | Compression boots may support comfort but are not necessary recovery. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0018 | Neves et al. 2025, pneumatic-compression review. [PMID 40325678](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40325678/) | Six eligible studies did not establish superiority or inferiority versus other methods. | Evidence remains limited despite anecdotal appeal; absence of proof is not proof of no subjective value. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0019 | Zhu et al. 2026, percussive-therapy meta-analysis. [PMID 42286692](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42286692/) | Low-certainty jump benefit; no clear maximum-strength or soreness benefit; dose findings exploratory. | A massage gun may affect one outcome without being complete recovery; no prescriptive threshold. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0020 | Bieuzen et al. 2013, contrast-water meta-analysis. [PMID 23626806](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23626806/) | High-risk-of-bias trials suggested benefits over passive rest but little superiority over other modalities. | Optional symptom-management choice; no flushing, lymphatic or optimality claim. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-RMY-0021 | Baker 2019, sweat-gland physiology review. [PMID 31608304](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31608304/) | Sweat's toxicant-excretion role is minor relative to kidney and gastrointestinal routes. | Reject exercise or sauna detox claims; sweating mainly manages heat. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0022 | Gagnon and Crandall 2018, sweating physiology review. [PMID 30454591](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30454591/) | Sweating is the main evaporative heat-loss response and varies with environment, acclimation and hydration. | Sweat volume is not workout quality, fat loss, detox or an individual electrolyte prescription. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0023 | Hew-Butler et al. 2015, exercise-associated hyponatraemia consensus. [PMID 26102445](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26102445/) | Excess fluid intake relative to loss is the principal behavioural risk for exercise-associated hyponatraemia. | Avoid forced universal water rules; trainers do not diagnose or clinically prescribe fluid or sodium. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0024 | Salem et al. 2024, BCAA meta-analysis. [PMID 38625669](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38625669/) | Time-specific CK and DOMS reductions appeared, but not LDH, with heterogeneous protocols. | BCAA may change soreness but is not required and does not override protein adequacy or supplement scope. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0025 | Torre et al. 2021, antioxidant vitamins and DOMS review. [PMID 33673055](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33673055/) | Fourteen mostly young-male studies were insufficient to show vitamin C/E prevents DOMS. | Recovery does not require an antioxidant stack; normal food and clinical care are unaffected. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0026 | Righi et al. 2020, vitamin C acute-exercise meta-analysis. [PMID 32162041](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32162041/) | Some oxidative and inflammatory markers fell, but CK, cortisol, soreness and strength did not improve. | A favourable biomarker is not felt or functional recovery. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0027 | Clifford et al. 2020, vitamin C/E training-adaptation meta-analysis. [PMID 31851538](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31851538/) | No significant group-level impairment of aerobic capacity, endurance, lean mass or strength; power remained limited. | Reject both guaranteed recovery and the absolute claim that antioxidant supplements always erase gains. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0028 | Saw et al. 2016, athlete-monitoring systematic review. [PMID 26423706](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26423706/) | Subjective measures were generally more sensitive and consistent with load change than common objective measures. | Ask the member and combine report, observation and context; no diagnosis or infallible score. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-RMY-0029 | Duignan et al. 2020, single-item wellbeing review. [PMID 32991706](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32991706/) | Associations with training load were inconsistent and usually trivial to moderate. | One answer begins a conversation; it does not settle readiness or create a cut-off. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-RMY-0030 | Düking et al. 2021, HRV-guided endurance-training meta-analysis. [PMID 34489178](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34489178/) | Medium submaximal-physiology effect but small non-significant performance and VO2peak differences versus predefined training. | HRV can be one longitudinal signal, not a universal readiness command or diagnosis. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

## Mindset and behaviour completion records

These records complete the parent Mindset/Behaviour evidence cohort. Each is approved only for the exact translation and boundary below; none authorises psychotherapy, diagnosis, forced disclosure, compulsory tracking or guaranteed adherence.

| ID | Study and locator | Evidence finding | Approved use and prohibited overreach | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-MBE-0001 | Silva et al. 2018, implementation-intention meta-analysis. [PMID 30427874](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30427874/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0206294` | Reinforced implementation intentions produced a small activity increase in adults. | Offer a when/where plan when useful; do not assume planning creates motivation, capability or access. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0002 | Peng et al. 2022, planning-intervention meta-analysis. [PMID 35742582](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35742582/); DOI `10.3390/ijerph19127337` | Action and coping planning improved activity on average across heterogeneous trials. | Use planning as an option; no compulsory template or guaranteed outcome. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0003 | Kompf 2020, implementation-intention systematic review. [PMID 31923898](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31923898/); DOI `10.1123/jpah.2018-0720` | Plans appeared more useful when intention and self-efficacy were already present. | Check desire and capability before adding a plan; a plan cannot diagnose or fix ambivalence. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0004 | Madigan et al. 2021, device-measured maintenance meta-analysis. [PMID 34129276](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34129276/); DOI `10.1111/obr.13304` | Part of a successful intervention's activity gain was retained; added maintenance support had small effects. | Behaviour can persist imperfectly after support; maintenance is not permanence. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0005 | Roordink et al. 2023, lapse/relapse prospective review. [PMID 34851220](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34851220/); DOI `10.1080/08870446.2021.1981900` | Self-efficacy was the only reasonably consistent predictor across several outcomes. | Review lapses without moral judgement; do not diagnose an individual's psychology from a lapse. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0006 | van Vliet et al. 2026, identity-intervention review. [PMID 41736138](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41736138/); DOI `10.1186/s13643-026-03103-2` | Identity-targeted interventions had mixed identity and behaviour findings. | Identity may be discussed only when member-owned; it is not a required target or personality diagnosis. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0007 | Zhu et al. 2025, habit–identity meta-analysis. [PMID 40105110](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40105110/); DOI `10.1111/aphw.70017` | Habit and identity were positively associated across several health behaviours. | Preserve a possible relationship; do not infer direction, causality or a universal identity method. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0008 | Rhodes et al. 2025, physical-activity identity meta-analysis. [PMID 40008714](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40008714/); DOI `10.1080/17437199.2024.2412996` | Interventions changed activity identity slightly and activity outcomes heterogeneously. | Confidence and identity may follow action; neither must be manufactured before participation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0009 | Tavares et al. 2021, exercise affect/enjoyment meta-analysis. [PMID 34112041](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34112041/); DOI `10.1177/00315125211024212` | Mode and intensity influenced pleasure and enjoyment differently in small studies. | Preference and experience matter; no mode is universally most enjoyable or adherent. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0010 | Chen et al. 2021, affective-variable intervention meta-analysis. [PMID 34177690](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34177690/); DOI `10.3389/fpsyg.2021.628993` | Selected techniques were associated with affective or activity outcomes amid major heterogeneity. | Use affect as feedback; component associations are not a causal motivational ranking. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0011 | Vasudevan and Ford 2022, women's strength-training barriers meta-synthesis. [PMID 34800250](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34800250/); DOI `10.1007/s11121-021-01328-2` | Stigma, knowledge, access, supervision, routine, time and caring demands shaped participation; support helped. | Improve coaching and environment; qualitative themes do not define every woman or guarantee attendance. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0012 | Peng et al. 2023, young-women activity barriers synthesis. [PMID 36849995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36849995/); DOI `10.1186/s12966-023-01411-7` | Personal, social and environmental influences interacted. | Ask about actual context; do not reduce participation to discipline or one universal female barrier. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0013 | Olasagasti-Ibargoien et al. 2023, women-with-disabilities barriers review. [PMID 37367246](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37367246/); DOI `10.3390/jfmk8020082` | Barriers spanned personal, social, environmental and programme-access domains. | Design for access and refer appropriately; do not substitute motivation coaching for accessibility or qualified care. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0014 | Rhodes et al. 2017, resistance-training participation review. [PMID 28404558](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28404558/); DOI `10.1136/bjsports-2016-096950` | Participation correlated with affect, efficacy, intention, self-regulation, norms and programme leadership. | Treat adherence as multi-factorial; associations are not a universal causal recipe. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0015 | Burton et al. 2017, older-adult resistance-training motivators/barriers review. [PMID 27620535](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27620535/); DOI `10.1123/japa.2015-0289` | Function and independence mattered; misconceptions and access could deter participation. | Use member-valued benefits and accurate education without fear-selling or age stereotyping. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0016 | Pearl et al. 2021, weight-stigma and activity review. [PMID 34747131](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34747131/); DOI `10.1002/oby.23274` | Discrimination, teasing and internalised stigma were often associated with lower activity, though findings were mixed. | Keep coaching weight-respectful; observational evidence does not diagnose one person's response. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0017 | Biber and Ellis 2019, self-compassion and health-behaviour review. [PMID 28810473](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28810473/); DOI `10.1177/1359105317713361` | A seven-study evidence base supported possible self-regulation benefits. | Support non-shaming review; do not present self-compassion as therapy, superiority or a guaranteed outcome. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0018 | Anderberg et al. 2025, diet/fitness-app and disordered-eating review. [PMID 39671845](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39671845/); DOI `10.1016/j.bodyim.2024.101836` | App use was associated with disordered eating, body-image concerns and compulsive exercise in young adults. | Make tracking voluntary and monitor burden; association does not prove the app caused harm. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0019 | Roth et al. 2024, weight-monitoring and eating-disorder symptom meta-analysis. [PMID 39069082](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39069082/); DOI `10.1016/j.appet.2024.107610` | The pooled general association with global eating-disorder symptoms was not significant. | Avoid claiming all tracking is harmful; the null average is not universal safety for vulnerable individuals. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0020 | Guest et al. 2019, positive-body-image intervention review. [PMID 31077956](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31077956/); DOI `10.1016/j.bodyim.2019.04.002` | Several small interventions improved selected positive-body-image outcomes, mainly in women. | Use functionality-respecting language; trainers do not deliver body-image therapy. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0021 | Makama et al. 2021, postpartum lifestyle barriers review. [PMID 33403746](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33403746/); DOI `10.1111/obr.13167` | Capability, childcare, time, support, knowledge, enjoyment and health context shaped participation. | Adapt demand to postpartum reality; no bounce-back pressure or character judgement. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0022 | Harrison et al. 2018, pregnancy activity barriers review. [PMID 29289592](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29289592/); DOI `10.1016/j.jphys.2017.11.012` | Women were generally positive; fatigue, time and discomfort were common barriers and social support helped. | Address practical barriers while retaining obstetric screening and clinical restrictions. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0023 | Gui et al. 2026, postpartum pelvic-floor adherence meta-synthesis. [PMID 41422633](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41422633/); DOI `10.1016/j.midw.2025.104695` | Physical, knowledge, time, social, feedback and emotional factors interacted. | Useful context for referral and support; no pelvic-health diagnosis, rehabilitation or universal prompt protocol. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0024 | Gintel et al. 2026, adolescent activity-intervention review. [PMID 41742105](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41742105/); DOI `10.1186/s12889-025-25618-4` | Support, self-monitoring and feedback appeared more often in effective programmes; few studies were high quality. | Use age-appropriate choice and feedback; no formula and no bypass of safeguarding or family context. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0025 | Ahmed et al. 2024, midlife behaviour-technique review. [PMID 38663855](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38663855/); DOI `10.1123/japa.2023-0140` | Personal goals, tailored feedback, support, education and monitoring appeared in effective programmes. | Offer matched supports; co-occurrence across 12 trials cannot rank isolated techniques. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0026 | Rodrigues et al. 2018, interpersonal behaviour and exercise-persistence review. [PMID 30459690](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30459690/); DOI `10.3389/fpsyg.2018.02141` | Autonomy-supportive professional behaviour related to need satisfaction and persistence. | Use choice, rationale and competence support; no coach style guarantees retention. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0027 | Rodrigues et al. 2025, exercise-adherence interventions in adults with overweight/obesity. [PMID 41523739](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41523739/); DOI `10.1155/jobe/4164477` | Group and supervised programmes appeared helpful, with substantial risk of bias. | Offer community and supervision without assuming weight status defines support needs or guarantees adherence. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-MBE-0028 | Howlett et al. 2019, inactive-adult behaviour-maintenance meta-analysis. [PMID 29506209](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29506209/); DOI `10.1093/tbm/iby010` | Interventions had small effects at end and follow-up; graded tasks, rehearsal, planning and cues appeared in effective programmes. | Support the Evolved practice-and-re-entry method; component associations do not prove isolated causality. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0029 | Larsen et al. 2022, physical-activity monitor meta-analysis. [PMID 35082116](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35082116/); DOI `10.1136/bmj-2021-068047` | Monitor-based interventions produced small-to-moderate average activity increases. | Tracking may help; it is not required, diagnostic or proof of commitment. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-MBE-0030 | Farrance et al. 2016, older-adult community group-exercise review. [PMID 26921655](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26921655/); DOI `10.1016/j.ypmed.2016.02.037` | Social connection, design, perceived benefit and instructor behaviour recurred in adherence findings. | Community is a possible support, not a causal guarantee or replacement for individual fit. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

## Supplementation cross-pillar records

These records govern ingredient, safety and translation claims across all four pillars. They do not form a supplement programme, product list or prescribing authority.

| ID | Study and locator | Evidence finding | Approved use and prohibited overreach | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-SUP-0001 | Supplement-safety global review. [PMID 41468235](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41468235/); DOI `10.1123/ijsnem.2025-0177` | Regulation and third-party-testing access vary; high-risk product categories occur across regions. | Product integrity needs separate review; certification does not prove efficacy, suitability or zero risk. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0002 | Elite-sport contamination review. [PMID 39442273](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39442273/); DOI `10.1016/j.forsciint.2024.112240` | Supplements, foods, medicines and handling can create inadvertent prohibited-substance exposure. | Preserve anti-doping vigilance; no risk estimate for one product or general-health conclusion. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0003 | Herb/supplement–drug interaction review. [PMID 23067030](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23067030/); DOI `10.1111/j.1742-1241.2012.03008.x` | Major documented interactions concentrated among a subset of products and medicines. | Medication and supplement interaction management is clinical, not trainer-led. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0004 | Botanical adverse-effects review. [PMID 25251944](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25251944/); DOI `10.1111/bcp.12519` | Adverse effects and drug interactions were documented for marketed botanicals. | Natural origin is not proof of safety; case literature does not establish incidence. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0005 | Sport-supplement adulteration review. [PMID 37841887](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37841887/); DOI `10.3389/fspor.2023.1239121` | Analytical studies found material but highly variable prohibited-substance contamination. | Independent testing reduces selected risk; it does not validate effectiveness or suitability. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0006 | Female creatine adverse-outcome meta-analysis. [PMID 32549301](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32549301/); DOI `10.3390/nu12061780` | No pooled serious-event or renal, hepatic, GI, weight or total-adverse-event signal appeared in studied women. | Limited reporting and population coverage prevent universal or pregnancy/renal-disease safety claims. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0007 | Creatine kidney-function RCT meta-analysis. [PMID 42035842](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42035842/); DOI `10.1053/j.jrn.2026.04.010` | Serum creatinine rose modestly without significant pooled urea or eGFR differences. | Clinical interpretation and long-duration evidence remain limitations. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0008 | Creatine kidney-function meta-analysis. [PMID 41199218](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41199218/); DOI `10.1186/s12882-025-04558-6` | Small serum-creatinine increase occurred without significant pooled GFR change. | Does not establish every-dose, every-duration or clinical-population safety. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0009 | HMB plus resistance-training older-adult meta-analysis. [PMID 41305674](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41305674/); DOI `10.3390/nu17223624` | Limited function signals appeared without lean-mass, gait-speed or body-composition benefit; half the trials were high risk of bias. | Retain as conflicting context; no routine HMB recommendation. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-SUP-0010 | HMB adjunct comprehensive meta-analysis. [PMID 41934514](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41934514/); DOI `10.1093/ageing/afag073` | HMB added to resistance training did not improve body composition or strength in adults aged 50 and older. | Reject routine use for capable trainees; clinically malnourished populations are a separate question. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0011 | Caffeine in female intermittent-sport athletes. [PMID 42253738](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42253738/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2026.1817134` | Agility and vertical jump improved, but sprint did not; cycle-phase moderation was unestablished. | No universal or menstrual-phase-specific recommendation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0012 | Acute caffeine in women meta-analysis. [PMID 42500255](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42500255/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2026.1876198` | A small average performance effect appeared with no clear cycle-phase or contraceptive moderation. | Low certainty prevents rigid phase-based or individual prescription. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0013 | Caffeine in female team-sport athletes. [PMID 34684665](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34684665/); DOI `10.3390/nu13103663` | Selected skill, jump, impact and grip outcomes improved; several other outcomes did not. | Athlete outcome specificity does not make caffeine necessary or suitable for standard members. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0014 | Female jumping and caffeine meta-analysis. [PMID 37191618](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37191618/); DOI `10.1080/27697061.2023.2212740` | A small jumping benefit and exploratory follicular-phase signal were reported. | Later synthesis and small samples prevent cycle-phase dosing rules. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-SUP-0015 | Sex differences in caffeine review. [PMID 31574901](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31574901/); DOI `10.3390/nu11102313` | Aerobic effects appeared similar by sex; selected anaerobic effects may have been smaller in women. | Ten-study evidence cannot predict individual benefit, tolerance or timing. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-SUP-0016 | Beta-alanine in women meta-analysis. [PMID 42370349](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42370349/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2026.1857513` | Time-to-exhaustion improved, while power, anaerobic, VO2 and body-fat outcomes were unclear or null. | Very-low certainty prevents routine use or broad performance claims. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0017 | Beta-alanine repeated-sprint meta-analysis. [PMID 41971372](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41971372/); DOI `10.3389/fnut.2026.1818755` | No clear benefit appeared for mean or peak repeated-sprint performance or fatigue decrement. | Does not negate possible effects in longer high-intensity efforts. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0018 | Beta-alanine body-composition meta-analysis. [PMID 35813845](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35813845/); DOI `10.1080/15502783.2022.2079384` | No body-mass, fat-mass, body-fat or fat-free-mass effect appeared. | Reject body-composition marketing; other performance outcomes remain separate. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0019 | Nitrate and female strength/power/sprint meta-analysis. [PMID 41010367](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41010367/); DOI `10.3390/life15091425` | A small-to-moderate power effect appeared without significant strength or sprint effects. | Narrow acute female evidence cannot support broad performance claims. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0020 | Nitrate umbrella review. [PMID 40085422](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40085422/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-025-02194-6` | Selected endurance, muscular-endurance and power outcomes improved, with null findings elsewhere. | Mostly weak source-review quality prevents universal or product-level recommendation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0021 | Sodium-bicarbonate running meta-analysis. [PMID 41416636](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41416636/); DOI `10.1080/15502783.2025.2538606` | Continuous-running benefit was negligible and GI symptoms increased. | Male-dominant evidence does not settle every task; tolerability remains individual. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0022 | Combined beta-alanine and bicarbonate meta-analysis. [PMID 38952910](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38952910/); DOI `10.5114/biolsport.2024.132997` | A small combined effect appeared while isolated effects were not significant. | Small mixed evidence does not establish additive benefit, female transfer or routine use. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-SUP-0023 | Collagen plus training meta-analysis. [PMID 39060741](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39060741/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-024-02079-0` | Selected body-composition, tendon, architecture, strength and recovery signals appeared. | Low or very-low certainty for many outcomes; collagen does not replace total protein, loading or rehabilitation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0024 | Omega-3 muscle meta-analysis. [PMID 36811583](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36811583/); DOI `10.1016/j.advnut.2022.11.005` | A very small strength effect appeared without muscle-mass or function improvement. | No female, ageing, recovery or longevity requirement follows. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0025 | Tart-cherry recovery meta-analysis. [PMID 41945263](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41945263/); DOI `10.1186/s40798-026-00993-3` | Selected force and CRP recovery signals appeared without consistent soreness, CK, cytokine or ROM effects. | Heterogeneity and sensitivity prevent a universal daily-recovery claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0026 | Magnesium and sleep review. [PMID 35184264](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35184264/); DOI `10.1007/s12011-022-03162-1` | Observational associations coexisted with contradictory randomized supplementation findings. | Magnesium is not an approved generic sleep treatment. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0027 | Melatonin in shift-work health personnel. [PMID 36011832](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36011832/); DOI `10.3390/ijerph191610199` | Small heterogeneous trials reported selected sleep and daytime improvements. | No trainer recommendation or circadian-reset claim; formulation and dose are clinically bounded. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-SUP-0028 | Iron and performance in reproductive-age women. [PMID 24717371](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24717371/); DOI `10.3945/jn.113.189589` | Maximal and submaximal performance improved on average, with few low-risk-of-bias trials. | No unscreened iron use; assessment, cause, dose and monitoring are clinical. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-SUP-0029 | Oral iron in athletes meta-analysis. [PMID 38407751](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38407751/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-024-01992-8` | Ferritin benefit was clearest at very low baseline ferritin; pooled haemoglobin and VO2max did not significantly improve. | Supports assessment-led care, not generic athlete or female supplementation. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-SUP-0030 | Curcumin recovery meta-analysis. [PMID 37408367](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37408367/); DOI `10.1177/02601060231186439` | No significant pooled effect appeared for damage markers, soreness or inflammation; strength evidence was insufficient. | Curcumin is not proven recovery, pain treatment or injury management. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

## Omega-3 focused correction records

These records correct the initial supplementation expansion's underweight treatment of omega-3. Biomarker status, food intake, retail supplementation, prescription formulations and clinical outcomes remain separate questions.

| ID | Study and locator | Evidence finding | Approved use and prohibited overreach | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-NFA-0001 | US NHANES omega-3-index prevalence. [PMID 42291144](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42291144/); DOI `10.1016/j.cdnut.2026.107715` | Mean index was 4.12%; 54% were below the authors' 4% band. | Prevalence context only; no Australian deficiency diagnosis, universal 8% target or supplement dose. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0002 | Workplace feedback and incentive study. [PMID 28783061](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28783061/); DOI `10.3390/nu9080842` | Reported intake and repeat testing related to a small index increase; coupon redemption was rare. | Does not establish testing, incentive or supplementation efficacy. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0003 | Framingham omega-3 index and mortality/CVD. [PMID 29559306](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29559306/); DOI `10.1016/j.jacl.2018.02.010`; table/legend correction [PMID 33138960](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33138960/) | Higher baseline index was associated with lower mortality and incident CVD. The correction revised ALA/DPA quintile values and the Figure 1 legend, not this bounded association. | Observational association is not proof that supplementation extends life or prevents disease. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0004 | Seventeen-cohort biomarker and mortality pooling. [PMID 33888689](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33888689/); DOI `10.1038/s41467-021-22370-2` | Higher circulating long-chain marine omega-3 was associated with 15–18% lower mortality comparing extreme quintiles. | Preserve association; no causal supplement or target claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0005 | Framingham fatty-acid mortality fingerprint. [PMID 34134132](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34134132/); DOI `10.1093/ajcn/nqab195` | A four-fatty-acid model including omega-3 index predicted mortality about as well as a selected risk-factor model. | Prediction needs external validation and is not treatment, causation or biological age. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0006 | PAD omega-3 index and pro-resolving mediators. [PMID 28778393](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28778393/); DOI `10.1016/j.jacl.2017.06.011` | High-dose fish oil changed the index and selected pathway biomarkers over one month. | Mechanism only; no symptom, recovery or healthy-population benefit follows. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0007 | Fish/supplement intake and omega-3 index. [PMID 30773210](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30773210/); DOI `10.1016/j.plefa.2019.01.002` | More reported fish and supplement use related to higher index; two fish meals did not reliably reach 8%. | Does not validate 8% as a universal target or make supplements necessary. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0008 | EPA/DHA dose and omega-3-index response model. [PMID 31396625](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31396625/); DOI `10.1093/ajcn/nqz161` | Dose, baseline index and chemical form predicted much biomarker response. | Predicts a biomarker, not a clinical outcome or trainer-led dose. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0009 | Cardiovascular RCT meta-analysis. [PMID 39076869](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39076869/); DOI `10.31083/j.rcm2401024` | No significant MACE, MI, stroke or all-cause-mortality benefit; small selected signals coexisted with more AF. | Reject generic heart-protection; formulation, indication and risk context matter. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0010 | Long-term supplementation and atrial fibrillation. [PMID 34612056](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34612056/); DOI `10.1161/CIRCULATIONAHA.121.055654` | AF risk increased across large cardiovascular trials, with a larger signal above 1 g/day. | Clinical safety signal; does not imply harm from fish foods or determine every user's risk. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0011 | General supplementation safety meta-analysis. [PMID 37567449](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37567449/); DOI `10.1016/j.advnut.2023.08.003` | Minor GI/taste and broadly reported bleeding-tendency signals occurred without definite attributable serious events. | Heterogeneous reporting cannot replace individual medicine, pregnancy or procedure review. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0012 | Bleeding-risk RCT meta-analysis. [PMID 38742535](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38742535/); DOI `10.1161/JAHA.123.032390` | No significant overall or major-site bleeding increase; high-dose purified EPA added about 0.6% absolute bleeding risk. | Dose and formulation matter; clinical clearance remains outside trainer scope. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0013 | Cognition without dementia dose-response meta-analysis. [PMID 38468309](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38468309/); DOI `10.1186/s12916-024-03296-0` | A possible executive-function trend appeared under selected conditions. | No broad cognitive-enhancement or dementia-prevention claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0014 | Fifty-eight-RCT cognitive dose-response meta-analysis. [PMID 40836005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40836005/); DOI `10.1038/s41598-025-16129-8` | Selected cognitive-domain signals were low-to-moderate certainty and sometimes non-linear. | Must be reconciled with large null trials; no universal dose or prevention claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0015 | Large omega-3 cognition-trial review. [PMID 39422948](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39422948/); DOI `10.3233/JAD-231467` | Five of 24 trials were positive; all four Alzheimer-disease trials and most other large trials were null. | Supports rejecting broad treatment/prevention claims while retaining research hypotheses. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0016 | Omega-3/DHA/EPA/Souvenaid in Alzheimer disease. [PMID 38924283](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38924283/); DOI `10.1002/npr2.12455` | One clinical scale improved while several cognitive, functional, safety and imaging outcomes did not. | Mixed products and designs cannot support omega-3 monotherapy or public dementia claims. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| STU-NFA-0017 | Pregnancy/lactation supplementation and child neurodevelopment. [PMID 34383914](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34383914/); DOI `10.1093/jn/nxab238` | Limited selected cognitive evidence coexisted with null findings in every pregnancy trial and insufficient evidence elsewhere. | No guaranteed fetal/child benefit; obstetric and dietetic guidance governs. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0018 | Cochrane pregnancy omega-3 review. [PMID 30480773](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30480773/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD003402.pub3` | Preterm and early-preterm birth were reduced; prolonged gestation probably increased and other outcomes were mixed. | Qualified clinical pregnancy evidence, not trainer dosing or a universal prenatal product. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0019 | Depression RCT dose-response meta-analysis. [PMID 37726108](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37726108/); DOI `10.1017/S0007114523002052` | No general-population prevention effect; symptom-severity signal appeared in existing depression. | Depression is clinical care; no trainer-led treatment or nonspecific mood explanation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0020 | Marine omega-3 and metabolic-syndrome RCT meta-analysis. [PMID 41156531](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41156531/); DOI `10.3390/nu17203279` | Triglycerides fell, especially at higher doses; most other outcomes were inconsistent or insufficient and LDL could rise. | Therapeutic lipid dosing and monitoring are clinical; no broad metabolic or fat-loss claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| STU-NFA-0021 | Eccentric-exercise soreness meta-analysis. [PMID 32382573](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32382573/); DOI `10.1155/2020/8062017` | Statistical soreness reduction was below clinical importance; strength and ROM did not improve; certainty was low. | Reject guaranteed or meaningful recovery claims. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` |
| STU-NFA-0022 | 2026 exercise-recovery meta-analysis. [PMID 42124047](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42124047/); DOI `10.3390/nu18091447` | Selected peak-impairment recovery signals appeared, but evidence was equivocal and no dose could be established. | Optional outcome-specific context only; no female-specific, adaptation or routine-dose claim. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |

## GLP-1 muscle, function and safety records

These records are governed in full by `glp1-muscle-and-safety-register.md`. `Lean mass`, skeletal muscle, strength and function are not interchangeable. Review date for all records: 17 August 2026. Immediate review applies on retraction/correction, Australian regulatory or label change, newer major synthesis, material safety signal, materially broader claim or clinical application. Source `SRC-PUBMED-GLP-001`.

| ID and citation | Design and population | Finding and material limitation | State and approved use | Prohibited overreach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-GLP-0001 — Wachiraphansakul N, et al. *Comparative Effects of Individual GLP-1RA-Based Medications on Direct Measurement of Body Composition.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2026. [PMID 42209204](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42209204/); DOI `10.1111/dom.70884` | Systematic review and network meta-analysis; 43 RCTs, 3,379 adults with overweight/obesity, with or without T2D. | Multiple adiposity measures improved; selected injectable regimens reduced absolute lean mass. Direct measures varied and function was not established. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — support fat-loss-dominant but non-zero lean-loss translation. | Do not call all lean loss skeletal-muscle wasting or imply every agent/dose has the same effect. |
| STU-GLP-0002 — Laverde LP, et al. *Effect of GLP-1 receptor agonists at doses for obesity management on muscle health.* Int J Obes. 2026. [PMID 42321502](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42321502/); DOI `10.1038/s41366-026-02118-y` | Systematic review/meta-analysis; seven RCTs, 821 adults with obesity. | Lean-mass proportion improved while absolute lean mass fell 1.74 kg; heterogeneity for absolute and percentage change was 98%. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — explain absolute-versus-relative body composition. | Do not present pooled or drug-subgroup estimates as a personal forecast or a reason to avoid prescribed care. |
| STU-GLP-0003 — Alissou M, et al. *Impact of Semaglutide on fat mass, lean mass and muscle function: SEMALEAN.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2026. [PMID 41068996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41068996/); DOI `10.1111/dom.70141` | Prospective cohort; 106 completers with obesity, 68.9% female, semaglutide 2.4 mg for 12 months. | Lean mass fell 3 kg by month 7 then stabilised; grip improved 4.5 kg. No untreated control and high baseline BMI limit transfer. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — show that lean mass and function can diverge. | Do not claim semaglutide improves strength or preserves lean mass in every user. |
| STU-GLP-0004 — Jiao R, et al. *Characterizing body composition modifying effects of a GLP-1 receptor-based agonist.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2025. [PMID 39431379](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39431379/); DOI `10.1111/dom.16012` | Meta-analysis; 19 RCTs. | Versus controls, fat mass fell 2.25 kg and lean mass 1.02 kg; lean-mass percentage was comparable. Regimens and populations varied. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — support proportional and absolute reporting together. | Do not equate a DXA/BIA lean-mass change with damaged muscle or lost function. |
| STU-GLP-0005 — Iorra F, et al. *Effects of GLP-1 Receptor Agonists on Muscle Mass, Strength, and Quality in MASLD.* Liver Int. 2026. [PMID 41999054](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41999054/); DOI `10.1111/liv.70643` | Systematic review; 12 studies, 810 adults with MASLD. | Relative muscle preservation and no strength deterioration were reported, but strength and quality data were sparse and heterogeneous. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — disease-specific context that function need not worsen. | Do not generalise to healthy women, all agents or long-term frailty outcomes. |
| STU-GLP-0006 — Chrysavgis L, et al. *The Influence of GLP-1 Receptor Agonists and Other Incretin Hormone Agonists on Body Composition.* Int J Mol Sci. 2025. [PMID 41465555](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41465555/); DOI `10.3390/ijms262412130` | Current narrative review across preclinical and clinical evidence. | Absolute lean reductions are commonly described as 20–30% of total loss, but functional meaning is unclear and methods are heterogeneous. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — contextualise the promoted fraction and research gap. | Do not publish 20–30% as a universal muscle-loss rule. |
