| STU-GLP-0007 — Lundgren JR, et al. *Healthy Weight Loss Maintenance with Exercise, Liraglutide, or Both Combined.* N Engl J Med. 2021. [PMID 33951361](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33951361/); DOI `10.1056/NEJMoa2028198` | Four-arm placebo-controlled RCT; 195 adults after diet-induced weight loss. | Exercise plus liraglutide improved maintenance and fat percentage more than either alone; programme was mainly moderate-to-vigorous mixed exercise. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — exercise complements clinician-managed pharmacotherapy. | Do not represent this as proof of resistance-training hypertrophy or semaglutide/tirzepatide equivalence. |
| STU-GLP-0008 — Jensen SBK, et al. *Physical Fitness with Exercise and GLP-1RA Treatment Alone or Combined.* Sports Med. 2026. [PMID 41579235](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41579235/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-025-02386-0` | Prespecified secondary analysis of the S-LITE RCT; 193 adults. | Exercise and combination improved stair performance and fitness; absolute strength was preserved. Liraglutide alone did not improve fitness. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — support structured exercise for function. | Do not claim the drug built muscle or that exercise cancels adverse effects. |
| STU-GLP-0009 — Vandoni M, et al. *Comparative Efficacy of GLP-1RAs, Exercise, and Their Combination.* Obes Rev. 2026. [PMID 42444070](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42444070/); DOI `10.1111/obr.70189` | Network meta-analysis; nine RCTs, 1,009 adults, 589 female. | Combination ranked best for weight and fat mass; exercise modes and secondary-outcome certainty varied. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — combination context for adiposity and metabolic outcomes. | Do not call this direct evidence of muscle gain or a women-specific prescription. |
| STU-GLP-0010 — Silver HJ, et al. *Liraglutide compared with caloric restriction in adults with obesity and prediabetes.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2023. [PMID 37188932](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37188932/); DOI `10.1111/dom.15113` | Randomised trial comparing liraglutide with caloric restriction. | Appetite, intake, fat distribution and biomarkers differed by intervention; it does not isolate resistance training or prove muscle preservation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — preserve comparator and energy-deficit context. | Do not transfer the findings to all GLP-1 medicines or hypertrophy. |
| STU-GLP-0011 — Chiang CH, et al. *GLP-1 Receptor Agonists and Gastrointestinal Adverse Events.* Gastroenterology. 2025. [PMID 40499738](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40499738/); DOI `10.1053/j.gastro.2025.06.003` | Systematic review/meta-analysis; 55 placebo-controlled RCTs, 106,395 participants. | Cholelithiasis increased by about two cases per 1,000 and GERD by about four per 1,000; little or no effect was found for other prespecified serious GI/biliary events, but rare-event power and regimen heterogeneity remain. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — support accurate class-level GI safety education. | Do not use pooled trial results to dismiss a label warning, diagnose, rank products for an individual or advise dose changes. |
| STU-GLP-0012 — Elkin J, et al. *GLP-1RA use and peri-operative pulmonary aspiration.* Anaesthesia. 2025. [PMID 40230298](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40230298/); DOI `10.1111/anae.16601` | Systematic review/meta-analysis; 28 observational studies. | Residual gastric contents increased; aspiration was not significantly increased (OR 1.04), with low/very-low certainty. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — justify procedural disclosure and clinician direction. | Do not say there is no risk or tell a member to continue/withhold medicine. |
| STU-GLP-0013 — Hocking SL, et al. *2025 ADS/ANZCA/GESA/NACOS peri-procedural recommendations.* Anaesth Intensive Care. 2025. [PMID 40814081](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40814081/); DOI `10.1177/0310057X251355288` | Australian/New Zealand multidisciplinary clinical recommendations. | Provides clinician-led peri-procedural management under uncertainty. Guidance may change as evidence evolves. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — establish disclosure and clinical-ownership boundary. | Trainers do not give fasting, clear-fluid, continuation or withholding instructions. |
| STU-GLP-0014 — Kushner RF, et al. *Safety profile of semaglutide versus placebo in SELECT.* Obesity. 2025. [PMID 39948761](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39948761/); DOI `10.1002/oby.24222` | Large long-term randomised safety analysis in adults with cardiovascular disease and overweight/obesity. | Serious adverse events were lower overall, but discontinuation was higher due to GI events; gallbladder disorders were slightly higher and suicide/self-injury was balanced. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — balanced long-term semaglutide safety context. | Do not call semaglutide risk-free or generalise one indicated population to all users. |
| STU-GLP-0015 — Davies MJ, et al. *Cagrilintide-Semaglutide in Adults with Overweight or Obesity and Type 2 Diabetes.* N Engl J Med. 2025. [PMID 40544432](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40544432/); DOI `10.1056/NEJMoa2502082` | Phase 3a placebo-controlled RCT; 1,206 adults with T2D. | GI adverse events occurred in 72.5% versus 34.4%, mostly transient and mild/moderate; substantial weight and glycaemic effects. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — exact-combination efficacy and tolerability context. | Do not infer muscle preservation, long-term safety or product approval from efficacy. |
| STU-GLP-0016 — Aroda VR, et al. *CagriSema in early-stage type 2 diabetes: REIMAGINE 1.* Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol. 2026. [PMID 42251860](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42251860/); DOI `10.1016/S2213-8587(26)00126-9` | Phase 3a placebo-controlled RCT; 189 adults. | Adverse events affected 79% and 75% at active doses versus 66% placebo, mainly mild/moderate GI events; 40-week trial. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — emerging combination-specific safety context. | Do not transfer adverse-event frequency to semaglutide alone or promise tolerability. |
| STU-GLP-0017 — Jastreboff AM, et al. *Triple-Hormone-Receptor Agonist Retatrutide for Obesity.* N Engl J Med. 2023. [PMID 37366315](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37366315/); DOI `10.1056/NEJMoa2301972` | Phase 2 placebo-controlled RCT; 338 adults. | Large dose-responsive weight reduction; dose-related mostly mild/moderate GI events and heart-rate increases peaking at 24 weeks. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED — INVESTIGATIONAL` — emerging efficacy/safety literacy. | Do not recommend, source or call retatrutide an approved or established safe upgrade. |
| STU-GLP-0018 — Rosenstock J, et al. *Retatrutide for people with type 2 diabetes.* Lancet. 2023. [PMID 37385280](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37385280/); DOI `10.1016/S0140-6736(23)01053-X` | Phase 2 placebo/active-controlled RCT; 281 adults, 56% female. | Glycaemic and weight effects were dose responsive; GI tolerability varied with dose and escalation. Long-term outcomes are unknown. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED — INVESTIGATIONAL` — exact-agent frontier context. | Evolved does not advise access, dosing or escalation. |
| STU-GLP-0019 — Han S, et al. *GLP-1RA Use and Risk of Psychiatric Disorders.* Diabetes Obes Metab. 2026. [PMID 41852258](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41852258/); DOI `10.1111/dom.70692` | Systematic review/meta-analysis; 86 RCTs, 133,378 participants. | No significant increase in psychiatric disorders, depression, suicide, anxiety or sleep disorder; adverse-event reporting still limits rare-event inference. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — reject a generic psychiatric-harm claim. | Do not dismiss an individual's symptoms or replace mental-health assessment. |
| STU-GLP-0020 — Mandal L, et al. *Semaglutide exposure and fetal and neonatal outcomes.* Eur J Obstet Gynecol Reprod Biol. 2026. [PMID 41313865](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41313865/); DOI `10.1016/j.ejogrb.2025.114836` | Systematic review; five heterogeneous studies, 1,128 exposed pregnancies. | No consistent major-malformation association was identified, but bias and heterogeneity preclude firm conclusions. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — preserve uncertainty and prompt clinical review. | Do not call semaglutide safe in pregnancy or advise continuation/discontinuation. |

## Adjunct therapies and elective procedures

These records govern therapies women may encounter through cosmetic, recovery, allied-health, wellness or specialist clinics. Local contour change is not whole-body fat loss; symptom relief is not healing; a clinical indication is not a general-wellness indication; and device clearance or commercial availability is not proof of meaningful benefit. Review date for all records: 18 August 2026. Immediate review applies on retraction/correction, device or regulatory safety action, major sham-controlled trial, new clinical guideline or materially broader proposed use. Source `SRC-PUBMED-ADJ-001`.

| ID and study | Design and finding | State and approved use | Prohibited overreach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-ADJ-0001 — Ravindran R, et al. *Cryolipolysis and associated health outcomes, adverse events, and satisfaction.* [PMID 40211915](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40211915/); DOI `10.1111/obr.13925` | Systematic review/meta-analysis, 30 studies/3,158 participants. Local anthropometric measures improved from baseline; transient local adverse effects were common and study quality/controls varied. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — cryolipolysis may reduce selected local adiposity measures for some patients. | Not weight-loss treatment, metabolic-health care, cellulite cure or substitute for nutrition, activity or surgery; no guaranteed magnitude or durability. |
| STU-ADJ-0002 — Hedayati B, et al. *Adverse Events Associated With Cryolipolysis.* [PMID 32976167](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32976167/); DOI `10.1097/DSS.0000000000002524` | Systematic review of 53 reports. Erythema, numbness, bruising and oedema were common; persistent pain, dysaesthesia, neuropathy, pigmentation and paradoxical adipose hyperplasia were reported. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — informed-consent and complication boundary. | “Non-invasive” does not mean harmless, reversible or suitable for every person. |
| STU-ADJ-0003 — Ho D, Jagdeo J. *A Systematic Review of Paradoxical Adipose Hyperplasia Post-Cryolipolysis.* [PMID 28095535](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28095535/) | Ten eligible reports described 16 published cases and suggested the adverse event may have been under-recognised. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — PAH is a genuine, potentially corrective-surgery-requiring risk. | The small case literature cannot establish a precise modern incidence or individual risk. |
| STU-ADJ-0004 — Vale AL, et al. *Effects of radiofrequency on adipose tissue.* [PMID 30225929](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30225929/); DOI `10.1111/jocd.12776` | Systematic review/meta-analysis of 12 heterogeneous, generally low-quality human studies; anthropometric results were questionable despite biological and local signals. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — radiofrequency has plausible local tissue effects but weak outcome evidence. | No proven meaningful fat loss, skin tightening, cellulite removal or metabolic benefit. |
| STU-ADJ-0005 — Longano C, et al. *Do electrophysical agents work for cellulite treatment?* [PMID 38695965](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38695965/); DOI `10.1007/s10103-024-04068-1` | Systematic review found heterogeneous electrical-current, radiofrequency, ultrasound and photobiomodulation trials with questionable methodological quality. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — explain why selected appearance changes remain uncertain and device-specific. | Cellulite is not disease or “toxin”; no class-wide cure, inflammation treatment or permanent remodelling claim. |
| STU-ADJ-0006 — Qiao J, et al. *Manual lymphatic drainage after postmastectomy lymphoedema.* [PMID 35324506](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35324506/); DOI `10.1097/NCC.0000000000001061` | Meta-analysis of randomised trials/457 patients found no significant overall limb-volume advantage; longer or more numerous treatment subgroups showed signals. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — MLD may be one specialist component for diagnosed breast-cancer-related lymphoedema. | Does not support cosmetic “detox,” fat loss, immune boosting or routine drainage for healthy women. |
| STU-ADJ-0007 — Lin Y, et al. *Manual Lymphatic Drainage for Breast Cancer-related Lymphedema.* [PMID 35370085](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35370085/); DOI `10.1016/j.clbc.2022.01.013` | Systematic review/meta-analysis of 11 RCTs/1,564 patients found selected incidence and pain effects but not consistent volume or quality-of-life benefit. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — retain outcome-specific uncertainty within clinical care. | Do not collapse prevention, pain, volume and quality of life into one “lymph flow” benefit. |
| STU-ADJ-0008 — De Vrieze T, et al. *EFforT-BCRL trial.* [PMID 35428594](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35428594/); DOI `10.1016/j.jphys.2022.03.010` | Multicentre placebo-controlled RCT/194 people: adding fluoroscopy-guided or traditional MLD did not substantially improve standard decongestive lymphatic therapy. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — compression, exercise, education and skin care remain distinct from the added manual technique. | No claim that hands-on drainage is the essential or uniquely active component. |
| STU-ADJ-0009 — Wanchai A, Armer JM. *Manual Lymphedema Drainage after breast surgery.* [PMID 34461070](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34461070/); DOI `10.1016/j.nwh.2021.07.005` | Systematic review of ten RCTs could not establish prevention benefit; management effects were similar to comparators. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — reject routine prophylactic or stand-alone efficacy claims. | No prevention guarantee or general post-workout/post-travel drainage claim. |
| STU-ADJ-0010 — Wright T, et al. *Pneumatic compression and stocking use in women with lipedema.* [PMID 36519532](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36519532/); DOI `10.1177/02683555221145779` | Small industry-sponsored proof-of-principle RCT in diagnosed lipedema with secondary lymphoedema found short-term symptom and measurement improvements. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — clinical conservative-care signal requiring diagnosis and specialist fitting. | Not evidence that compression treats ordinary cellulite, causes fat loss or should be self-prescribed. |
| STU-ADJ-0011 — Canez MS, et al. *PBMT, pneumatic compression and NMES on muscle recovery.* [PMID 40954632](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40954632/); DOI `10.1016/j.jbmt.2025.06.021` | Meta-analysis of 19 RCTs/672 healthy adults: low-certainty pre-exercise PBMT soreness/performance signals; NMES and IPC did not reduce soreness in the evaluated windows. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — modality, timing and outcome must be named; effects are optional and modest/uncertain. | No general recovery acceleration, tissue repair, lymphatic detox or female-specific advantage. |
| STU-ADJ-0012 — de Oliveira RDJ, et al. *Whole-body vibration and postmenopausal BMD.* [PMID 36282343](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36282343/); DOI `10.1007/s00198-022-06556-y` | Systematic review/meta-analysis of 23 studies; selected lumbar-spine BMD effects depended on high-frequency, low-magnitude, high cumulative exposure. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — WBV may be an adjunct under a defined protocol. | Does not replace progressive loading, falls prevention, medication assessment or osteoporosis care; a consumer vibration plate is not equivalent. |
| STU-ADJ-0013 — Costello JT, et al. *Whole-body cryotherapy after exercise.* [PMID 26383887](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26383887/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD010789.pub2` | Cochrane review found very-low-quality, imprecise evidence in small male samples and inadequate adverse-event reporting. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — evidence is insufficient for routine soreness/recovery claims. | No female recovery, inflammation, metabolism, longevity or safety guarantee. |
| STU-ADJ-0014 — Li Q, et al. *Whole-body vibration in postmenopausal osteoporosis.* [PMID 39504068](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39504068/); DOI `10.1590/1414-431X2024e13996` | Meta-analysis of 13 RCTs/783 patients found small BMD and pain signals, with no significant muscle- or fat-mass effect. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — condition-specific adjunct evidence. | No muscle-building, fat-loss or broad anti-ageing claim; does not establish superiority to exercise. |
| STU-ADJ-0015 — Leonardo K, et al. *HIFEM for urinary incontinence in women.* [PMID 39760417](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39760417/); DOI `10.1002/nau.25658` | Systematic review/meta-analysis of seven heterogeneous, potentially biased studies found symptom/QoL signals but no clear contraction or resting-tone advantage. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — emerging pelvic-health device option for clinician-led discussion. | A magnetic chair is not a replacement for assessment, pelvic-floor muscle training or indicated medical care. |
| STU-ADJ-0016 — Vickers AJ, et al. *Acupuncture for Chronic Pain.* [PMID 29198932](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29198932/); DOI `10.1016/j.jpain.2017.11.005` | Individual-participant meta-analysis, 39 trials/20,827 people: acupuncture outperformed sham by a small effect and no-acupuncture control by a larger effect; benefits persisted with some attenuation. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — acupuncture can be a reasonable clinical adjunct for selected chronic-pain conditions. | Not a cure, energy-meridian proof, injury repair or substitute for diagnosis and active care. |
| STU-ADJ-0017 — Rubinstein SM, et al. *Spinal manipulative therapy for chronic low back pain.* [PMID 30867144](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30867144/); DOI `10.1136/bmj.l689` | Meta-analysis of 47 RCTs/9,211 adults: similar pain effects to recommended therapies and small function differences; adverse-event reporting was incomplete. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — manual therapy can be one preference-sensitive option. | No spinal “realignment,” structural correction, prevention or superiority claim; serious/red-flag presentations require clinical care. |
| STU-ADJ-0018 — Yu H, et al. *WHO needling-therapy review for chronic primary low back pain.* [PMID 37991648](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37991648/); DOI `10.1007/s10926-023-10125-3` | Review of 37 RCTs found low/very-low certainty, little or no difference for most outcomes and selected short-term signals. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — communicate uncertainty and adjunct status. | No guaranteed pain relief or inference that needling corrects the cause. |
| STU-ADJ-0019 — Lara-Palomo IC, et al. *Dry needling for chronic low back pain.* [PMID 36399082](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36399082/) | Meta-analysis of eight RCTs/414 patients found short-term pain effects, especially combined with other therapies; disability evidence was not established. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — short-term symptom adjunct. | Not tissue healing, long-term resolution, diagnosis or permission to train through worsening neurological symptoms. |
| STU-ADJ-0020 — Zhang Z, et al. *Cupping therapy on low back pain.* [PMID 38184285](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38184285/); DOI `10.1016/j.ctim.2024.103013` | Meta-analysis of 11 trials/921 participants reported short-term pain effects; protocols, controls and bias limit confidence. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — possible temporary symptom relief for selected people. | Bruising is not “toxins leaving”; no injury repair, circulation cure or durable functional benefit is established. |
| STU-ADJ-0021 — Acosta RD, Cash BD. *Colonic cleansing for general health promotion.* [PMID 19724266](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19724266/); DOI `10.1038/ajg.2009.494` | Systematic review found no rigorous controlled trials supporting general-health use and multiple adverse-event case reports. | `NOT-APPROVED-FOR-CLAIM` — supports rejecting wellness detox/health-promotion claims. | Evolved does not recommend colonics, cleanses or bowel “detox”; medical bowel preparation is a separate clinical indication. |
| STU-ADJ-0022 — Ali A, et al. *Myers' Cocktail for fibromyalgia.* [PMID 19250003](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19250003/); DOI `10.1089/acm.2008.0410` | Double-blind pilot RCT/34 adults: both infusion and placebo groups improved; no significant between-group difference. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — the only controlled signal does not establish efficacy. | No IV-vitamin wellness, energy, immunity, recovery or deficiency-treatment claim; invasive therapy and dosing remain medical. |
| STU-ADJ-0023 — Izquierdo-Alventosa R, et al. *Low-pressure HBOT in women with fibromyalgia.* [PMID 38383268](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38383268/); DOI `10.1016/j.medcli.2023.12.016` | Small RCT/33 women found psychological-pain and QoL improvements after 40 sessions versus control; no active sham and condition-specific transfer limits apply. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — emerging clinical hypothesis in diagnosed fibromyalgia. | No general recovery, oxygenation, fatigue or pain cure; low-pressure protocol is not interchangeable with medical HBOT. |
| STU-ADJ-0024 — Garland MM, et al. *Floatation-REST in anxious and depressed individuals.* [PMID 38843272](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38843272/); DOI `10.1371/journal.pone.0286899` | Randomised feasibility study/75 participants found six sessions feasible and generally well tolerated; efficacy was not the primary test. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — optional relaxation experience with preliminary safety/feasibility. | Does not treat anxiety or depression, regulate the nervous system permanently or replace mental-health care. |
| STU-ADJ-0025 — Choquette EM, et al. *Floatation therapy in anorexia nervosa.* [PMID 37936658](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37936658/); DOI `10.1016/j.eclinm.2023.102173` | Single-site RCT/68 hospitalised women/girls found acute anxiety/body-dissatisfaction changes alongside inpatient care. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — specialist research signal only. | Not an Evolved eating-disorder service, stand-alone therapy or evidence for general body-image coaching. |
| STU-ADJ-0026 — Li FG, et al. *Fractional CO2 laser versus sham for postmenopausal vaginal symptoms.* [PMID 34636862](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34636862/); DOI `10.1001/jama.2021.14892` | Double-blind sham RCT/85 women found no significant symptom, QoL, vaginal-health or histology advantage at 12 months. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — reject established-efficacy claims for this indication/device protocol. | Commercial availability does not establish rejuvenation, tissue restoration or durable symptom relief. |
| STU-ADJ-0027 — Mension E, et al. *LIGHT vaginal-laser trial in breast-cancer survivors.* [PMID 36763359](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36763359/); DOI `10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2022.55697` | Sham-controlled RCT/72 women on aromatase inhibitors: both groups improved, with no significant laser advantage across sexual, symptom, QoL or objective outcomes at six months. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — preserve placebo/context and oncology clinical boundary. | No special efficacy for cancer survivors and no substitution for oncology/gynaecology care. |
| STU-ADJ-0028 — Prodromidou A, et al. *CO2 laser versus sham for GSM.* [PMID 38138921](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38138921/); DOI `10.3390/jpm13121694` | Review of seven RCTs/407 women found most outcomes comparable with sham and one symptom-scale signal sensitive to study inclusion. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — overall efficacy remains uncertain and outcome-specific. | No blanket menopause, continence, sexual-function or “rejuvenation” claim. |
| STU-ADJ-0029 — Zerzan NL, et al. *Energy-based interventions for GSM.* [PMID 39774067](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39774067/); DOI `10.1097/GME.0000000000002465` | Systematic review of 32 studies: CO2 laser produced little/no difference versus sham or vaginal estrogen on evaluated outcomes; other devices were very uncertain and harms reporting limited. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — current evidence ceiling and informed-consent boundary. | No class-wide efficacy or safety; CO2, Er:YAG and radiofrequency are not interchangeable. |
| STU-ADJ-0030 — Sacarin G, et al. *Chair-based magnetic pelvic-floor stimulation and sexual function.* [PMID 41375799](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41375799/); DOI `10.3390/jcm14238496` | Systematic review of five heterogeneous studies/~219 women found promising continence/sexual-function signals with limited standardisation and long-term evidence. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — emerging adjunct requiring pelvic-health assessment and better trials. | No passive replacement for pelvic-floor training, diagnosis or guarantee of continence/sexual outcomes. |
| STU-ADJ-0031 — Gunter J, Parcak S. *Vaginal Jade Eggs: Ancient Chinese Practice or Modern Marketing Myth?* [PMID 30365448](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30365448/); DOI `10.1097/SPV.0000000000000643` | Archaeological-collection review of more than 5,000 viewable jade objects found no vaginal jade eggs and no evidence supporting the claimed ancient-Chinese provenance. It did not test clinical efficacy or safety. | `NOT-APPROVED-FOR-CLAIM` — reject the “ancient Chinese women’s-health practice” authority claim. | This historical finding does not prove benefit, harm, pelvic-floor strengthening, hormone effects, sexual improvement, fertility, detoxification or energetic outcomes. |
| STU-ADJ-0032 — Herbison GP, Dean N. *Weighted vaginal cones for urinary incontinence.* [PMID 23836411](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23836411/); [PMCID PMC7086390](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7086390/); DOI `10.1002/14651858.CD002114.pub2` | Cochrane review of 23 small trials/1,806 women found tentative evidence that purpose-designed weighted cones may outperform no active treatment for stress urinary incontinence and may be similar to pelvic-floor muscle training; heterogeneous outcomes, uncertain quality and dropout limit confidence. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — clinician-selected vaginal cones can be one acceptable conservative option for diagnosed stress incontinence when a woman finds them suitable. | A graded medical/rehabilitation cone is not evidence for a jade, quartz or obsidian yoni egg; no transfer to general strengthening, sexual wellbeing, prolapse, fertility or wellness claims. |
| STU-ADJ-0033 — van Reijn-Baggen DA, et al. *Pelvic Floor Physical Therapy for Pelvic Floor Hypertonicity.* [PMID 34127429](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34127429/); DOI `10.1016/j.sxmr.2021.03.002` | Systematic review of ten generally low- or medium-quality studies found pelvic-floor physical therapy may help selected hypertonic pelvic-floor conditions, while better trials are needed. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — pelvic-floor function includes relaxation and coordination, not only greater contraction; symptoms warrant pelvic-health assessment. | Does not prove yoni eggs cause hypertonicity or that every woman with pain has an overactive pelvic floor; trainers do not diagnose or prescribe internal devices. |
| STU-ADJ-0034 — Kadah S, et al. *Pelvic floor muscle tone in women with and without persistent pelvic pain.* [PMID 36897234](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36897234/); DOI `10.1093/jsxmed/qdac002` | Systematic review/meta-analysis of 21 studies found higher measured myoelectrical activity and resistance, and a smaller levator-hiatus diameter, in women with persistent non-cancer pelvic pain. Measurement methods varied and association does not establish causation. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — supports screening against the assumption that every pelvic floor needs more gripping or loading. | Does not establish that continuous clenching or a yoni egg caused pain, and does not authorise self-diagnosis or internal-device treatment. |

## Late-life capability and cardiorespiratory reserve

This cohort separates four different questions: what grip predicts in populations; what whole-body cardiorespiratory fitness predicts; what may represent an approximate late-life functional floor; and how skeletal-muscle mitochondria adapt to exercise. Peter Attia's Centenarian Decathlon is a useful backcasting framework, not a validated scientific battery. Review date for all records: 18 August 2027. Source `SRC-PUBMED-LCR-001`.

### Grip strength and later independence

| ID and study | Design and finding | State and approved use | Prohibited overreach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-LNG-0010 — Xu J, et al. *Bidirectional association between handgrip strength and ADLs disability: a prospective cohort study.* [PMID 37663846](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37663846/); [PMCID PMC10470652](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10470652/); DOI `10.3389/fpubh.2023.1200821` | Four-year CHARLS cohort of adults aged at least 45. In women, low grip was associated with later basic-ADL disability (OR 1.68, 95% CI 1.20–2.35) and instrumental-ADL disability (OR 1.48, 1.16–1.91). Disability also predicted later low grip, supporting a bidirectional relationship. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — grip is a useful midlife-and-older functional sentinel, interpreted with mobility and daily function. | The AWGS female threshold of 18 kg is population-specific screening context, not an Evolved goal, and the association does not prove a carry standard preserves independence. |
| STU-LNG-0011 — Al Snih S, et al. *Hand grip strength and incident ADL disability in elderly Mexican Americans over a seven-year period.* [PMID 15739601](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15739601/); DOI `10.1007/BF03327406` | Prospective cohort of 2,493 Mexican Americans aged at least 65, including 1,443 women. Among initially non-disabled women, the lowest versus highest grip quartile was associated with incident ADL limitation (HR 2.28, 95% CI 1.59–3.27). | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — lower grip can mark materially greater later disability risk in older women. | Ethnicity-, age- and cohort-specific quartiles are not universal cut-offs; observational risk is not proof that improving grip alone changes the outcome. |
| STU-LNG-0012 — Sarkisian CA, et al. *Modifiable risk factors predict functional decline among older women.* [PMID 10682946](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10682946/); DOI `10.1111/j.1532-5415.2000.tb03908.x` | Prospective study of 6,632 community-dwelling women over 65 with four-year validation. Weak grip predicted decline in vigorous activities, while it was not one of the retained predictors of basic-activity decline. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — function is multidimensional; grip contributes information but its relationship differs by the daily-life outcome. | Do not collapse vigorous activity, basic self-care and independence into one claim or call grip the strongest single predictor. |
| STU-LNG-0013 — McGrath R, et al. *Muscle Weakness and Physical Disability in Older Americans: Longitudinal Findings from the U.S. Health and Retirement Study.* [PMID 29582889](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29582889/); [PMCID PMC6472265](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6472265/) | Longitudinal analysis of 8,725 adults aged at least 65, 57% women. Weakness was associated with onset (OR 1.54) and progression (OR 2.16) of ADL disability over two years in the full cohort. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — reinforces grip weakness as one disability-risk marker in older adults. | Sex/race classification thresholds used by the study are screening variables, not performance targets, and pooled estimates are not women-only causal effects. |

### Cardiorespiratory fitness, reserve and late-life function

| ID and study | Design and finding | State and approved use | Prohibited overreach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-LCR-0001 — Gulati M, et al. *Exercise capacity and the risk of death in women: the St James Women Take Heart Project.* [PMID 12975254](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12975254/); DOI `10.1161/01.CIR.0000091080.57509.E9` | Prospective cohort of 5,721 asymptomatic women, mean age 52. Adjusted mortality risk decreased 17% for each 1-MET higher exercise capacity. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — women-specific evidence that measured exercise capacity is a strong prognostic marker. | A 1-MET training gain does not guarantee a 17% mortality reduction; the association is not a personal life-expectancy calculator. |
| STU-LCR-0002 — Kodama S, et al. *Cardiorespiratory fitness as a quantitative predictor of all-cause mortality and cardiovascular events in healthy men and women.* [PMID 19454641](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19454641/); DOI `10.1001/jama.2009.681` | Meta-analysis of 33 cohorts: 102,980 participants for mortality and 84,323 for CHD/CVD. Each 1-MET higher fitness was associated with 13% lower all-cause mortality and 15% lower CHD/CVD-event risk. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — 1 MET (3.5 mL/kg/min) is a useful population-level unit for communicating the graded CRF association. | The historical <7.9, 7.9–10.8 and ≥10.9 MET study categories are not universal Evolved targets or causal treatment thresholds. |
| STU-LCR-0003 — Myers J, et al. *A Reference Equation for Normal Standards for VO2 Max: Analysis from the FRIEND Registry.* [PMID 28377168](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28377168/); DOI `10.1016/j.pcad.2017.03.002` | Treadmill data from 7,783 healthy adults aged 20–79 plus a 1,287-person validation cohort. Age, sex and bodyweight produced a better-fitting reference equation than older standards, especially for women and heavier adults. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — interpret a measured VO2 value against age- and sex-aware reference data or percentage predicted rather than one absolute universal target. | Reference-normal is not optimal, and treadmill reference equations are not interchangeable with every cycle, wearable or field estimate. |
| STU-LCR-0004 — Paterson DH, et al. *Aerobic fitness in a population of independently living men and women aged 55–86 years.* [PMID 10613433](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10613433/); DOI `10.1097/00005768-199912000-00018` | Cross-sectional study of 298 independently living adults, including 146 women. Modelling suggested about 15 mL/kg/min for women at age 85 as the minimum aerobic capacity compatible with independent living. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — an approximate late-life functional floor that explains why building reserve earlier may matter. | Not a validated pass/fail threshold, a midlife goal, a safety margin or evidence that 15 guarantees independence. |
| STU-LCR-0005 — Paterson DH, et al. *Longitudinal study of determinants of dependence in an elderly population.* [PMID 15450038](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15450038/); DOI `10.1111/j.1532-5415.2004.52454.x` | Eight-year follow-up of 373 initially independent adults aged 55–86; 43 became dependent. Lower VO2max was independently associated with later dependence, with about 14% higher odds per 1 mL/kg/min lower fitness in the reported model. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — aerobic reserve contributes to later independence alongside strength, activity and health. | Small event count and mixed-sex analysis do not establish a women-specific cut-off or prove that one prescribed VO2 increase prevents dependence. |
| STU-LCR-0006 — Horder H, et al. *Midlife cardiovascular fitness and dementia: A 44-year longitudinal population study in women.* [PMID 29540588](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29540588/); [PMCID PMC5894933](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5894933/); DOI `10.1212/WNL.0000000000005290` | Longitudinal cohort of 191 women aged 38–60 at baseline. High versus medium midlife fitness was associated with much lower dementia incidence (HR 0.12, 95% CI 0.03–0.54) and about 9.5 years later onset among cases. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — valuable women-specific signal linking midlife CRF with later cognitive health. | Small observational cohort cannot prove that exercise or a VO2 target prevents dementia; dementia subtypes and residual confounding matter. |

### Mitochondrial adaptation and interpretation

| ID and study | Design and finding | State and approved use | Prohibited overreach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STU-LCR-0007 — Robinson MM, et al. *Enhanced Protein Translation Underlies Improved Metabolic and Physical Adaptations to Different Exercise Training Modes in Young and Old Humans.* [PMID 28273480](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28273480/); [PMCID PMC5423095](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5423095/); DOI `10.1016/j.cmet.2017.02.009` | Twelve-week trial of interval, resistance and combined training in younger and older adults. Interval and combined training improved aerobic capacity and muscle mitochondrial respiration; interval training produced robust mitochondrial transcript/protein responses, including in older adults. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — human mechanistic support that older muscle retains meaningful mitochondrial plasticity. | Mechanistic change is not proof of longevity, “rejuvenation,” disease reversal or superiority of one commercial protocol. |
| STU-LCR-0008 — Mølmen KS, et al. *Effects of Exercise Training on Mitochondrial and Capillary Growth in Human Skeletal Muscle: A Systematic Review and Meta-Regression.* [PMID 39390310](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39390310/); [PMCID PMC11787188](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11787188/); DOI `10.1007/s40279-024-02120-2` | Quantitative synthesis of 5,973 participants across 353 mitochondrial and 131 capillary articles. Adjusted mitochondrial-content gains were similar with endurance (23%), high-intensity (27%) and sprint training (27%); age, sex and menopause did not materially alter the response. | `APPROVED-ANCHOR` — multiple aerobic intensities can stimulate mitochondrial adaptation; dose, initial fitness and adherence matter. | Zone 2 is not uniquely proven to “build mitochondria”; per-hour efficiency, tolerability, recovery and long-term programme fit are different questions. |
| STU-LCR-0009 — Konopka AR, et al. *Molecular adaptations to aerobic exercise training in skeletal muscle of older women.* [PMID 20566734](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20566734/); [PMCID PMC2954235](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2954235/); DOI `10.1093/gerona/glq109` | Nine women, mean age about 70, completed 12 weeks of cycling. Aerobic capacity, muscle cross-sectional area, force and the mitochondrial marker COX IV increased. | `CONTEXT-ONLY` — direct older-women proof-of-principle that aerobic training can improve capacity and muscle molecular markers. | The tiny study does not define an optimal intensity, predict clinical outcomes or justify exact percentage claims for all women. |
| STU-LCR-0010 — Zhang X, et al. *Preserved skeletal muscle oxidative capacity in older adults despite decreased cardiorespiratory fitness with ageing.* [PMID 34032280](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34032280/); [PMCID PMC8284426](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8284426/); DOI `10.1113/JP281691` | Cross-sectional physiological study of 23 young and 52 older men and women. Older adults had lower VO2peak but no age-related reduction in measured muscle mitochondrial capacity after adjustment for sex and activity; moderate-to-vigorous activity related to both. | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` — ageing does not equal uniform mitochondrial failure, and whole-body VO2peak reflects more than muscle mitochondria. | Does not show mitochondria never decline, that ageing has no central cardiovascular effect or that a wearable VO2 estimate measures mitochondrial health. |

## First quarterly-surveillance promotion cohort

These eight records were promoted from the protected 350-candidate appraisal cohort. PMID is the controlling locator for this cohort because the first discovery run exposed cross-record DOI contamination; no DOI from that snapshot was accepted without separate verification.

### STU-ARP-0013 — Protein intervention during resistance training in frail older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Effect of a protein intervention during resistance training with varying training intensities on muscle outcomes in frail community-dwelling older adults: a randomized controlled trial.* J Nutr Health Aging. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41931963](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41931963/) |
| Design and population | Secondary analysis of a 12-week RCT in 295 frail community-dwelling older adults, 69% women; all received supervised progressive resistance training and one group received counselling plus whey to target 1.2–1.5 g/kg/day. |
| Finding and material limitation | Added protein produced no overall advantage for leg-press strength, appendicular lean mass or physical performance. Exploratory subgroups below 1.2 g/kg/day, especially below 0.8 g/kg/day, showed larger strength gains; these were not the primary overall result and need confirmation. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Baseline adequacy matters more than supplement-first escalation; extra protein is more plausibly useful when intake is low. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not present 1.2–1.5 g/kg/day as proven necessary for every older woman or the exploratory subgroup result as a confirmed treatment threshold. |

### STU-FAP-0014 — Reporting quality of resistance training during pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *The missing pieces: A systematic review of the reporting of resistance training variables in studies of pregnant women.* J Sci Med Sport. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 41963142](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41963142/) |
| Design and population | Systematic review of 117 studies from 29 countries involving 66,133 participants and some form of resistance-training exposure during pregnancy. |
| Finding and material limitation | Frequency and supervision were usually reported, but sets, repetitions, intensity and external load were incompletely reported, limiting synthesis of work rate and total load. This is a reporting review, not an efficacy or safety estimate. |
| State | `CONTEXT-ONLY` |
| Approved use | Explain why the pregnancy evidence supports participation more confidently than an exact optimal resistance-training dose. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer that resistance training is unsafe, ineffective or unprogrammable, and do not invent trimester-specific thresholds from missing data. |

### STU-SUP-0031 — Creatine in postmenopausal women

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Creatine monohydrate for lean mass, strength, and bone density in postmenopausal women: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* J Int Soc Sports Nutr. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42141930](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42141930/) |
| Design and population | Seven placebo-controlled RCTs, 608 postmenopausal women, lasting 12–104 weeks. |
| Finding and material limitation | Creatine favoured lean mass by 0.37 kg and leg-press 1RM by 7.5 kg in the available pooled analyses, especially when at least 5 g/day was combined with resistance training. Bone density did not improve overall; prediction intervals, small samples, risk-of-bias concerns and an unregistered review limit certainty. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Creatine can be discussed as an optional evidence-supported adjunct to resistance training for some postmenopausal women after product and individual-safety review. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not guarantee benefit, claim bone protection, prescribe it in standard coaching or treat a pooled dose pattern as an individual instruction. |

### STU-FAP-0015 — Broad prenatal exercise and maternal outcomes

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Effects of prenatal exercise interventions on physical activity, sedentary behaviour, maternal health, and pregnancy outcomes: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* Int J Nurs Stud. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42176368](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42176368/) |
| Design and population | Systematic review and meta-analysis of 88 prenatal-exercise RCTs; most studies had some risk-of-bias concerns. |
| Finding and material limitation | Exercise increased activity and was associated with lower gestational-diabetes, hypertensive-disorder and caesarean-section risk, while birthweight and preterm birth were not significantly changed. Interventions were broad and heterogeneous rather than resistance-only. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Reinforce screened, modified prenatal exercise as generally compatible with maternal health and no demonstrated adverse birthweight/preterm signal in the pooled outcomes. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not assign causality to resistance training alone, guarantee prevention, prescribe an exact dose or claim long-term offspring benefit. |

### STU-LNG-0014 — Elastic bands and free weights in older adults

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Effects of elastic band training versus free weight training in community-dwelling older adults: A randomized controlled trial.* Exp Gerontol. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42217686](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42217686/) |
| Design and population | Twelve-week, twice-weekly progressive resistance trial in 48 community-dwelling older adults, including 20 women, comparing elastic bands, free weights and control. |
| Finding and material limitation | Both training modes improved selected task and mobility outcomes compared with control; the small mixed-sex study did not establish broad superiority of one implement. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Progressive resistance can be delivered with different tools when load, progression, safety and adherence are appropriate. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim bands and free weights are identical for every strength outcome or that light, unprogressed band work is automatically sufficient. |

### STU-SRP-0031 — Chronic insomnia treatment during peri- and postmenopause

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Pharmacological and non-pharmacological treatments for chronic insomnia in perimenopausal and postmenopausal women: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* Sleep Med. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42247852](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42247852/) |
| Design and population | Review of 24 RCTs with 17 meta-analysed in peri- or postmenopausal women with chronic insomnia. |
| Finding and material limitation | Non-pharmacological treatments improved selected sleep-quality and insomnia-severity scores; pharmacological treatment improved PSQI in the pooled analysis, while total sleep time did not clearly change. Interventions and outcomes were heterogeneous and many are treatments outside trainer scope. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Reinforce that chronic insomnia warrants qualified assessment and treatment; sleep-supporting habits can coexist with that care. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not say a bedtime routine treats or fixes insomnia, prescribe medication, or treat yoga, massage or acupuncture as interchangeable with CBT-I. |

### STU-FTM-0031 — Large versus modest resistance-training volume progression

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Large increases in resistance training volume do not impair muscle hypertrophy or anabolic-catabolic molecular signaling in trained individuals.* J Appl Physiol. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42461790](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42461790/) |
| Design and population | Eight-week within-person unilateral RCT in 25 resistance-trained men and women aged 18–35, comparing a 120% versus 20% increase above habitual leg-training volume. |
| Finding and material limitation | Both legs increased muscle cross-sectional area with no between-protocol difference, and the large increase did not impair the assessed adaptations or signalling. The study was small, short, trained, young and muscle-specific. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Reject a simplistic universal volume ceiling and retain individual response, programme purpose and recoverability as the governing interpretation. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not infer that abrupt volume increases are superior, harmless for every person, necessary, or evidence against Evolved's conservative session standard. |

### STU-SRP-0032 — Sleep disorders and hypertensive disorders during pregnancy

| Field | Record |
| --- | --- |
| Citation | *Association between sleep disorders during pregnancy and hypertensive disorders of pregnancy: A systematic review and meta-analysis of cohort studies.* Eur J Obstet Gynecol Reprod Biol. 2026. |
| Locator | [PMID 42526228](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42526228/) |
| Design and population | Meta-analysis of 19 cohort studies including 9,460,018 pregnancies. |
| Finding and material limitation | Sleep disorders were associated with gestational hypertension, pre-eclampsia and hypertensive disorders overall; obstructive sleep apnoea showed the strongest association. Observational diagnosis and residual confounding prevent a coaching-level causal claim. |
| State | `APPROVED-BOUNDED` |
| Approved use | Strengthen pregnancy screening and referral when sleep-disorder symptoms or hypertensive risk are present. |
| Prohibited overreach | Do not claim sleep hygiene prevents hypertensive disorders, diagnose OSA or substitute coaching for obstetric or sleep-medicine care. |

## Review control

- Routine review: 15 August 2027.
- Immediate review: new major systematic review, guideline or consensus; source correction or retraction; a study is proposed for a materially broader claim; clinical application; or a downstream asset omits a recorded limitation.
- Public evidence packs should cite the primary paper directly and repeat the bounded use, not cite this bank as if it were the research itself.
